## A.20 Executing Entity information

The relevant Executing Entities for the implementation of Components 1 and 2 will be:

- In Kenya: The Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock Development (the "MaALP") (for the implementation of the Programme's Activities) and the Ministry of Finance (for the channelling of the GCF Proceeds) and in the Republic of Rwanda, acting through its Ministry of Finance and Economic Development (for the channelling of the GCF Proceeds) and the Tanzania Agricultural Cooperative Bank (TACB) (for the implementation of the Programme's Activities), in each case with respect to the Activities to be implemented by the Host Country of the relevant Executing Entity, together as the "National Executing Entities" or "NEX".

The Executing Entities for the implementation of Components 3 and Output 5.1 will be:

- In Kenya: The Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock Development (for the implementation of the Programme's Activities) and the Ministry of Finance (for the channelling of the GCF Proceeds) and the Agricultural Finance Corporation (for the implementation of the Programme's Activities), in each case with respect to the Activities to be implemented by the Host Country of the relevant Executing Entity, together as the "National Executing Entities" or "NEX".
- In Tanzania: The Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock Development (for the implementation of the Programme's Activities) and the Ministry of Finance (for the channelling of the GCF Proceeds) and the TANZANIA Agricultural Cooperative Bank (TACB) (for the implementation of the Programme's Activities), in each case with respect to the Activities to be implemented by the Host Country of the relevant Executing Entity, together as the "National Executing Entities" or "NEX".
- In Uganda: The Ministry of Agriculture and Animal Resources ("MINAGRI") (for the implementation of the Programme's Activities) and the Ministry of Finance (for the channelling of the GCF Proceeds) and the Uganda Development Bank for the implementation of the Programme's Activities), in each case with respect to the Activities to be implemented by the Host Country of the relevant Executing Entity, together as the "National Executing Entities" or "NEX".

## A.21 Executive summary

### 1. Climate context
Observed and projected climate change scenarios (Representative Concentration Pathways [RCPs] 6.0 and 8.5) in Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania, and Uganda indicate significant changes in precipitation and temperature patterns that are expected to have a profound impact on the agricultural sector.

The frequency and severity of droughts are projected to increase, resulting in substantial losses of dairy production and livestock mortality. In Kenya, for example, climate change is expected to negatively affect smallholder farmers and dairy farmers in East Africa, leading to lower milk yields and production, reduced cattle health, deterioration in pasture quality, and increased incidence of diseases such as Johne's disease.

The projected changes also make adaptation strategies essential to its sustainability and resilience. On the mitigation side, methane (CH4) emissions from the dairy sector dominate the greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions inventory of Kenya, Rwanda,

1 According to the World Bank statistics (http://data.worldbank.org/indicator/9027711), there are 54.2 million people in Kenya, 63.5 million in Tanzania and 47.2 in Uganda and 13.8 in Rwanda.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 4

## Programme objectives and intervention approach

The proposed Programme will be implemented in Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania, and Uganda, aims to reduce methane and other GHG emissions from the dairy sector while increasing productivity and resilience of smallholder dairy farmers and cooperatives. The Programme will focus on institutional strengthening and knowledge dissemination at national and regional levels.

### Component 1: The innovation component

This component focuses on initiating a production shift towards more productive, climate-resilient, and low-emission dairy sector in East Africa. Its strategic approach is to review and update existing policies and regulations that govern the dairy sector; promote investments aligned with the NDCs ensuring these promote gender equality and social inclusion.

### Component 2: Smallholder dairy farmers and cooperatives

This component targets smallholder dairy farmers and cooperatives, with a view to a low-emission and climate-resilient dairy sector. It will focus primarily on farm-level interventions where >90 percent of sector's emissions occur and will be implemented in the four countries. The Programme will also promote investments aligned with NDCs.

### Component 3: Dairy transformation

This component is key to driving the transformation of the East Africa’s dairy sector towards sustainability and climate resilience. It encompasses two strategies:

1. Launching the Green Dairy Financing Facility (GDFF) to provide grants, loans, and other financial services for smallholder farmers.
2. Ensuring the participation banks and borrowing dairy sector businesses to ensure the achievement of DAFFA CC adoption and implementation.

### Implementation arrangements

As an Accredited Entity (AE) for the Programme, IFAD will be responsible for overall management of the Programme approach, fiduciary and day-to-day oversight, fund management, quality assurance, and reporting to the GCF on progress towards achieving the Programme’s objectives. The Programme is expected to be implemented in four countries: Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania, and Uganda.

A Regional Steering Committee will ensure regional alignment and added value for the Programme. Country Coordination Units (CCUs) will be established in each country to oversee (for the former) and coordinate (for the latter) the implementation of the Programme.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

## Program costs and financing.

The total cost of the Programme is US$286.06 million, of which US$50.30 million for Component 1, US$234.98 million for Component 2, US$4.50 million for Component 3 (Green Dairy Financial Facility), and US$7.32 million for the Green Climate Fund Trust Fund.

B. GCF will provide US$151.50 million grants to finance Components 1, 2, and 3, as well as programme management and monitoring and evaluation (M&E) to the four countries. FAD will contribute US$193.80 million co-financing for Component 1, US$476.00 million for Component 2, and US$5.00 million for Component 3.

Finally, for the Green Dairy Financial Facility under Component 3, a total of US$45 million is allocated. Of this amount, US$8.9 million will be provided by GCF as grants and US$36.1 million from FAD as co-financing.

## Beneficiaries and impacts.

The Programme aims to directly benefit 2.6 million people. The Programme aims to reduce the milk emission intensities by 29 percent, of which by 28 percent in Kenya, 50 percent in Rwanda, 70 percent in Uganda, and 41 percent in Tanzania. This increase in milk production is expected to reduce total GHG emissions on the targeted dairy farms, while significantly increasing milk production by 36 per cent (i.e., 9.2 million litres) at a national level.

This increase in milk production will contribute to agricultural GDP growth in these four countries, marking a substantial improvement over baseline levels. The economic benefits of this are expected to be as follows: (a) GHG emissions are expected to decrease by 4.01 percent at the level of the Programme, or (b) by 2.02 percent (-8.967 tCO2e) in Kenya; (c) By 5.39 percent (-10.82 tCO2e) in Rwanda; (d) By 4.01 percent (-10.82 tCO2e) in Uganda, and (e) By 4.61 percent (-2:372 tCO2e) in Uganda. Methane from enteric fermentation is expected to decrease by 4.81 per cent, while methane emissions from manure management are expected to increase by 0.59 per cent. The Programme is also expected to generate 54,435 tCO2e savings from energy generation and access.

## Economic and financial analysis.

The financial analysis assumes that the scenario's proposed dairy farm types of dairy farms will be adopted in all four countries. At a regional level, the aggregated Net Present Value (NPV) of the countries' incremental net benefit streams, discounted at 6 per cent, is US$158 million. The NPV for Kenya is US$20.4 million; Rwanda US$37.9 million; Uganda US$25.4 per cent for the baseline scenario. At a 6 per cent social discount rate, the EMNPV is US$158.0 million. Two additional scenarios are also considered: (a) with a low carbon market price (LCP) and (b) with a high carbon market price (HCP). Under the LCP scenario, the EMNPV is US$243.6 million; under HCP scenario, it is US$105.8 million. The sensitivity analysis shows that the balance with a low carbon market price (LCP) or with a high carbon market price (HCP). Under the LCP scenario, the EMNPV is approximately US$254.6 million. The sensitivity analysis confirms that DAMPA represents an economically worthwhile investment.

## Programme will directly contribute to the following targeted results in alignment with the GCF's Updated Strategic Plan (USP 3.1) by supporting the Country’s adaptation and mitigation goals).

T4 Food (2.5 million beneficiaries): The programme will support the development of a national dairy sector strategy, which includes the conservation of rangelands/pastures to be conserved, restored and sustainably managed through T9 locally-led adoption strategies. The Programme will also contribute to the achievement of GCF’s Strategic Objective 10: Support to T3 Climate Informational and Early Warning Systems (EWS). The Programme also aligns with T10 by supporting resilient agricultural systems.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## Observed climate change

15. Precipitation declined over most of the four countries during the 1950s (-4 mm/yr) and the 1960s (-21 mm/yr), with a significant decline over the 1970s (-16 mm/yr). There are also statistically significant differences on seasonal precipitation trends (p-value <0.05) across central Uganda between January and March, extending to the north of Lake Victoria in April and May, and extending southwards from Lake Victoria into Tanzania with a rate of -4 to 8 mm/yr over 1981-2016. On the other hand, a precipitation increase has also been observed in the southeast of Tanzania during the summer (January to March) over 1981-2016 (Figure 1).

### Figure 1: Changes in accumulated seasonal precipitation (mm) over the 1981–2016 period using the WISE dataset

| January-March (JFM) | April-June (AMJ) | July-September (ASO) | October-November (CDN) |
|---|---|---|---|
| | | | |

### 16. Temperature: The annual average maximum temperature (mean Tmax) has increased significantly (p-value <0.05) across Kenya, Uganda, and Tanzania. In particular, the western northern region and Kenya's north of Lake Turkana (for example), the annual mean Tmax has increased by +0.03 to 0.05°C/year, equivalent to a warming rate of -1.2 to -2.4 mm/yr. The eastern part of Uganda is also one of the warmest mean Tmax year-round among the four targeted countries. There is also a significant change in average day-to-day temperature (mean daily minimum temperature) across Kenya and Tanzania, with an increase in mean Tmin, the increase is observed in the east of Uganda and the northeast of Kenya where temperatures have remained relatively stable.

### 17. Mean relative humidity: The Rift Valley in Kenya has the lowest average annual relative humidity (mean RH <50 percent), while central and southern Uganda, especially the areas bordering Lake Victoria, have the highest mean RH (>80 percent). This is due to the high evaporation rates of water from lakes and rivers during the dry season (December to March) and the low rainfall in this region. The average annual relative humidity across Kenya has increased by 1-2 percentage points per year around due to the incoming moisture from the Indian Ocean. A statistically significant decrease (p-value <0.05) is observed over most of Tanzania, especially the northwestern part of the country.

### 18. Rainfall variability: The region's climatic seasons are influenced by a coupled tropical ocean-atmosphere system that includes the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ). A long rainy season occurs from March to May (MAM), and a short rainy season in October/November (OND). There is high rainfall variability due to large-scale oceanic atmospheric patterns such as the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) and Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## Extreme weather events

19. Dry-spells and very wet days. The average annual number of dry days (R≤1mm/day) in East Africa exceeds 365 days in the northern Rift Valley and northeastern Kenya. Conversely, the annual number of dry days decreases toward the interior of Kenya, Tanzania, and Uganda. For example, the average number of dry days per year is 208 days in the Republic of Congo (DRC). On average, Tanzania records 185 dry days/year; along its border with Uganda and Rwanda it averages only about 160 dry days/year. In Kenya, the average annual number of dry days is 347 days/year, equivalent to ~36 dry days per year. In 1981, in Uganda, the number of dry days by 0.5 day/year. The decrease in the number of dry days has been statistically significant (p-value < 0.05) over the entire Great Rift Valley in Kenya and Tanzania, with an average decrease rate of about -0.16 days per year since 1981. In contrast, neither the interior of Kenya, Tanzania nor Uganda have seen an increase or decrease in the number of very wet days since 1981.

### Figure 2: Spatiotemporal analysis of SPI-3 month over the 1981–2016 period in (a) Kenya, (b) Rwanda, (c) Uganda, and (d) Tanzania

![](image.png)

20. Extreme temperatures. Most of the increase in the number of very warm days (where T ≥ 30°C) on annual average is reported in the northern Rift Valley, where the number of very warm days has increased by 1.2 to +2.8 days/year since 1981. In addition, Uganda experienced an additional 40 to 100 very warm days per year as an annual average since 1981. In addition, northern Uganda experienced an increase in very warm days at a rate of ~0.8 days/year although this increase is not statistically significant.

Footnote 1: The C4MIP simulations are based on two representative concentration pathways (RCPs) namely RCP 2.6 (low emission scenario) and RCP 8.5 (high emission scenario). Climate and Agriculture Risk Visualization and Assessment (CAVA) is an approach that uses the WRF model to simulate climate change impacts in Africa, including East Africa. CAVA aims at simplifying and streamlining the use of past and future climate and impact data. CAVA analysis uses the WRF merged dataset over the 1976–2005 period to bias-correct the outputs of atmospheric models. More information available here: <https://www.cava.africa/>

21. **Announcement:** Some parts of East Africa may experience an increase in rainfall in the distant future (Figure 3) under various Representative Concentration Pathways (RCPs). And semi-arid regions are expected to become drier by mid-century, with a decrease in precipitation by the end of the century. In Kenya and Tanzania, a decrease in rainfall is expected under RCP 2.6 (<0.50mm/yr) and an increase under RCP 8.5 (+41.8mm/yr). In Uganda, climate projections show a decrease in rainfall of -0.30mm/yr by both RCPs (Figure 3).

**Figure 2:** Total annual rainfall (mm) projections under RCP 2.6 and 8.5 in (a) Kenya; (b), Rwanda; (c), Uganda, and (d) Tanzania.

22. **Mean maximum temperatures:** Under RCP 2.6, the mean Tmax is expected to peak in the mid-term (2040-2069); whereas under RCP 8.5, the highest mean Tmax increase is expected by the end of the century (2070-2099). Under RCP 8.5, mean Tmax may increase by up to +4.5°C in central parts of East Africa by the end of the century compared with the baseline period (1976–2005). The coastal areas are expected to experience a mean Tmax increase of approximately +3.5°C under RCP 8.5, respectively, by the end-of-century (2070-2099) compared to the baseline period (1976-2005). Similarly, because of the higher temperatures in the coastal areas, the mean minimum temperature is expected to increase more than inland areas over time. Overall, most models agree on a positive climate change signal for mean Tmax.

23. Mean minimum temperatures: The mean Tmin increase over East Africa is expected to follow the same spatial pattern as that of Tmax. However, the rate of change in the inland areas will be higher than that of Tmax. Overall, mean Tmin could increase by +1.5 to +2°C (RCP 2.6) and by +4.0 to +5.0°C (RCP 8.5) inland and by +3.0 to +4.0°C (RCP 2.6) and +3.5 to +4.0°C (RCP 8.5) on the Tanzanian and Kenyan coast by 2070–2099 compared to 1976-2005.

24. The mean relative humidity (mean RH) will change over the course of the century for RCPs 2.6 and 8.5. The annual mean RH (+2%) is expected to increase along Kenya and Lake Victoria. Under RCP <8.5, however, the mean RH is projected to decrease on a yearly average (-2 to +4%) in the central parts of Tanzania, with a high rate towards the south.

25. Rainfall variability: In all countries, positive SPI-3 values will remain lower under RCP 8.5 than RCP 2.6, implying fewer wet years under RCP 8.5. For negative SPI-3 values (dry periods), negative trends are accumulated in western Uganda and Tanzania, implying that 3-month precipitation values are increasingly more dispersed from the mean.

26. Dry spells: The models show a positive agreement in the trend of emergence on the number of dry days (R40mm/day), slightly earlier and widespread for RCP 8.5 (2020-2050) compared to RCP 2.6 (2040-2060). The potential increase is mainly over western Kenya, all of Uganda and Rwanda, and most parts of Tanzania. Conversely, the timing of the emergence of moderate dry spells (R10mm/day) will be delayed for RCP 8.5 compared to RCP 2.6, mainly in eastern and southern Kenya. In addition, most of the increase in very wet days (R200mm/day) is projected over western Uganda, Rwanda, Tanzania, and some parts of Kenya under RCP 8.5.

The number of dry spells per year will be reduced by about one-third for RCPs 2.6 and 4.5 compared to the present day. For RCP 8.5, this reduction is projected to be more than half a spell per year by the end of the century (2070-2099). For RCP 8.5, the increase in very wet days is widespread over Uganda, Rwanda, Tanzania, and some parts of Kenya.

In the low emission scenario (RCP 2.6), +20 to +75 additional very warm days on average per year are expected in Uganda, the Rift Valley of Kenya, and central Tanzania in the mid-term (2040-2069). Most of the increase in very warm days is projected for the low emission scenario compared with historical conditions. ## EXPOSURE

### Exposure in the presence of people, livelihoods, species, and ecosystems that could be affected by hazards, more particularly droughts and floods that cause disasters (Figure 7).

#### In East Africa, droughts tend to be more frequent, longer, and more severe in boreal spring and summer; as rainfall and water storage decrease overall. Table 1 shows how drought increases under different climate scenarios. Climate change is expected to increase the frequency of extreme events such as droughts, floods, heatwaves, and cold spells. These changes will lead to large-scale livestock losses, decreased pasture regeneration, and an increased prevalence of drought-induced diseases in animals. Droughts are also likely to reduce the availability of water for pasture regeneration, feed production, water and hygienic practices in the dairy production system.

B


### 30. Floods
East Africa experiences frequent flooding due to climate variability and anthropogenic factors that exacerbate people's exposure to flooding (Figure 7). Among its flood-prone areas, Uganda has the highest number of flood-related casualties, particularly in the Northern, Eastern, and Central regions along the White Nile River. Each year, floods cause an average of 100 deaths per administrative boundary in Uganda. In Kenya, floods affect up to 36,000 people per administrative boundary in the Northern region. In Tanzania, floods affect 10,000 to 25,000 people annually.

### 31. Disasters
In East Africa, between 1971 and 2023, more than 95 million people were affected by disasters caused by meteorological (storms), hydrological (floods, landslides) and climatological (droughts) events. Since 1971, the number of people affected has increased from about 48% to over 60%. Over this period, floods accounted for about 80 percent related to floods and 17 percent drought. Over time, the number of disasters has increased exponentially, with a significant increase in the number of droughts.

### Vulnerability
GC also poses many challenges and threats to the East African dairy sector, which is subject to pre-existing physical and socio-economic vulnerabilities. By reducing these vulnerabilities, which will be exacerbated by climate change, the sector becomes more resilient. These vulnerabilities are presented below.

**Page 16**

### Poverty and income inequality

Kenyan, Tanzanian, Rwandan and Ugandan societies are marked by important poverty and income inequality. The Human Development Index (HDI) 2022 ranking in Kenya (146th), Rwanda (135th), Tanzania (178th) and Uganda (190th) reflects this reality. Women, who make up the majority of the agricultural workforce in these countries, are particularly vulnerable to climate change.

### Limited access to quality inputs

Feed that reduces enteric emissions, veterinary services and climate resilient crops are limited for smallholder farmers. This leads many farmers to prefer local or crossbred animals that are not as high-yielding, whereas exotic breeds are highly susceptible to local disease. In addition, limited access to improved breeding induces genetic progress.

### Frequent disease outbreaks

Foot-and-mouth disease, mastitis, and East Coast Fever (ECF) are common in the four countries. These diseases reduce milk production, increased veterinary costs. Poor disease control measures, inadequate quarantine facilities, and limited veterinary services contribute to the prevalence of diseases in the dairy farming sector.


| Figure 8: Level of degradation of rangelands and pastures in the four countries between 2012 and 2020 (left) and carrying capacity, expressed in above-ground biomass sustain (ton of dry matter/night) |

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 14

#### Dairy processing facilities, energy supply and access to markets
Dairy farmers in East Africa face inadequate infrastructure along the dairy value chain. The availability of reliable (and sustainable) energy supply is also a challenge. Milk prices are subject to significant fluctuations and market volatility are common challenges. Most milk prices are determined by bulkers and processors who buy directly from small-scale dairy farmers, which can lead to low prices for producers. Small-scale dairy farmers and pastoralists sell their milk at mobile collection centers (MCCs), dairy cooperatives and business alliances. More details are provided in the Market Assessment section.

#### Limited access to credit and financial services
Critical aspects of dairy farming such as infrastructure, especially fencing and sheds require access to credit and finance. Small-scale dairy farmers and pastoralists lack access to credit facilities for investments in modern equipment, infrastructure, and improved livestock.

#### Land tenure insecurity
Land tenure security is a major barrier to sustainability, particularly for women’s land ownership. Access to natural capital, particularly women's and youth ownership, management and control of resources are critical issues which are major barriers to sustainability, and the adoption of climate-resilient and low-carbon practices (Annex 2).

#### CLIMATE CHANGE IMPACTS
33. About 70 percent of East African agriculture is rain-fed, with small-scale farmers relying on seasonal rainfall for feed, fodder crops and pasture. As dairy systems are subject to significant seasonal, temporal, and spatial variability in climate conditions, the resilience of the dairy system depends on its ability to adapt to different climate conditions and to cope with the impacts of climate change. Identifying adaptation needs facilitates prioritizing actions and allocating resources for addressing critical challenges.

34. Rising temperatures are changing agriculture, livestock and dairy, the economic backbone of East Africa. Water scarcity, pest outbreaks, and declining productivity threaten food and nutrition security. Droughts and extreme weather events are becoming more frequent in East Africa as a result of climate change and different climatic scenarios. Areas affected by extreme drought will expand faster than those affected by severe and moderate droughts. The dairy sector is particularly vulnerable to these impacts due to the high mortality rates, deaths, damage to infrastructure and loss of livelihoods. The dairy sector's adaptive capacity stems from the following:

**Page 18**

B

# Access to climate-smart practices and technologies. Improved dairy breeds, drought-tolerant forage varieties, and efficient milk processing equipment are some of the climate-smart technologies farmers need. These can be provided through public-private partnerships.

## Capacity building and knowledge transfer. Farmers' technical knowledge and skills in dairy management can be enhanced by providing training programmes, Livestock Farmer Field Schools (LFFS), and extension services on sustainable practices that reduce vulnerability to climate change.

## Financial and market support. Affordable financing supports farmers’ investments in productive assets, such as improved livestock breeds, forage varieties, and efficient milk processing equipment. Agribusiness can provide input and output markets that improve the productivity of smallholder dairy farmers.

## Strengthening infrastructure. Improving rural infrastructure, such as water harvesting systems, renewable energy sources, and access to credit, reduces vulnerability by increasing resilience against climate change impacts on farmers, and reduce the drudgery associated with fetching water by women.

## Climate information services. Early warning of droughts, floods, pests, diseases, and disease control can be made through climate information, early warning systems, and advisory services. This will help dairy farmers anticipate and manage climate-related risks. These skills equip youth in agri- and service providers in dissemination of new information.

## Policy support and institutional strengthening. To enable dairy farmers to thrive, it is imperative to develop policies that address the challenges faced by smallholder dairy farmers. These include addressing gender inequalities and youths; as well as strengthening research and extension advice. Key adaptation recommendations are: (i) improving access to credit; (ii) promoting collaboration between governments, research institutes, dairy cooperatives, and financial institutions.

### IMPACT OF CLIMATE CHANGE

35. East Africa’s dairy sector is one of the most vulnerable sectors to climate change, the impact of which are felt at all levels of the dairy value chain (figure 9). The impacts on the dairy farm level are as follows: (i) a decrease in milk production and quality; (ii) an increase in disease prevalence due to pathogens, parasites, and other pests; (iii) increased mortality rates for calves and young stock; (iv) reduced reproductive performance of cows due to heat stress; (v) increased incidence of mastitis; (vi) increased incidence of dystocia; (vii) increased incidence of abortion; (viii) a decrease in the quantity and quality of pasture; (ix) degeneration of fodder in excess access to moisture and fertility; (x) a decrease in range land and pasture productivity and forage quality. At the dairy value chain level, climate change can compromise the safety of milk and dairy products, higher risks of milk losses, and higher costs per unit of produce.

Figure 9: Potential impacts of climate hazards on the livestock land-based food supply chain

# Climate change and milk yields

Temperature and humidity have a significant impact on milk production (Amer et al., Feasibility Study, Chapter 2). Significant milk production losses have been observed in Kenya, Uganda, and Tanzania's coastal regions between 1981 and 2016, from 4 to 8 kg/day for the Holstein-Friesian breed and below 3.5 kg/day for indigenous breeds.

## Ruminant feed and forage supplies are most affected by climate change due to changes in cereal, forage, and rangeland productivity

Changes in temperature and CO2 levels will affect the composition of pastures by altering the species competition dynamics due to changes in optimal growth rates. In addition, availability of nutrients is expected to decrease because of increased evaporation and transpiration.

## B

### Greenhouse Gas Emission Profile of the Dairy Sector

#### 40. Dairy production systems in Kenya:
In Kenya, 39 percent of milk is produced by intensive systems or zero-grazing systems, 43 percent by semi-intensive systems, and 16 percent by extensive systems.
In Rwanda, 93 percent of the milk comes from traditional systems (Figure 10). In Tanzania, dairy systems are traditional and intensive. The traditional system is characterized by meat production and the intensive system by milk production. In Uganda, 85 percent of the milk is produced in semi-intensive or extensive systems.
In Ethiopia, dairy systems are also traditional and intensive (Figure 10). Traditional systems produce 98 percent of the country's milk.

#### Figure 10: Livestock Production Systems in East Africa

| Kenya | Rwanda | Tanzania | Uganda |
|---|---|---|---|
| ![Kenya](kenya.png) | ![Rwanda](rwanda.png) | ![Tanzania](tanzania.png) | ![Uganda](uganda.png) |

#### 41. Absolute GHG Emissions (Baseline, Present and Future):
Methane (CH₄), nitrous oxide (N₂O) and carbon dioxide (CO₂) are released by ruminants (cattle, sheep, goats) during the enteric fermentation and through manure production.
Enteric methane emissions from the dairy sector dominate the GHG emission inventories of Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania and Uganda. The majority of these emissions come from cattle in East Africa. Manure represents a waste in the production process that doesn't result in milk or meat. In addition, cattle manure releases N₂O, which is 298 times more potent than CO₂ over the long-term (100-year time period) [IPCC, 2007], significantly contributes to the region's carbon footprint.
See studies produced by I&M and Company (acting FAO), in the context of the project formulation studies.

## Prepared under Feasibility Study
The dairy sector in East Africa is a major source of GHG with 27 times higher global warming potential than carbon dioxide over the long term - 100 year time period [IPCC, 2007], significantly contributes to the region's carbon footprint.

## GHG EMISSIONS FROM DAIRY CATTLE IN AFRICA

### Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania and Uganda

42. In Kenya, GHG emissions have increased from 57 MCO2e in 1990 to 94 MCO2e in 2010. Agriculture produces the largest emission. The GLEAM3 tool of the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO), estimated that dairy cattle (4.5 million animals) emit 64 MCO2e eq (based on data from 2010). The dairy sector emits about 78 percent of total GHG emissions, while enteric methane accounts for 93 percent of these emissions caused by enteric methane, while 11 percent is caused by manure management. While business-as-usual (BAU) scenarios are projected to increase dairy cattle numbers and milk production and enteric CH4 emissions will respectively increase by 43 and 36 percent by 2050 under current practices, in a baseline scenario, the number of dairy cattle is expected to decrease by about 17.5 percent.

43. In Rwanda, total GHG emissions were estimated to be 6.7 MCO2e in 2018, of which 3.3 MCO2e or 49 percent from agriculture. During the last decade, agriculture, forestry, and other land use (AFOLU) emissions increased by 34 percent. Methane emissions from the livestock sector represent about 46 percent of total GHG emissions in Rwanda. The dairy sector is responsible for 70 percent of these methane emissions with a contribution of 15 percent and 11 percent, respectively. Dairy systems in Rwanda emit about 3.6 MCO2e eq in 2018. In the baseline scenario, dairy cattle numbers are expected to increase by 49 percent between 2017 and 2050 while milk production is projected to decrease by 11 percent. The number of dairy cows will increase from 36,000 in 2017 to 53,800 in 2050. Projections based on milk demand under a BAU scenario (no policy change) indicate that the number of dairy cattle is expected to decrease by about 4 percent between 2017 and 2050.

44. In Tanzania, total GHG emissions are estimated to be 286.5 MCO2e eq in which agriculture contributed 49.7 MCO2e or 17 percent. Methane emissions represent 94 percent of total GHG emissions from dairy cattle systems. Enteric methane emissions account for about 30 percent and manure management for the remaining 64 percent. Emissions from the dairy sector are expected to increase by mid-century, in response to the projected daily herd growth to satisfy increasing demand for milk. The number of dairy cows is expected to increase from 152,800 in 2017 to about 390,000 in 2030 and to billion litres in 2050. Projections based on milk demand under a BAU scenario (no policy change) indicate that the number of dairy cattle is expected to decrease by about 4 percent between 2017 and 2050.

FAO’s GLEAM model estimates that enteric CH4 emissions will increase by 117 percent by 2050, under a baseline scenario.

### GHG EMISSIONS IN UGANDA

45. GHG emissions in Uganda totalled 95 MCO2e in 2017, of which the AFOLU sector accounted for 79 MCO2e or 84 percent. The GLEAM tool estimated that the dairy sector in 2019-24 is MCO2eq (30 percent of the total). The dairy sector emits about 56 percent of these methane emissions with a contribution of 2 and 7 percent, respectively. FAO’s GLEAM model estimates that emissions from manure management (4 percent or 2 MCO2eq) dominate GHG emissions from dairy cattle sector while enteric methane emissions are the largest in Eastern, Central, Southern and Northern/humid regions). FAO’s GLEAM model estimates that milk production and enteric CH4 emissions will increase by 50 percent by 2050, under a baseline scenario.

### GHG EMISSIONS FROM DAIRY CATTLE IN KENYA, RWANDA, TANZANIA AND UGANDA

46. Total GHG emissions from dairy cattle in Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania and Uganda are presented in Figure 11. The pattern follows the distribution of the dairy herd in the country.

## Figure 11: Direct GHG emissions from Dairy Cattle population (a) Kenya; (b) Tanzania; (c) Uganda; (d) Rwanda[4]

### Emission Intensities (EI): The methane emission intensity, defined as the volume of methane emissions generated per kg of milk, varies widely across intensive, semi-intensive and extensive dairy production systems, with more productive systems having much lower EIs per kg of milk. In East Africa, EIs vary from 1-5 kg CO2eq/kg milk for extensively managed herds to less than 0.3 kg CO2eq/kg milk in highly productive commercial dairy farms. There are performances between (but also within) systems and the potential for improvements, including by increase of efficiency of feed use, reduction of methane emissions per unit of milk produced or improvement of animal health status.

There are performances between (but also within) systems and the potential for improvements, including by increase of efficiency of feed use, reduction of methane emissions per unit of milk produced or improvement of animal health status.

52. PROPOSED CLIMATE SOLUTIONS: The Programmes will address the climate change adaptation and mitigation issues across the various systems, including livestock farming along the value-chain value, while addressing the barriers identified in the theory of change (see B.2). It will scale-up: (i) farm and herd-level practices to increase productivity, reduce emission intensity, improve resilience and reduce emissions; (ii) improved feed management and nutrient recycling, to improve circularity, resilience and reduce emissions; (iii) landscape-level interventions to enhance soil health, water retention, and water and biomass supply for animal feed; and (iv) value-chain level interventions, to reduce milk losses (and consequently methane emissions), increase the efficiency of livestock production systems, and increase the productivity of dairy farms. Each intervention will be accompanied by a detailed plan, outlining the climate drivers with interventions and their benefits is available in Annex 2, Chapter 6.

53. Interventions at farm and herd levels: Interventions will focus on various climate risks and impacts, namely the decrease in milk yields, heat stress of animals, the increase in cattle mortality, the change in dairy cattle diseases, and the change in feed availability due to changes in rainfall patterns and humidity. For all dairy systems, the Programme will encourage the combined adoption of climate adaptation and mitigation interventions at farm level (e.g., improved pasture management, use of drought-tolerant and heat-adapted improved indigenous livestock breeds, seed selection), improved herd health (e.g., vaccination against East Coast Fever, Johne’s disease, and other diseases), and improved feed management. For smallholder dairy and zero-grazing systems, in addition to the above, interventions will include the availability of forage grasses and legumes, as well as the use of silage. The intervention at herd level will include the establishment of forage grasses and legumes (Calliandra, Leucaena, etc.), the use of conserved silage

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 21

## On-farm solutions for nutrient management and reduction of GHG emissions

### Interventions at the landscape level

54. **On-farm solutions for nutrient management and reduction of GHG emissions**. These interventions will address the CH4 emission problem, which is a major contributor to the loss of soil carbon and other ecosystem services. Interventions include promoting simple circular manure management practices to reduce nutrient losses and GHG emissions (e.g., rotational grazing), the use of improved pasture species and management practices to improve forage quality and quantity, and the use of multipurpose leguminous trees.

55. **Interventions at the landscape level** will address the negative impact of CH4 on ecosystem degradation through practices that result in reduced soil carbon sequestration and increased vulnerability and quality of pasture, reduced feed and water availability, and livestock carrying capacity, all leading to lower milk yields. The interventions will promote simple circular manure management practices (e.g., rotational grazing) and other nutrient reduction practices. Land management activities will rehabilitate degraded rangelands to increase soil carbon sequestration, provide additional forage resources, and improve pasture quality.

56. **Interventions at the dairy value chain level** will include the upgrading or equipping MCGC milk collection points (MCPs) and processing centers. This will increase the supply of milk through formal aggregation and processing, reduce post-harvest losses and carbon footprint, and reduce GHG emissions from the use of photovoltaic (PV) systems as a renewable energy (RE) source for cooling, heating, and lighting.

### Target Areas

58. **Climate risk**. A climate risk mapping (Figure 13) was conducted by overlaying the sum of climatic hazards, exposure, vulnerability, and subtracting the adaptive capacity. The maps show that most of the region has a high risk to climate change impacts in both the mid-term (2040-2069) and far future (2070-2089).

### ADDITIONALITY OF THE PROGRAMME
(2) The Programme is a unique comprehensive framework to address concomitantly climate change impacts and mitigation in the dairy sector. As mentioned above, it will contribute to the NDCs, NAP, Nationally Appropriate Mitigation Action (NAMA), the Integrated Agriculture in National Adaptation Plans (NAP-Ag), the National Communication (NC), Biennial Update Reports (BURs) in East Africa.
- It is also fully in line with IFAD's climate adaptation and mitigation priorities outlined in the first IFAD Country Strategic Programme (CSP).
- The Programme aims to leverage existing technologies, the Programme aims to trigger resilient low-carbon development of the dairy sector, with a focus on emission reduction.

### Sources of methane from the dairy sector are among the main categories of anthropogenic GHG emissions from Agriculture, Forestry and Land Use (AFOLU) sector identified in national inventories (e.g., National Communication (NC), Biennial Update Reports (BURs)) in East Africa. Long-term low emission development strategies (LEDS) for agriculture should be developed to address these sources.
- In addition, the dairy sector is currently affected by several CC impacts, which requires more investment in the adaptation and mitigation measures that are needed to reduce the vulnerability of this sector and contribute to long-term gains in global efforts this decade. This strategy is regarded as the most effective in achieving the goal of limiting warming.

### In this regard, the Programme is a flagship and pioneering investment vehicle to support the Global Methane Pledge (GMP). The GMP was launched at the Conference of Parties (COP) 26 in November 2021 in Glasgow. Pledges are voluntary agreements by countries to reduce methane emissions from agriculture by at least 34 percent from 2020 levels by 2030, which could eliminate warming of more than 0.2°C by 2050.
- Participants also commit to using the highest tier IPCC good practice inventory methodologies, as well as continuously improve the accuracy, transparency, consistency, comparability and completeness of national

69. The TcC presented below outlines the intervention logic of the Programme (Figure 15a). This section describes the logical framework for the Programme’s goal statement and its underlying assumptions.

Programme's Goal Statement is as follows: "If practices and technological solutions for reducing methane and other greenhouse gas emissions are adopted, and if complementary services that efficiently sustain production and marketing are utilized. THEN a decrease in producers' and other stakeholders' vulnerability to climate change will be achieved."

This is the case because the pathways to low-emissions and climate-resilient dairy production will be perceived as financially beneficial to farmers, pastoralists, and processors.

### Barriers

- Misalignment of multiple sector policies, programs, and funding streams.
- Limited technical capacity to adapt to the changing climate in dairy farmers and extension workers.
- Lack of access to information on climate variability and its impacts on livestock production.
- Dependence on rainfall for animal fodder and feed production, as well as rangeland degradation.
- Limited milk buffering capacity due to lack of storage facilities and infrastructure.
- Limited decision-making power over land use decisions and resource allocation.
- Limited access to extension and control of resources.

Adaptation and mitigation needs include: access to climate-smart technologies; improved livestock management practices; improved infrastructure and facilities; climate information systems; and policy support and institutional strengthening. More details on the barriers are found in the accompanying study.

70. As highlighted in the TcC, the multifaceted barriers (institutional, planning, knowledge, social, natural, technological and financial) that hinder the achievement of the Programme goal are: (i) misalignment of multiple sector policies, programs, and funding streams; (ii) limited technical capacity to adapt to the changing climate in dairy farmers and extension workers; (iii) lack of access to information on climate variability and its impacts on livestock production; (iv) dependence on rainfall for animal fodder and feed production, as well as rangeland degradation; (v) limited milk buffering capacity due to lack of storage facilities and infrastructure.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 27

## Pathways to Zero Emission and Resilient Dairy Farming in East Africa

### Background

The dairy sector faces significant challenges related to climate change in East Africa. Dairy farming contributes substantially to GHG emissions, with CC threatening its sustainability. Inefficiencies at the dairy farm level result in economic losses for farmers and increased costs of milk production. These inefficiencies include poor feed management, low-quality feed and fodder, obtaining extension and financial services, and managing risks. Low genetic potential, herd health problems, lack of access to quality inputs, inadequate infrastructure, limited availability of high-yielding and low-carbon practices and technologies, rangeland degradation and gender inequalities further hamper the dairy sector.

### Programme Aim

The Programme aims to address the following challenges: (i) strengthening climate resilience; (ii) reducing emissions across the dairy value chain; (iii) the reduction of GHG emissions per kg of milk; and (iv) improving the livelihoods of smallholder dairy farmers. The Programme will achieve this through a multi-sectoral approach, addressing barriers from improving policy, institutional and service delivery environments to building the capacity of smallholder dairy farmers in East Africa.

### Objectives

1. **Outcome 1: Systemic and Institutional capacities in the dairy sector are strengthened to enable smallholder dairy farmers to adopt low-emission, climate-resilient practices and technologies that reduce GHG emissions.**
   - To achieve this objective, the Programme aims to achieve the following three Outcomes:

2. **Outcome 2: Smallholder dairy farmers and private sector value chain operators adopt low-emission, climate-resilient, and sustainable practices for increased production and access market knowledge and tools.**

3. **Outcome 3: The Programme will establish a climate-responsive policy and regulatory environment for the dairy sector (Outcome 1.4) that ensures improved animal health, feed quality, manure, and herd management issues. This will help scaling up climate-smart practices and technologies in the dairy sector to reduce GHG emissions per kg of milk while improving farm productivity, animal health, feed quality, manure, and herd management issues.**

### Programme Components

1. **Outcome 2.1: Supports dairy farmers to ensure that smallholder dairy farmers have a stable market for their products, are financially interested in adopting the pathway innovations, and have access to energy solutions at cooperative level will be supported. The promotion of integrated pathway-specific solutions (Output 2.2) will enable smallholder dairy farmers to adopt low-emission practices and technologies while improving farm productivity, animal health, feed quality, manure management for沼气production and nutrient recycling. This will increase productivity, improve circularity and reduce GHG emission intensity at animal, herd and farm levels.**

## B

### Dairy value chain actors (including agro-input and technology providers, well-organized dairy cooperatives of SFP aggregators and processors) will be supported in overcoming technological and financial barriers to increase their productivity and profitability.

To contribute to the envisaged paradigm shift, two Outputs will be delivered (Figure 15b). Under Output 2.1.a, a GDFF investment window will invest in climate-smart practices and technologies. The Investment Window will have a dual purpose: (a) it will provide access to finance for dairy actors to invest in multiple productivity and climate-smart technologies and practices; and (b) it will provide technical assistance to the dairy sector to support the development of business cases, including financial models, for dairy sector businesses. Under Output 2.1.b & GDFF Technical Assistance (TA) Facility will be established to maximize both impact and scale of investments in climate-smart technologies and practices by providing technical assistance to smallholder and/or green dairy sector portfolios and its borrowing companies to implement effective green strategies, improve operational efficiency and reduce costs. The TA facility will also provide support for the development of business cases and financial models for greater climate-smart financing in the GDFF target regions. Together, these outputs contribute to the overall outcome of increasing productivity and profitability of dairy value chains.

The three Outcomes described in the ToC are delivered through 3 dedicated and interconnected components, namely: Component 1: Enabling low-emission and climate-resilient dairy value chains development; Component 2: Enabling low-emission and climate-resilient dairy value chain diversification; and Component 3: Enabling low-emission and climate-resilient dairy value chain transformation. The achievement of Outputs under full control of the Programme, they aim to result in specific outcomes, whether in behavioural or systemic change/outcomes will take place between the completion of the program outputs and the achievement of impact.

## PROGRAMME BENEFICIARIES

The Programme will benefit about 2.5 million individual dairy farmers and dairy value chain actors, at least 40 percent of whom are women, 30 percent of whom are youth and 10 percent are marginalized groups (including people with disabilities). As the geographic and social targeting of BaMa is fully aligned with the IFAD projects in the four countries, the Programme will be implemented through existing IFAD projects. The dairy farmers who participate directly or aim to sell milk in formal and informal markets. These smallholder dairy farmers generally have between 1 to 50 dairy cows per farm. They are mostly located in rural areas of the four countries and can be considered as the main target group and can be considered as rural poor. They are the essential segment of producers who need to be scaled up, supporting the formulation of the dairy sector and broader sustainable transformation based on sound

### Benefits of Component 3 (Figure 17a) are:
- medium-sized/medium-large commercial dairy farmers (5 or more dairy cows, 5–9 kg of milk/livestock in semi-intensive systems)
- smallholder dairy farmers and pastoralists
- agro-pastoralists and agro-pastoralists.
- medium-and large-scale extensive farmers through the rehabilitation/investment of existing dairy farms and/or establishment of new dairy farms.

### Programme beneficiaries and focus:
- 178,362 ha
- 16.35 million individuals
- 2.7 million dairy cattle
- 2.5 million individuals

## COMPONENT 1: STRENGTHENED NATIONAL CAPACITIES AND ENHANCED ENABLING ENVIRONMENT FOR LOW EMISSIONS CLIMATE RESILIENT DAIRY SECTORS

### This Component will support the paradigm shift towards a more productive, climate-resilient, and low-emission dairy sector. It will focus on:
- reducing methane (CH4) and other GHG emissions from livestock
- addressing knowledge barriers identified in the ToC
- fostering an enabling environment for policy, institutional, and service delivery reforms to support smallholder dairy farmers and value chain actors.

### Key areas of focus include:
- "Most vulnerable people and communities", and ARAA “Ecosystems and ecosystem services”.

### Section 8: The Component 1 Will have four Outputs, namely (i) an enabling policy and regulatory environment including improved policies, regulations, and national standards (ii) improved public services for the dairy sector through the provision of specialized livestock extension services and veterinary services; (iii) improved capacity to monitor GHG emissions from livestock systems in Kenya; and (iv) improved climate-smart management. Component 1 activities will provide public service goods and will therefore be funded by grants.

#### 87. The activities carried out under the Component are sustainable. The Programme will carry out a series of activities that will build capacity for the dairy sector, improve the efficiency of livestock production systems, increase the capacity of key stakeholders of the dairy sector, to provide efficient services for greater efficiency, climate resilience and adaptation. These include: (i) improving the capacity of key stakeholders in the dairy sector; (ii) implementing climate-smart policies at national and regional levels, enabling trade across Africa to unlock potential benefits from these policies; (iii) supporting the implementation of climate-smart policies and to ensure that the dairy sector fully contributes to the NDC of the participating countries.

#### 87. The activities carried out under the Component have the potential for scaling-up and replication. The Programme will carry out a series of activities that will build capacity for the dairy sector, improve the efficiency of livestock production systems in East Africa which has been piloted by the FAO and IRLR, particularly in Kenya with a Gold Standard pilot project. In addition, innovative climate risk analysis support tools are being developed to provide the necessary work needed to obtain data on GHG emissions from Eastern African livestock systems which remain scarce, as most emission inventories have been based on estimates or assumptions rather than actual measurements and do not represent East African livestock systems well. In addition, in the absence of specific data on the regional dairy sector, it is difficult for countries to meet their NDC targets under the Paris Agreement, including through their GHG inventories and prevents African countries from tracking progress on mitigation in their livestock sectors. In addition, innovative climate risk analysis support tools are being developed to provide the necessary work needed to obtain data on GHG emissions from Eastern African livestock systems which remain scarce, as most emission inventories have been based on estimates or assumptions rather than actual measurements and do not represent East African livestock systems well.

#### 87. The activities carried out under the Component will need the data and capacity to set up and maintain MRV systems. In addition, innovative climate risk analysis support tools are being developed to provide the necessary work needed to obtain data on GHG emissions from Eastern African livestock systems which remain scarce, as most emission inventories have been based on estimates or assumptions rather than actual measurements and do not represent East African livestock systems well.

### Sub-activity 1.1.1.1 Support the review and preparation of national dairy policies, strategies, and regulations for animal breeding, feed formulation, and mitigation practices

The main activities to be carried out are as follows: In Kenya (i) The Dairy Industry Strategy and Investment Plan to guide the sustainable development of the dairy sector; (ii) the National Strategy for Animal Breeding and regulations (iii) the draft National Dairy Act (2015); (iv) the National Strategy for Animal Breeding and regulations (v) the draft National Dairy Act (2015); (vi) the National Strategy for Feed Formulation and feed quality standards; and (vii) milk and milk product standards on food safety and quality. In Rwanda, the Strategic Plan for Livestock Development (SPLD) will be reviewed to ensure alignment with the national dairy sector framework. Within this framework, the Programme will support the review of: (i) national climate and national environment policies; (ii) feed formulation regulations; (iii) animal breeding regulations; (iv) mitigation practices currently under development which includes climate resilient livestock production; (v) the regulatory framework for the private veterinary practice including Community Animal Health Workers (CAHWs); (vi) the National Dairy Act to update regulations on raw milk trade; (vii) the formulation of disease contingency and control plans; (viii) review of national dairy policies, strategies, and regulations for animal breeding, feed formulation, and mitigation practices. In Tanzania, the Programme will support finalizing the draft national Livestock Policy as well as supporting the review of: (i) national climate and environment policies; (ii) feed formulation regulations; (iii) animal breeding regulations; (iv) review of the Animal Diseases Act (2003); (v) review of the Animal Welfare Act (2008), with focus on livestock welfare in dairy production systems; (vi) draft National Livestock Policy; and (vii) draft National Dairy Policy. In Uganda, the Programme will support: (i) finalizing the National Livestock Policy; (ii) review of national climate and environment policies; (iii) feed formulation regulations; (iv) animal breeding regulations; (v) research strategies on climate-resilient feed germplasm to offset predicted yield declines in maize; (vi) draft National Dairy Policy; (vii) draft National Livestock Policy; (viii) draft National Animal Breeding Act; and (ix) support drafting of regulations for the Food and Agriculture Regulatory Authority, livestock inspection services, and animal health services. In Zambia, the Programme will support: (i) review of national climate and environment policies; (ii) feed formulation regulations; (iii) animal breeding regulations; (iv) draft National Dairy Act; (v) review of national dairy policies, strategies, and regulations for animal breeding, feed formulation, and mitigation; (vi) review land policies and regulate to mitigate land tenure insecurity for dairy farmers.

#### Sub-activity 1.1.1.2 Support the regional policy dialogue to mainstream climate change adaptation and mitigation practices in national dairy platforms

The Programme will support a regional level assessment and prioritization of climate change adaptation and mitigation practices, including feed formulation regulations (common external tariffs), to accelerate regional convergence in diary standards, regional trade and market access for dairy products, and the development of climate-resilient dairy production systems. For this to happen, recurrent national policy dialogues will be organized at the sub-regional level involving key stakeholders from participating countries. Activities will be coordinated by regional coordination and policy dialogue experts working with the Regional Dairy Platform (RDP) and other relevant actors in the region including women and young people, who will be supported to participate.

#### Sub-activity 1.1.1.3 National dairy value chain governance and awareness creation on climate change adaptation and mitigation practices

At national level, the national dairy boards (KDB, DDA, TDB, Phiru Dairy Board) will organize regular meetings with key stakeholders from the dairy value chain including milk collectors, milk processors, service providers of artificial insemination (AI), veterinary services, extension staff and local government, ensuring participation quotas for women and youth.

#### Sub-activity 1.1.1.4 District/regional level dairy value chain governance and planning

Local-level stakeholder platforms will organize meetings from the dairy value chain stakeholders will participate in the development of district or regional dairy plans to mainstream climate change adaptation and mitigation practices, including feed formulation regulations (common external tariffs), into national dairy policies and strategies.

94. Activity 1.1.2 Launch awareness campaigns on nutritional benefits of milk and food safety aspects. Support the development and nutrition education accompanied by social change, targeting smallholder producer and consumer households, informal dairy value chain actors and milk handlers in Kenya (ii) Rwanda, Uganda, Tanzania, Malawi, Mozambique, Zambia, Zimbabwe, and South Africa to enhance their individual health and climate resilience. The Programmes will support milk consumption and nutrition education through various platforms such as extension services, radio, television, mobile phone applications, social media, products, accompanied by nutrition education and social behavior change communication (SBC) to reach out to the target groups. The SBC will be conducted in collaboration with relevant stakeholders including NGOs (see B.3 and A.20), through service providers, i.e., (i) SDLO, KDB in Kenya; (ii) PNPD and the Single Programme Implementation Unit (SPIU) in Rwanda; (iii) MAF in Uganda; (iv) NEMA and the National Dairy Development Board (NDA) together with the Ministry of Health (MoH), Ministry of Education and Sports, SNV and UNDP, in Uganda.

95. Sub-activity 1.1.2.1 Public awareness on the contribution of milk to nutrition and resilience, with focus on food safety aspects. The Programmes will use various communication platforms such as extension, FLEET, MCC/Cooperatives. Topics will include improved milk handling practices, safe storage, and consumption of milk. In Kenya, the Programmes will target various community platforms such as extension, FLEET, MCC/Cooperatives. Topics will include improved milk handling practices, safe storage, and consumption of milk. In Rwanda, it will include participation in dairy fairs/sessions to showcase climate-friendly and energy efficient local processing technologies and products. The Programmes will also support the implementation of the UPU school milk programs for improved nutrient of vulnerable children.

96. Sub-activity 1.1.2.2 Build capacity of dairy inspection to ensure milk quality and safety and reduce waste. The Programmes will build capacity of dairy inspectors in Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda, Tanzania, and Malawi on handling formal methods of milk testing and sampling for the detection of growth and reduction of milk rejection and waste (less GHG emission intensities). The Programme will promote the use of mobile phone applications to monitor milk quality and safety standards. The Programmes will also support enforcement of milk quality and safety standards. Women and young people who are often involved in the enforcement of milk quality and safety standards.

97. Activity 1.1.3 Improve land tenure security for sustainable dairy farming. The Programme will adopt a fit-for-purpose approach to identify and mitigate tenure risks. Activities to mitigate this risk will vary depending on the type of tenure system, including: (i) developing national guidelines for the identification, assessment, and protection of rangelands and on supporting participatory planning and management; In Rwanda, all land is registered and managed by the government. The Programme will support the development of a national framework for land use and land use planning. Activities will include capacity building and support to inclusive and participatory planning processes at local level. The Programmes will also support the establishment of a national land information system and enforcement of tenure laws, including the registration of communal lands; (ii) National Land Commission (NLC) and County Governments in Kenya; (iii) the district land management committee in Tanzania; (iv) the national land commission in Malawi; (v) the national land use planning commission in Tanzania; and (vi) MAAP and District Local Government (DLG) in Uganda.

98. Sub-activity 1.1.3.1 Mapping of local tenure risks and preparation of training materials. The Programmes will conduct a participatory mapping exercise to identify and assess the tenure security risk, including capacity assessment, including a tenure risk assessment for the Programme target areas; (ii) develop training material on land use planning and management, focusing on the identification and mitigation of tenure risks at public’s capacity to identify and secure legitimate tenure rights. Based on the national tenure context, each partner will develop appropriate tools and materials to support the security of land use and be identified and assessed, and appropriate mitigating strategies will be proposed.

99. Sub-activity 1.1.3.2 Developing capacity on land tenure at decentralized level. The focus will be on establishing a framework for the identification and mitigation of tenure risks in order to improve tenure security, especially for women and youth. The Programme can support public awareness campaigns on tools to secure tenure.

Output 1.2 Improved service delivery to the dairy sector

100. Activity 1.2.1 Strengthen veterinary services for better animal health, higher milk yields and reduced losses. Climate change exacerbates the emergence of diseases and productivity of dairy animals is aimed at enhancing health and welfare. The Programme will support improved livestock production by reducing GHG emissions from dairy farms through: (i) improving herd management practices to increase productivity of dairy animals; (ii) increasing animal health services, including vaccination, deworming, and treatment of disease outbreaks; (iii) promoting the use of high-yielding dairy breeds; (iv) supporting the development of a national veterinary service delivery system that is more productive, with less milk to be discarded, leading to a reduction of 'unproductive' GHG emissions. The higher

## Sub-activity 1.2.1.1 Support the installation of both private and public veterinary services and extension services at all levels, using the "One Health" approach.

The activity will be implemented by EES through service providers (i.e., Kenya Veterinary Board and MAFIF), in collaboration with other partners such as the Rwanda Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (RCAV) in Rwanda; (ii) Directorate of Veterinary Services (DVS) and Tanzania Veterinary Laboratory Agency (TVLA) in Tanzania; (iii) MAIFF (Directorate of Animal Resources, DRA), in partnership with service providers (e.g., VSF), in Uganda.

### Sub-activity 1.2.1.1.1 Support the installation of both private and public veterinary services at all levels using the "One Health" approach.
The approach will have three levels: (i) support private veterinarians, including women, through training and extension work; (ii) provide additional training to local farmers, including women from the L-FTEC project, through training and extension work; (iii) provide veterinary education, and training programs to both private and public veterinarians and veterinary graduates. The programme will also support the installation of young veterinary doctors, veterinary technicians and Community Animal Health Workers (CAHWs) in all three countries. In Kenya, the Programme will build capacity of private veterinarians, veterinary technicians and community animal health workers through training services while creating jobs. In Tanzania, Livestock Extension Workers (LEWs) will be equipped and trained, and veterinary extension services will be strengthened at district level. In Uganda, the programme will build capacity of veterinarians, para-veterinary technicians, and community animal health workers (CAHWs) on One Health principles, disease diagnosis, emerging and re-emerging diseases because of CC.

### Sub-activity 1.2.1.1.2 Strengthening diagnostic capacity of veterinary laboratories and capacity to test for targeted diseases.
To tackle the problem of lack of laboratory services in many areas, the programme will strengthen monitoring, contingency planning, and treatment of targeted diseases including modern diagnostic equipment and testing kits. In Kenya, the Programme will support the establishment of a laboratory at Karatina (Garsia and Maralal), testing kits; laboratories in Rwanda, four satellite laboratories will ensure timely diagnosis of livestock diseases, as well as equipment for handling biological and hazardous waste and the establishment of a mobile laboratory to serve local communities. In Uganda, the Programme will establish veterinary laboratories (NLV) to handle advanced diagnostic capacity for viral diseases affecting livestock. Accessory services such as testing kits, reagents, consumables, and other materials will be provided to laboratory services on testing of feeds. In Tanzania, The Programme will: (i) strengthen the Tanzania Veterinary Laboratory Agency (TVLA) in Arusha; (ii) establish a mobile laboratory at Zanzibar; (iii) support the establishment of district mobile laboratories that will serve LEWs (local parishes); (iv) train veterinary technicians and CAHWs on laboratory testing services. The programme will also strengthen equipment, reagents, consumables for sample collection and mobile laboratory facility for testing of livestock diseases in all three countries. This activity will be implemented by EES through service providers (i.e., Kenya Veterinary Board and MAFIF), in collaboration with the College of Agriculture, Animal Science and Veterinary Medicine (ILR-CAVM) in Rwanda, NARO in Uganda, and TVLA in Tanzania.

### Sub-activity 1.2.1.1.3 Strengthening extension services for low-carbon and climate resilient dairy sector.
To reach out to dairy farmers, raise awareness and build their capacity in the field of climate resilience and adaptation, the Programme will strengthen extension services at all levels (national, district, and private) and enhance collective action among stakeholders through the establishment of a collective action-based extension system. This activity will be implemented by EES through service providers (i.e., Kenya Veterinary Board and MAFIF), in collaboration with other partners such as the Rwanda Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (RCAV) in Rwanda; (ii) Directorate of Veterinary Services (DVS) and Tanzania Veterinary Laboratory Agency (TVLA) in Tanzania; (iii) MAIFF (Directorate of Animal Resources, DRA), in partnership with service providers (e.g., VSF), in Uganda. International technical assistance will be provided by IRI and FAO to develop the tools and enhance national capacity.

104\. Sub-activity 1.2.2.1 Preparation of training modules for extension in climate mitigation and adaptation (CMA) relevant topics that are part of the proposed pathways under Outputs 1.2.3 and related to CC will be added to the modules of the L-FFS (or other extension approaches). The topics in the training modules include: improved livestock management practices, including feed resources harvesting and conservation; use of conserved sludge and UAMG; supplementation (use of non-conventional feed resources); integrated pest management; animal health management; reproductive management; health management including One-Health aspects and management of anti-biotics resistance; breeding and selection for climate resilience; improved livestock genetics; seed production (certification, traceability, quality control), improved diet formulation to reduce emission intensity; use of alternative energy sources in livestock production; integrated crop-livestock systems; animal welfare, including housing in zero-grazing practices. Also guidelines will be prepared on improved fodder conservation and management for livestock feed resources, including the use of leguminous trees for seed production, and agroforestry practices (e.g. leguminous fodder trees as standalone or tree) The L-FFS modules will include a module on climate change adaptation and mitigation technical assistance. Also general inclusive training on the implementation of guidelines will be carried out.

105\. Sub-activity 1.2.2.3 Training of master trainers (TsT) and facilitators of extension systems in climate change relevant topics that are part of the proposed pathways under Outputs 1.2.3 and related to CC will be added to the modules of the L-FFS (aiming at 40 percent women and 30 percent youth). To support the sustainability and impact of the L-FFS, existing L-FFS master trainers/facilitators will be trained on the specific topics listed above.

106\. Sub-activity 1.2.2.3 Promote innovative extension tools such as: (i) Participatory Climate Smart Agriculture for PICASA), which was piloted in Kenya, Rwanda and Tanzania; (ii) e-extension tools by using bulk SMS and mobile phones to disseminate climate information and best practices; (iii) the development with other stakeholders in development of content and capacity and to improve ability to access and share knowledge on climate change adaptation and mitigation. The use of innovative extension tools such as innovative extension tools could include: In Kenya, the use of the agro-weather platform namely Kenya Agricultural and Livestock Meteorological Information System (KALIMET) in collaboration with KALRO and the KMD, designed to provide weather information to support decision-making in the agricultural sector. The system is being used by farmers, extension workers, researchers and policy makers. In Tanzania, the Cow app and/or other innovative extension tool will be developed and promoted.

Output 1.3: Improved GHG MRV capacities & monitoring of climate vulnerabilities

107\. To reliably track progress on the ambitious climate goals of the national NGOs, state-of-the-art systems of MRV will need to be established in all countries. This will require capacity building at all levels for such systems, more particularly in the livestock sector. This Output will enable countries to report with accuracy on emissions from their livestock sectors and other agricultural sources. The MRV system will be based on the Guidelines, and address the institutional, planning and knowledge barriers identified in the ToC. It will also allow countries to better understand how their livestock sector contributes to their national GHG inventory and their climate commitments. Improved MRV systems will ensure that participating countries can conduct their own GHG inventories and report them to the UNFCCC. This will be achieved through capacity building for public and private finance climate, including through carbon credits. This Output will also address the issues related to the livestock sector in the context of the national MRV system and the national adaptation plan.

The national research centres and Government institutions will receive implementation support from LR (Maiztriga Centre, IITA, ILRI, etc.).

108\. Activity 1.3.1: Develop capacity in the country on MRV systems. The Programme will identify critical capacity needs at national and local levels to improve the operation of MRV systems. This will help developing countries to better understand how their livestock sector contributes to their national GHG inventory and their climate commitments. The progress made by each country under this Output, as well as the Programme, and beyond, can be monitored over time, and progress can be assessed against an established baseline.

109\. Sub-activity 1.3.1.1: Capacity assessment and development in capturing, recording, tracking, and reporting data on dairy GHG emissions. This sub-activity will carry out in depth capacity assessments of

B

existing tools and frameworks in the four countries, and the place of diary in them. Based on these assessments, capacity development will be provided to design data activity questionnaires for Tier 2 and/or Tier 3 livestock sub-sectors (e.g., dairy, beef, sheep, goats) based on their needs and relevant interventions, manure management and grassland management; and develop a data management system to ensure the quality of collected data. The project will also provide capacity development in data analysis.

Countries will receive capacity development on data collection protocols adjusted to their needs to relevant livestock sub-sectors (e.g., dairy, beef, sheep, goats) based on Tier 2 and/or Tier 3 livestock sub-sector (e.g., dairy actors). ILRIP will provide dedicated digital data collection tools (e.g. data quality control, and calculation of GHG emissions in dairy value chains).

### Sub-activity 1.3.1.2: Developing MRV systems and engine in policy dialogue to align with national MRV for the livestock sub-sector

After assessment of national MRV systems, this intervention will include capacity development on MRV systems and engines (e.g., Tier 2 and/or Tier 3 livestock sub-sectors) to ensure emissions intensity and institutional arrangements. To ensure sustainability and impact at the scale, the sub-activity will develop a policy dialogue with relevant stakeholders in the four countries to align national inventories outlined by IPCC on other cattle and fisheries. This will allow countries to track livestock productivity and GHG emissions from livestock production systems (e.g., dairy, beef, sheep, goats) and monitor changes in grassland rangelands**, following international inventory guidelines using IPCC Tier 2 methodology.

#### Activity 1.3.2: Improve GHG emission measurements in dairy value chains

On-farm data like animal live weight, feed intake, milk yield, etc., will be collected on a daily basis to reflect local conditions (e.g., animal metabolism, feed composition). This activity will complement the following sub-activities.

#### Activity 1.3.2.1: Collect activity data on farm to calculate Tier 2 dairy emissions under various herd, feed, animal health and manure management situations

This programme will finance the collection on-farm data (e.g., animal live weight, feed intake, milk yield) using a mobile data collection tool. This includes data on animal numbers, weights, productivity, feed rations and manure management practices. The data collected from these activities will be shared with national agricultural research institutions and academia of the four countries to ensure sustainability and replication through the transfer of knowledge and technology. The programme will develop a data collection system and tools for baseline and "with project" situations.

#### Sub-activity 1.3.2.2: Conduct experiments and run models in Tier 2 livestock emission factors (EFs) for baseline and "with project" situations under various herd, feed, animal health and manure management situations

Evidence shows that local feeding conditions and indigenous livestock breeds result in different emissions from the same type of livestock production system. In this context, the sub-activity will develop local content-specific emission factors (EF) and inventory equations through a combination of field experiments and model development. The programme will also conduct mitigation selection of feed interventions using state-of-the-art cattle rumination chambers** to derive methane emission factors for baseline and "with project" situations under various herd, feed, animal health, and manure management.

### Sub-activity 1.3.2.3: Develop a national MRV system in Tier 2 livestock sub-sectors

DaMIP, in partnership with ILRI, will support the member countries to monitor existing SOC stocks via targeted soil sampling and developing a national MRV system for Tier 2 livestock sub-sectors (e.g., dairy, beef, sheep, goats) at the four countries. The Program will aim impacts on grassland management on soil carbon storage and sequestration. The Programme will also support the development of a national MRV system in Tier 2 livestock sub-sectors via targeted soil sampling and developing a national MRV system for Tier 2 livestock sub-sectors (e.g., dairy, beef, sheep, goats) at the four countries.
