### Subject 1.3.2.3: Training on the Global Livestock Environmental Assessment Model - biannual (GLEAM) and IPCC test tool to calculate GHG emissions biannually, and to inform the national MRV system and inventories.

By using collected activity data and Tier 2 generated, Programme results will include a GLEAM model output for each livestock subsector in Kenya. The process will be biannually. Throughout the whole process, LIR will transfer capacity on Tier 2 eff., data collection and GHG emissions reporting.

#### Activity 1.3.1: Climate risk and vulnerability assessments

Together with improvements to extension services under output 1.2, the Programme will work to improve the accuracy and use of information (climate feed) for livestock production in Kenya. This will be achieved through decentralized meteorological offices to develop and disseminate climate information services, including (i) impacts of CC on livestock production; (ii) vulnerability assessments; and (iii) adaptation strategies.

This activity will be implemented by the relevant Executing Entities in each country (see sections B.3 and A.2), and through the Kenya Meteorological Department (KMD), the Ministry of Water and Environment (MWE, CCO in Uganda), the Soil and Land Management and Climate Change Department (SOILMC, Kenya), the National Agricultural Research Centre (NARC) in Tanzania, and the Rwanda Meteorology Agency.

#### Sub-activity 1.3.1.2: Climate risk and vulnerability assessments, including impacts on the dairy sector

The objective is to carry out climate risk assessment and vulnerability analysis at the local level, based on historical and projected climate data supported by impact-based models, analytical change scenarios, and participatory approaches.

Sector sub-baselines maps based on the current and future climate; (ii) assessing the direct impacts of climate change on livestock production in Kenya; (iii) identifying adaptation strategies for livestock production; and (iv) developing projections/forecasting of agricultural and pastoralism drought through e.g. machine learning and artificial intelligence.

#### Sub-activity 1.3.1.3: Review and enhancement of national feed balance mechanisms, and support to early warning systems

The national feed balance mechanism and results will be reviewed and improved in order to provide a more accurate picture of livestock production and its impact on the environment.

Improvements will focus on seasonality and quality of feed and fodder resources (metabolizable energy and crude protein content).

#### Sub-activity 1.3.1.4: Facilitate low-carbon certification and reduce barriers to access carbon finance

This activity will leverage MRV systems developed under activities 1.1, 1.3.2 and 2 track GHG emission reduction from improved livestock management practices in the dairy sector. The Programme will also support the development of carbon and high-quality milk production and engage on the voluntary carbon market.

The Programme will pilot this initiative and provide technical assistance to Kenya’s National Carbon Exchange (NCE) to develop a national registry for low-carbon certification and a database strengthening the position of e.g. the Kenya Climate Exchange in regional cooperation.

#### Sub-activity 1.3.1.4: Develop a carbon certification system for the dairy sector

In order to reduce barriers to carbon finance, the Programme will mobilize technical assistance (i) to review the alignment of national MRV systems with international standards and best practices; (ii) to develop monitoring and verification standards suitable for each national context (e.g. Verra, Gold Standard, etc.) in collaboration with relevant stakeholders including farmers’ organizations, private sector actors and research institutions; (iii) support the establishment of local monitoring and verification companies – to reduce barriers to carbon finance; and (iv) to establish a public dialogue strengthening the position of e.g. Kenya’s National Carbon Exchange in regional cooperation.

#### Output 1.4: Knowledge management and regional cooperation enhanced

The Programme will enhance knowledge management through SNSC and SSTC, with the objective to replicate the transformative practices for climate-resilient and low-carbon dairy adopted by the Programme. LIR Macgrima will be responsible for this output. The TNC will provide technical assistance in developing a Memorandum of direct agreement with the EBR in each country. This Output will address the institutional, planning and knowledge barriers identified in the TNC. Shared knowledge will enhance future low-carbon planning in the dairy sector, monitor

#### Activity 1.4.1: Support to Regional Centres of Excellence for technology validation and for maintaining a regional database repository on progress towards achieving the above-mentioned objectives.
To assist in building the capacity of national institutions, the Programme will provide scientific support combined with capacity development activities to help countries to develop their own methodologies for measuring GHG emissions from livestock production systems. National Centres of Excellence (or "knowledge hubs") will include national agricultural research and extension services, universities, private sector actors, and other relevant stakeholders at both local and national levels (e.g., Makerere and Busitema in Uganda; University of Nairobi, Eldoret or Maseno; University of Dar es Salaam in Tanzania). The Programme will also support the development of a regional database repository on progress towards achieving the above-mentioned objectives. This will be built on the legacy of the East Africa Agricultural Productivity Programme (EAAPP, World Bank-funded), which has built up a wealth of data and information.

#### Sub-activity 1.4.1.1: Support to regional Centres of Excellence in technology validation and dissemination.
The Programme will assist the above-mentioned institutions with (i) technical training and practical exercises on GHG emissions measurement, livestock production systems, adaptation indicators, and climate change mitigation; (ii) building their own capacity for research, MRV and technology validation linked to climate change mitigation; (iii) research capacity development in areas such as: developing methodologies for measuring GHG emissions from livestock production systems, nutrient recovery and building efficient biogas technologies; (iv) vocational training and development of mentoring programmes on the above-mentioned topics. The Programme will also support the development of quality products related to the four focus areas feeding, animal health, manure management and biogas, and grassland management.

#### Sub-activity 1.4.1.2: Develop and maintain a regional database repository on progress.
GHG emissions per sources and systems, and climate change adaptation. This sub-activity will consist in the development of a regional database repository for GHG emissions per source and livestock production system, and adaptation indicators. IIRI has a large repository of information on livestock sector CO2 emissions (COSAR) which will be leveraged, and will also support all actors in the Programme.

#### Activity 1.4.2: Establish national cooperation platforms to promote regional global network for knowledge sharing and early-stage transition.
The networking platforms will be organized by each country to include various public and private stakeholders active at the regional level and within each country. The platforms will be supported by IIRI, CGIAR, and other partners (e.g., East African Sub-regional Agricultural Development Authority (ESADA), local government partners, centres of excellence, international organisations, and non-governmental organizations). The Platform for Eastern Africa (PfEA) is a good example of such an initiative. PfEA is led by the Centre in Africa – ORIECA; Global Research Alliance Marine Management Network, Global Dairy Platform, NutriNetSanté, CGIAR, IIRI, and other partners. Through its Centre for Pastoral Areas and Livestock Development (CPLD), through its Centre for Pastoral Areas and Livestock Development (CPLD), the Programme will be invited to share its experience in developing methodologies for measuring GHG emissions from livestock production systems, manure management, recycling and biogas feedstock.

#### Sub-activity 1.4.2.1: Regional platform for knowledge sharing to enhance low-emission, climate-resilient livestock production.
The above-mentioned entities will be organized on a rotational basis in each of the participating countries to share experiences and scientific innovations related to GHG emissions from livestock production systems. The Programme will develop a management mechanism for the Programme. It will capture and disseminate good practices and lessons learned through workshops, conferences, seminars, tours (exchange visits including women and youth), meetings and policy briefs.

The activity will provide evidence – generated from the Programme’s system – on how efficient gains can be made in terms of GHG emissions reduction, feedstock recycling, manure management, biogas production, nutrient recovery and building efficient biogas technologies. Success stories (e.g., Kenya for Tier 2 MRV, Tanzania for scientific innovation on livestock nutrition / TMRP) will be documented. It will finance annual regional workshops, conferences, seminars, tours (exchange visits including women and youth), meetings and policy briefs.

#### Sub-activity 1.4.2: Participation in regional and global climate policy mechanisms.
In practice, the sub-activity will consist of a joint participation of the 4 countries in global and regional climate policy events (such as the UNFCCC COPs, the World Climate Change Conference 2025, the African Agriculture Conference, the African Climate Summit, the African Dairy Conference, and the UNCCFD COP7). #### Sub-activity 1.4.2: South-South and Triangular Cooperation (SSTC).
This sub-activity will support the countries to participate in SSTC initiatives on dairy value chains, including methane in dairy production and value chains, as well as financing solutions to support these reductions. This sub-activity will also support the countries to participate in the “Dairy Value Chain Innovation Routes” with e.g. Argentina, Brazil (Embrapa), Costa Rica and Uruguay on T2X analysis and climate impact assessment of the routes. The SSTC activities will be coordinated by the GCF Secretariat and the Steering Committees. ### COMPONENT 2: ENABLING LOW EMISSION AND CLIMATE RESILIENT DAIRY VALUE CHAIN DEVELOPMENT

125. The second part of the Programme shift component. Component 2 aims putting dairy smallholders and private sector value-chain operators on a transformational pathway towards a low-emission and climate-resilient dairy sector. Climate mitigation and adaptation strategies will be developed, implemented and scaled up in the dairy sector to contribute to GCF MRAT “Energy Generation and Access”, BMRR “Foresty and Land Use”, ARAI “Most vulnerable places”. The component will support the development of a low-emission and climate-resilient dairy value chain through climate-smart technologies and practices have been identified in section 8.1 and the TAC as key barriers to low emission and climate-resilient dairy sector, including: (i) lack of access to finance; (ii) lack of access to climate-smart technologies and practices for smallholder dairy farmers as well as medium and large scale commercial farmers, dairy cooperatives, MCIs, MCOs and SMEs. The component will support the development of a low-emission and climate-resilient dairy value chain through climate-smart technologies and practices have been identified in section 8.1 and the TAC as key barriers to low emission and climate-resilient dairy sector, including: (i) lack of access to finance; (ii) lack of access to climate-smart technologies and practices for smallholder dairy farmers as well as medium and large scale commercial farmers, dairy cooperatives, MCIs, MCOs and SMEs.

129. This Component will contribute to the reduction of total GHG emissions and lower GHG emission intensity per tonne of milk produced by 50% in 2030 compared with baseline scenario. The component has three-pronged approach: (i) at farm level, differences between dairy production at farm and post-farm levels; (ii) at the supply chain level, differences between dairy products at farm and post-farm levels; (iii) at the market level, differences in emissions from dairy products at farm and post-farm levels. The component will focus on carbon sequestration and removal through improved pastures and grassland management. This strategy will be based on a combination of climate-smart technologies and practices for smallholder dairy farmers as well as medium and large scale commercial farmers, dairy cooperatives, MCIs, MCOs and SMEs. The component will support the development of integrated solutions for the dairy smallholder farm including: animal breeding and health, feed and fodder, manure management, water use efficiency, soil conservation, agroforestry, including access to water to restore ecosystems’ functions and increase soil carbon sequestration.

130. Beneficiaries of the component are smallholder dairy farmers operating in zero-grazing intensive or semi-intensive systems (under Output 2.1) and medium/large scale commercial dairy farmers operating in all segments to scale, grow the formal dairy sector and drive sustainable intensification while addressing climate change adaptation needs (under Outputs 2.2-2.3). The component will also benefit smallholder dairy farmers organised in dairy cooperatives and SMEs. Under Output 2.3, the Programme will also benefit pastoralists, agroforestry practitioners and other stakeholders involved in the dairy sector to reach the dairy farmers include dairy cooperatives and MCIs, LFS/IFS and private business-based organisations.

131. Given the focus on climate vulnerable smallholder dairy farmers, Component 2 will utilise (non-reimbursable) grants to support the development of a low-emission and climate-resilient dairy sector through a gradual graduation of small-scale operators and strengthen their capacity to adopt productive enhancing and emission reduction technologies and practices. The component will also support the development of a low-emission and climate-resilient dairy value chain by supporting the development of integrated solutions for the dairy sector, drive increased awareness and understanding. Once this grant approach (under Outputs 2.1, 2.2 & 2.3) is successful, the component will be scaled up to support the development of a low-emission and climate-resilient dairy value chain by supporting the development of integrated solutions for the dairy sector, drive increased awareness and understanding. Once this grant approach (under Outputs 2.1, 2.2 & 2.3) is successful, the component will be scaled up to support the development of a low-emission and climate-resilient dairy value chain by supporting the development of integrated solutions for the dairy sector, drive increased awareness and understanding.

### Activities under this Component are innovative, with scale-up and replication potential Transforming the dairy sector in Rwanda will require a multi-faceted approach that includes: improving milk production, reduce absolute emissions, and lower emissions intensities The proposed pathways, as identified in the Federal Government of Rwanda’s (FGR) National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (NCCSAP), are mitigation and adaptation impacts These technologies will be promoted through the L-IFFS, as all required modules will have an explicit focus on the pathway In addition, digital app will be promoted to improve herd management

### Activities under the Component are sustainable The strengthening of dairy cooperatives will significantly impact the sustainability of the dairy sector in Rwanda by: (i) improving access to markets and services; (ii) enhanced management capacity, business development services, expanding/upgrading the network of MCCs, and increasing the number of milk collection points (MCPs); (iii) promoting good agricultural practices and technologies Cooperatives not only provide a stable outlet for milk, but also (i) work to comply with climate change mitigation and adaptation measures; (ii) improve access to markets and services by structuring the dairy value chain and engage in upstream and downstream supply-chain agreements; and (iii) facilitate access to finance

#### Output 2.1: Smallholders' and cooperative capacity development strengthened

The aim of Output 2.1 is to provide dairy farmers with consistent and stable access to market outlets for their milk, while also improving the overall management practices and sustainability of dairy cooperatives and their network of MCCs and milk collection points (MCPs) Dairy cooperatives are an essential entry point to unlock the potential of smallholder dairy production in Rwanda The output will be achieved by: (i) promoting good agricultural practices and technologies; (ii) improving access to markets and services; and (iii) building linkages with upstream and downstream dairy value chain stakeholders, as well as access to markets

##### 2.1.1 Strengthen smallholder farmers' capacity and cooperative management and service delivery

This activity aims to form and strengthen cooperative, organizational, and technical capacities of dairy groups and cooperatives in Rwanda The focus will be on the development of a gender-sensitive and social inclusion considerations in their management teams and membership This will aim to increase women and producers’ participation in decision-making processes at all levels of the cooperatives, including within their network of MCCs and MCPs, and their business linkages Activities 2.1.1 and 2.1.2 will be implemented by the executing agency (EA) in collaboration with the Rwanda Dairy Development Agency (RADA), the National Dairy Board (NDB) in Kenya, (ii) the Rwanda National Dairy Platform (RNPD) in Rwanda; (iii) the Dairy Development Authority (DDA), in Uganda; and (iv) the National Dairy Development Project (NDP) in India Implementation will rely on specialized service providers, such as farmers’ organizations (e.g All National Alliance of Co-operatives in Uganda, MACO, NDCN etc e.g Heifer International, SVA) and agricists for diagnostic activities

##### 2.1.2 Strengthen the governance and management structure of dairy cooperatives

This activity aims to improve the quality of milk produced by smallholder farmers through better milk collection practices and improved herd management The focus will be on improving the governance and management structures for all management, supervisory and general committees of dairy cooperatives; introduce innovative technologies and business models that will increase the capacity of cooperatives to provide services to their members; develop development strategies and plans for the formation and registration as a formal dairy cooperative; and (iv) the participation in national and regional networks and platforms on chain governance, promoting policy and business, and improving access to finance

##### 2.1.3 Strengthen smallholder farmers' capacity and cooperative management and service delivery

This activity aims to improve the quality of milk produced by smallholder farmers through better milk collection practices and improved herd management The focus will be on improving the governance and management structures for all management, supervisory and general committees of dairy cooperatives; introduce innovative technologies and business models that will increase the capacity of cooperatives to provide services to their members; develop development strategies and plans for the formation and registration as a formal dairy cooperative; and (iv) the participation in national and regional networks and platforms on chain governance, promoting policy and business, and improving access to finance

## Sub-activity 2.1.1.3 Expand the outreach and capacity of dairy cooperatives, MCCs and NCPs

The expansion of existing successful dairy operations and higher productivity in dairy crops, which leads to lower emission intensity of milk produced and sold, requires the nearly availability of milk collection capacity. Therefore, the Programme will support the development of new milk collection points (MCPs) for cooperatives that are not linked 1 out of reach from existing formal market processes and have sufficient potential to be connected to them. The Programme will also provide technical assistance to link these cooperatives to MCCs and NCPs, for example with heavy-duty bicycles initially owned by the rural organizations, to be transferred to the cooperatives.

## Sub-activity 2.1.1.4 Create awareness on gender transformative approaches among households of dairy farmers

Gender awareness has been integrated into the Programme interventions. The Enterprise Development Component (EDC) will support women’s groups in addressing inequalities especially at household level, thereby enhancing behavioural changes towards positive gender and social norms. This will be achieved through capacity building for women’s groups to promote women’s empowerment, and climate resilience. As men and women have diverse ways of coping and adapting to the impacts of climate change, it is important that both genders are involved in decision-making processes and adaptive capacities of households and communities to CC: food nutrition and food safety aspects. Support will be provided for the following activities: (i) awareness raising on gender equality among dairy contractors; (ii) training of GALS lead farmers and cooperative management as ToT; (iii) setting up of GALS in MCCs and NCPs, with a focus on women’s participation in decision-making processes and management of dairy cooperatives. The Programme will also organize trainings on Gender-based Violence (GBV) awareness raising and prevention, including the use of the GBV Risk Assessment Tool (RAT) and Prior and Informed Consent (PIC) approach to ensure stakeholders’ and communities’ buy-in for the various activities.

## Sub-activity 2.1.2 Build and enhance productive Alliances between dairy cooperatives and enterprises with farmer-aligned engagement models

The Programme will provide support to productive alliances between dairy cooperatives and enterprises with farmer-aligned engagement models, including: (i) facilitation of access to credit; (ii) provision of technical assistance in the development of business plans; (iii) provision of training on business management and financial planning. The aim is to ensure that dairy cooperatives and their MCCs are run as viable business enterprises. Therefore, the Programme will provide support for the following activities: (i) establishment of Business Development Services (BDS) will be mobilized. In addition, the members of dairy cooperatives and MCCs will receive: (ii) financial literacy training; (iii) training on business management and financial planning; (iv) training in inclusive business management and business plan development training using (using participatory tools such as Rural-Urban Linkages).

## Sub-activity 2.1.2.2 Build linkages along the dairy value chain

The BDS service providers engaged to strengthen cooperatives will further provide capacity building for negotiation of offtake agreements between cooperatives and enterprises, including: (i) facilitation of access to credit; (ii) provision of technical assistance in the development of business plans; (iii) provision of training on business management and financial planning. The BDS service providers engaged to strengthen cooperatives will further provide capacity building for negotiation of offtake agreements between cooperatives and enterprises, including: (i) facilitation of access to credit; (ii) provision of technical assistance in the development of business plans; (iii) provision of training on business management and financial planning. ### Activity 2.1.3 Promote renewable energy (RE) and waste management technologies

The document discusses the need to promote renewable energy sources such as solar or less water-intensive energy sources for milk cooling and processing, and to support the development of solid waste management systems in order to reduce methane emissions from livestock. The programme will provide more suitable solutions for larger MICS (>3m head). For processing, cold chain and quality preservation solutions are needed. This includes the use of solar-powered refrigeration units, solar water heaters, and solar dryers. Solar power can also be used for waste management systems to reduce methane emissions from livestock (e.g., manure composting) and to provide energy for cooling milk (losses and thus productive GHG emissions), yet not always used by small-scale processors. Solar is the key technology in this area, but other technologies such as biogas digesters can also be considered. The programme will support the management of waste and wastewater will be promoted. There is limited adoption of waste management solutions for livestock production systems in the four countries. This includes the use of solar-powered refrigeration units, cold chain and quality preservation solutions are needed.

### Activity 2.1.3.1 Assess the need and viability of renewable energy and waste management solutions

The document discusses the need to assess the need and viability of renewable energy and waste management solutions for livestock production systems in the four countries. This includes the upgrading or acquisition of dairy cooling equipment, solar water heaters, PV-powered technology for milk coolers for milk aggregation, milk transfer tanks, solar water heaters, water recycling systems, heat pumps, and biogas digesters. The programme will provide more suitable solutions for larger MICS (>3m head). The document also discusses the need to assess the viability of such investments not yet made and potential tech solutions.

### Activity 2.1.3.2 Support the acquisition of renewable energy and waste management solutions

The document discusses the need to support the acquisition of dairy cooling equipment, solar water heaters, PV-powered technology for milk coolers for milk aggregation, milk transfer tanks, solar water heaters, water recycling systems, heat pumps, and biogas digesters. The programme will provide more suitable solutions for larger MICS (>3m head). The document also discusses the need to assess the viability of such investments not yet made and potential tech solutions.

### Activity 2.1.3.3 Organize capacity building in energy efficiency and waste management

This training will be provided by the implementing partners. The programme will include strategies to avoid waste (including e-waste) through repair of the installed equipment and the establishment of a recycling system for electronic waste. This will also include strategies to reduce methane emissions from livestock production systems.

### Output 2.3.6 Improving animal health, feed and fodder, manure management improved

As detailed in section B.1 and Feasibility Study (Anna, Chapter 2), the pathways to reduce farm-level GHG emissions from dairy cattle are based on the following building blocks: (i) promotion of dairy breeds that are resistant to heat stress; (ii) provision of adequate feed and water for animals; (iii) adoption of feeding strategies to increase productivity of dairy cattle and reduce mortality, and herd management to optimize the composition of the diet and the amount fed to each animal in order to reduce waste and improve efficiency; (iv) reduction of enteric methane emissions; (v) manure management and use to recycle nutrients and reduce GHG emissions; and (vi) improving farm-level heat treatment and recover methods for household energy use while providing clean energy for cooking.

### Activity 2.2.1 Promote the adoption of climate-smart dairy breeds adapted to climate change: Breeding and selection of dairy cattle adapted to climate change

The document discusses the need to promote the adoption of climate-smart dairy breeds adapted to climate change in order to reduce GHG emissions from livestock production systems by crossing indigenous and exotic breeds through AI. Exotic breeds, such as Holstein-

**B**

Friesian or Jersey cows are more vulnerable to high temperatures and consequently to climate change, but possess a higher biological potential for milk production than indigenous breeds. However, they have lower resistance to diseases and certain diseases. Different generations of crossbreds (excess x native x breeds) would contribute to maintaining the existing genetic diversity in dairy cattle herds. Crossbreeding has been shown to increase milk yield by 10%–25% compared with indigenous breeds[46,47]. This activity will be implemented by the relevant Existing Entities in each country and supported by the Kenya Animal Genetic Resources Centre (KAGIRC) in Kenya; (R&D and private sector entities); (MIL-TARIS, National Agricultural Research Institute of Tanzania; and KENAFRIC in Uganda). Support will be given to scale up the national breeding strategies with specific focus on climate resilience of dairy cattle.

### 150. Sub-activity 2.2.1.1 Make good quality semen for climate-smart breeds available, adaptable to extreme climatic conditions and to diseases

The Programme will enhance availability and accessibility of perishable inputs such as liquid nitrogen, semen, embryos, and other inputs required by the breeding of climate-smart cattle breeds; (i) the import of seed cows[48] and/or embryos, if needed; (ii) the procurement of high genetic merit bulls for artificial insemination; (iii) the development of cryopreservation techniques to preserve production and conservation of climate-smart breeds’ germplasm using laboratory techniques like in-vitro fertilization (IVF), embryo transfer (ET), semen collection and preservation, AI; and (iv) capacity development to produce semen and increase production of dairy heifers.

### 151. Sub-activity 2.2.1.2 Invest in breeding and artificial insemination (AI) centres

To solve bottlenecks in the supply chain for climate-smart cattle breeds, the Programme will invest in capacity building at existing AI centres and distribution of liquid nitrogen, semen, and embryos. This includes the availability of liquid nitrogen, storage and quality control of semen and embryos, as well as training of personnel to ensure that semen is used correctly.

The following investment will be supported: In Kenya, the Programme will involve the Kenya Livestock Breeders Association (KLA) in the development of a semen bank for climate-smart cattle breeds. This includes capacity building on recording and reporting feedback. In Rwanda, support will be given for the establishment of semen and heat monitoring centres. The Centre will also provide training to personnel involved in semen collection and micro-manipulators to enhance semen production operations. Moreover, RWB will be strengthened in record keeping and semen quality control.

In Tanzania, support would be given to establish an AI centre in Southern Highland. Also, the feasibility of an AI unit at Zanzibar Island will be assessed. In Uganda, support will be provided for the establishment of a Dairy Bank to improve the production and access to high-quality semen in small and medium size farms.

### 152. Sub-activity 2.2.1.3 Scale up the insemination of dairy cattle to make the herd more productive and climate resilient

The Programme will support the scaling-up of AI services by training personnel such as veterinarians, lead farmers of L-FPSs and District livestock officers (at least 30 percent youth and 40 percent women). This includes capacity building on recording and reporting feedback. The Programme will also enhance the interaction between dairy cattle husbandry and climate change in the L-FPSs centres. In Kenya, the Programme will support the scaling-up of AI services by training personnel such as veterinarians, lead farmers of L-FPSs and District livestock officers (at least 30 percent youth and 40 percent women). This includes capacity building on recording and reporting feedback.

In Tanzania, the Programme will support the scaling up of AI services by training personnel such as veterinarians, lead farmers of L-FPSs and District livestock officers (at least 30 percent youth and 40 percent women). This includes capacity building on recording and reporting feedback. In Uganda, the Programme will support the scaling-up of AI services by training personnel such as veterinarians, lead farmers of L-FPSs and District livestock officers (at least 30 percent youth and 40 percent women). This includes capacity building on recording and reporting feedback.

## Sub-activity 2.2.1 Make certifiable forage seed available to private and community multipliers

This sub-activity will support the development of a national forage seed certification system, including reducing fodder by the national mandated public services; (ii) facilitate a seed certification scheme and sensitize the stakeholders on the importance of certified seeds.

## Sub-activity 2.2.1.2 Support multiplication of climate-resilient seed for fodder crops and pastures

This sub-activity will include contracting community and private seed multipliers including the youth and women groups to produce and supply high-quality fodder seeds. The produced seeds will be used to promote fodder and pasture seed agri-business through facilitating multiplication of seeds and making them available in markets.

## Sub-activity 2.2.1.3 Build capacity of dairy farmers on forage production and conservation on intensive and semi-intensive dairy farms

The LFSS will be the knowledge-sharing vehicle to promote improved forage management practices (e.g., grass and legume intercropping, alley cropping, weed management, use of biocides); training and demonstration on the use of appropriate feed formulation techniques; training and demonstration on mechanization of forage production with fodder choppers/pullers and conservation equipment; (b) training and demonstration on the use of appropriate feed formulation techniques; (c) training and demonstration on protein content, digestibility variations. Youth groups will be supported to venture into appropriate commercial forage production.

## Sub-activity 2.2.1.4 Access to water for animals and forage production in intensive and semi-intensive dairy farms

The Programme will provide small-scale equipment for rainwater harvesting and recycling systems for livestock watering and forage irrigation.

## Sub-activity 2.2.4 Promote climate-smart feeding practices to reduce enteric methane emissions

Appropriate diet formulation is key to improve dairy cattle productivity, improve animal welfare, while reducing methane C02e emissions. The project will support the development of a national feed database and guidelines to reduce rumen fermentation and methane yield (kg CH4/kg DM) at matter intake. Better nutrient utilization (e.g., avoiding overfeeding), improved forage quality, and reduced waste are key strategies to achieve this objective. The project will develop the development of feed packages and plans; build national capacity on feed formulation, and promote these technologies through training and demonstration. The project will also support the development of a national feed database in Sardinia (Italy); at RMB and the University of Rwanda, College of Agriculture, Animal Science and Veterinary Medicine (Rwanda); at the National Agricultural Research Organization (NARO) in Uganda; and at the MAFF (Japan), in collaboration with NGOs (e.g. SNV, Heifer International) and private service providers in Uganda.

For each country, the national research and academic institutions will develop feed

### Sub-activity 2.2.5 Increase sustainable nutrient management

Lemongrass, banana leaves (Musa spp.), cowpea (Vigna unguiculata) and other cover crops are well known for reducing ammonia (NH3) and nitrate (NO3). At the same time, manure contains essential nutrients and is often the primary source of nitrogen in dairy systems. However, the use of manure as a fertilizer has several limitations: it is not always nutrient available for feed and forages, which increases their growth, biomass yield and nutritional quality; and alternative fertilizers are more expensive than manure. In addition to its role as a fertilizer, manure can be used in soil management practices such as mulching, composting, or incorporation into the soil. Manure application via livestock manure is the best suitable option to overcome these limitations, improve soil health, and improve on-farm nutrient cycling. The Programme will promote the use of manure as a fertilizer and for soil management by increasing its nutrient availability for feed and forages, which increases their growth, biomass yield and nutritional quality; and alternative fertilizers are more expensive than manure. In addition to its role as a fertilizer, manure can be used in soil management practices such as mulching, composting, or incorporation into the soil. Manure application via livestock manure is the best suitable option to overcome these limitations, improve soil health, and improve on-farm nutrient cycling.

### Sub-activity 2.2.5.1 Manure management in intensive and semi-intensive dairy production systems

The Programme will create awareness and build capacity on low-cost manure management for soil health improvement and nutrient recycling. The Programme will promote the use of manure as a fertilizer by increasing its nutrient availability for feed and forages, which increases their growth, biomass yield and nutritional quality; and alternative fertilizers are more expensive than manure. In addition to its role as a fertilizer, manure can be used in soil management practices such as mulching, composting, or incorporation into the soil. Manure application via livestock manure is the best suitable option to overcome these limitations, improve soil health, and improve on-farm nutrient cycling.

### Sub-activity 2.2.5.2 Promote animal housing systems

The Programme will promote adapted animal housing with the possibility of separating solid and liquid manure. Good example of animal housing promoted by the LFFS in Uganda is the use of a pit latrine for cattle (see Figure 10).

## Output 2.3: Pasture and grasslands management improved

174. Many dairy products rely on pasture and grasslands as main feed resource, which are constrained by limited water availability, poor soil fertility, and overgrazing. Improved pasture and restored pastures and rangelands increase climate resilience through healthier ecosystems, improved water access, biodiversity conservation, and emission reductions. In addition to the direct benefits of increased livestock productivity in agro-pastoral systems, the programme considers soil carbon sequestration as an important pathway of GHG emissions removal to the atmosphere. Grasslands are not only a source of food for animals but also provide other ecosystem services such as water purification, flood control and erosion reduction, but they are also more productive (increased biomass yield) and improve the animal diet and reduce enteric CH4.

### Activity 2.3.1 Strengthen rangeland governance structures for communal grazing areas

This activity will support the Ministry of Natural Resources and Tourism (MoNRT) in Tanzania, the MoEP in Uganda, and the Ministry of Agriculture, Animal Industry and Fisheries (MAAIF) in Kenya to strengthen rangeland governance through: (i) the SLDL; KALRO, County Governments and the MoNRT in Kenya; (ii) MUF and LGAs in Tanzania; (iii) SLDL and LGA in Uganda. In each country, facilitators specialized in participatory rangeland management will be mobilized.

### Sub-activity 2.3.1.1 Participatory rangeland management planning

In selected districts/counties, the Project will support a participatory process to develop district-level rangeland plans (including gender aspects), formed (e.g.) village land committees, environmental commissions, water user committees, local government district) and internal entities (grazing areas, traditional communities, clan

## Activity 2.3.2 Restoration of degraded rangelands, including access to water.

This activity will finance interventions to restore rangelands. The sub-activity will be implemented by (i) the SDSLs, KALRO, and NARO in Kenya; (ii) the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF) and the National Environment Management Authority (NEMA) with DGLA in Uganda. In each country, service providers specialized in rangelands restoration will be mobilized.

### Sub-activity 2.3.2.1 Training on rangeland and pasture management

The Programme will facilitate FFE/PFS aimed at guiding and engaging farmers towards improving the management of rangelands and pastures. This will include training on livestock grazing, water harvesting and storage, soil conservation, reseeding i.e., sale of seeds during the dry season and restocking during seasons of high food availability to reduce overgrazing and improve pasture quality. Training will be provided by service providers specialized in these areas including those that are medicinal or for fruits) but for better carbon sequestration and reduction of livestock exposure to diseases, it is recommended that farmers use locally available seeds and not imported ones. The training will also include the production of organic manure and compost as well as the production of biochar and biogas (bio-charger), elaborated from invasive bush species.

### Sub-activity 2.3.2.2 Rangelands and pasture restoration

This sub-activity focuses on degraded rangeland areas, with the aim to restore soil fertility and increase biomass in natural pastures: (i) integration of multipurpose leguminous trees and shrubs (improved agroforestry systems) and control of invasive tree species; and (ii) implementation of soil and water conservation practices to increase water availability and biomass productivity.

### Sub-activity 2.3.2.3 Water availability

Depending on the locality, in enhancing access to water for dairy cattle rearing, farmers will be provided with small-scale dams, tanks, wells, and other water harvesting structures. In small-scale valley dams, chancos dams and rock-catchment schemes. Feasibility studies will be carried out before the construction of these structures. The implementation of these structures will be done in collaboration with community-based water supply organizations (COWSOs) to better manage and operate all water harvesting and storage systems. The COWSOs will also be supported by the Ministry of Water, Irrigation and Livestock authorities, making gender considerations such as the provision of animal washing and bathing for women.

## COMPONENT 3: GREEN DAIRY FINANCING FACILITY (GDF®)

### GDF Financial Resources

The GUMA will provide catalytic financing of at least US$25 million to enable GDF to achieve access to finance for the target group and support the implementation of climate-smart technologies and practices, thereby facilitating the achievement of the programme objectives. The GDF will be implemented in collaboration with the financial and technical barriers identified in the TuC as key obstacles in greening the sector's operations. Under Component 3, the GDF will provide financing to dairy farmers, livestock keepers, agro-input and technology providers, and farmers' cooperatives, all of which are eligible to access DAIMA-supported financing. The GUMA is expected that these entities be suitable for GDF® financing with be proactively encouraged to apply for support under Component 3.

### Loan Terms

The loan terms will be competitive at a maximum interest rate of 7.75% from IFAD and the partner banks. Loans will be selected between the two: Wholesale Banks or co-lending through the Retail Banks to the programme target group. The GDF will provide loans in the range US$10,000 - US$200,000 per farmer. The total amount of financing is US $75 million (25 percent of retail loans) from the Retail Bank: local private sector resources. In addition,

### B

**substantial adaptation is included under Component 3 to support the GDFF lending and dairy sector development operations through a USA 10th millennium assistance facility.**

#### 185. The GDFF credit capital from Duma resources will be lent by IFAD to the four Governments in USD and by the Wholesale Banks to the targeted borrowers in local currencies. The interest rate on the loans will be determined by the terms of reference for the loan agreements between IFAD, the Wholesale Banks, and the target borrowers. The credit provided by the international financials and the USA 10th millennium assistance facility will be disbursed through the Wholesale Banks to the executing partners to the Governments according to the repayment schedule of the IFAD Duma loans.

#### 184. To achieve its development objectives, Component 3 activities are structured into two outputs: (i) Output 3.1: Effective Greening Finance for Dairy Value Chains; and (ii) Output 3.2: Capacity Building for Green Dairy Investments.

##### Output 3.1: Effective Greening Finance for Dairy Value Chains

###### 3.1.1 GDFF Operationalization
In each of the four participating countries, a GDFF credit line will be established to channel green funding to eligible dairy sector borrowers. This line of credit will be managed by a Public Development Bank (PDB) in each country. The Wholesale Banks will act as intermediaries between IFAD and the PDBs. Wholesale Banks will at national level manage the financial Duma services to the programme target group based on eligibility criteria, loan terms and conditions, and repayment schedule. In order to ensure that the Wholesale Banks are able to provide the required services in line with the agreements between IFAD, the Governments, and the Wholesale Banks. Based on IFAD’s rural finance experience in Ethiopia, Kenya, Tanzania, and Uganda, and taking into account the financial capacity of the Wholesale Banks, the following PDBs were selected as candidates for the Wholesale Banks for GDFF: Agricultural Finance Corporation (AFC) for Ethiopia; National Bank of Kenya (NBK) for Kenya; Rural Development Bank (RDB) for Rwanda; and Tanzania Agricultural Development Bank (TADB) for Tanzania. The criteria for Wholesale Bank selection included: (i) strong interest in the dairy sector, (ii) a track record of providing financial services to smallholder farmers, (iii) strong interest in agriculture and rural finance operations, (iv) corporate objective to develop and scale-up its lending activities in the dairy sector, (v) ability to provide a range of financial products and services including credit products, and (vi) strong expressed interest to manage the Duma wholesale financing operations in the dairy sector.

###### 3.1.2 Wholesale Banks
The Wholesale Banks will provide bulk loans for on-lending to the eligible Retail Banks, which can be combined with other sources of funding such as IFAD’s GDFF credit line and/or other financial resources. Financial resources provided as bulk loans by the Wholesale Banks to Retail Banks will be exclusively on-lend to eligible dairy sector borrowers at interest rates determined in accordance with the terms and conditions of the wholesale loan agreements between the Wholesale Banks and Retail Banks, the actual lending methodology agreed upon by the Wholesale Banks and Retail Banks, the IFAD’s GDFF credit line operational structure and network, rural and agriculture finance strategy, and their digital tools development approach.

###### 3.1.3 Retail Banks
Retail Banks for each country will be selected after programme effectiveness as a joint activity between the wholesale banks and the governments. The selection criteria include: (i) strong interest in dairy sector financing; (ii) supervisory with solid institutional and financial conditions as per central bank standards, and prudentially supervised, if applicable; (iii) ability to provide a range of financial products and services including credit products; (iv) scale-up the banks’ climate finance operations; (v) an appropriate network to provide services in the Duma programme target group based on eligibility criteria, loan terms and conditions, and repayment schedule; (vi) strong interest in agriculture and rural finance operations and can finance 25 percent of each investment. During the period leading to GDFM effective implementation, IFAD will support the Wholesale Banks and Retail Banks with technical assistance for programme activities. To support and co-ordinate the start-up phase, experienced Climate Finance Specialties will be seconded from IFAD to provide technical assistance in the areas of credit risk management, financial inclusion, and SME finance. The Wholesale Banks and Retail Banks will finalize the preparations for the selection and training of appropriate Retail Bank staff as well as the development of a retail bank business plan. Each Retail Bank selection will also go through the NFO no objection procedure.

###### 3.1.4 Targeted Borrowers and Key Lending Products
As indicated, the GDFF lending operations under Component 3 target medium-sized farmer-led dairy value chain actors, including aggregators and processors, agro-input suppliers, and input service providers. The Wholesale Banks will provide bulk loans to Retail Banks that are supported by donors including IFAD who have special lending windows for individual dairy farmers operating in these dairy value chains. The GDFF credit line will be provided on a revolving basis with the possibility of additional funding covering the same countries as Duma and targets smallholder farmers with CC adaptation finance services including

## Dairy Sector: The GIFF Financial Support to dairy value chain companies will be planned and operated in close co-operation with these other green financing interventions in the four target countries.

### 189. Based on the market and demand surveys conducted during the DaMa design (see attached to this Facility Project proposal), the structure of the Dairy Loan Products offered by GIFF in GDFs depends on the scale and maturity of the dairy value chain operations of the borrower (more information on the type of loans can be found for each target country).

#### 190. Green Dairy Investment Partnership Loan Product: These are non-term partnerships between mature, well-organised dairy businesses and the GIFF Rural Banks. The GIFF will offer tailored lending packages to these borrowers, which could include - but is not limited to - co-financing of capital financed financing opportunities could include - but are not limited to - to practice expansion and modernising the milk production process (e.g., through the use of solar-powered milking equipment, upgrading productive cows or energy-efficient fodder, solarizing cooling or processing equipment, and developing the milk supply chain), improving animal health and disease control, and providing access to credit for injections. While the exact loan conditions will depend on the identified needs and objectives of the business, the typical loan size is US$ 10-25 million, with a term of between 3 and 9 years, with the average loan size at US$400k. Eligible businesses will need to exhibit clear ambitions to invest, develop or distribute climate-smart technologies, productivity-enhancing technologies, and/or improve their environmental performance. They should also be financially sound, have a track record and solid plans for intensifying their smallholder partnerships. Based on the size of the business, the typical loan size is US$ 10-25 million, with an average loan size above US$800k, and investment decisions will be based on a thorough credit assessment by the Rural Banks.

#### 191. Green Dairy Loan Product: These are shorter-term, smaller-sized working capital or capital expenditure loans that seek to meet a specific financing need of businesses of small-scale SMEs and well-organised dairy farmers. The typical loan size is US$50k with an average loan size at US$250k, and the maximum loan size at US$ 250,000, estimated average ticket size of US$ 50,000, and loan tenor of between 18 months to three years. These loans are intended for businesses that have a track record to exhibit clear ambitions to invest, develop or distribute climate-smart technologies, productivity-enhancing technologies, and/or improve their environmental performance. They should also be financially sound, and the majority of the dairy sector SMEs will not be operating at a significant scale. These Dairy Loan Products will seek to meet specific financing needs of businesses that are not able to access other forms of finance to receive pre-investment TA or transition investments into investment requests, and they can be committed to invest in climate-smart technologies and/or improve their environmental performance.

### 192. End-of-GIFF Finance and Green Dairy Taxonomy: To achieve the development objectives and impact of DaMa and its Component 3, it is critically important that there is a clear joint understanding and agreement on what forms of finance are to be included in the GIFF funding. The taxonomy development process carried out in recent years on rural green finance taxonomy in East Africa, particularly during the IFAD/GCF design process for the East African Green Finance Facility (EAGFF), has been very useful in developing a common understanding and agreement on what forms of finance are to be included in the related investment options for the private sector financial institution implementation. Furthermore, there are various other ongoing initiatives that are also contributing to this taxonomy development.

### 193. The Taxonomy of investments for the GIFF lending will build on the findings of the Market Study carried out as part of a DaMa design (full Study attached to the Funding Proposal), and will include both agri-input supply investment options, such as inputs for climate-smart agriculture, livestock feed, fertiliser, seeds, etc., agri-finance providers, fodder producers, and biodigesters providers) and investments in technologies that reduce GHG emissions from dairy production (e.g. solar-powered milking equipment, energy-efficient cooling and processing equipment, milk transfer tanks, renewable energy solutions and water recycling technologies), as well as bio-waste management.

### 194. The guidelines on appropriate dairy sector green financing options for private sector finance in the four participating countries are based on the findings of the Market Study carried out as part of a DaMa design (full Study attached to this Facility Project proposal). These guidelines will form a core part of the training and capacity building activities with the participating banks and the borrowing SMEs that have more details can be found at page 192. As a key development objective both for the development of the dairy sector green financing taxonomy and continue, based on the documented experiences from

## Lending Terms to End-Users

The DaMA/GCF financing package will proactively encourage the Retail Banks to expand their operations in green dairy financing with the programme target group using appropriate and flexible lending terms. The GCF/IFAD Wholesale Bank will provide a full recourse loan to the Retail Banks, which will be used by the GDFT implementing partners to make it possible for the Retail Banks to offer appropriate loan and grace periods to end-users. This is necessary because the loans are intended to finance dairy farmers’ investments in CC resilience and mitigation but require longer repayment periods to attract investors from the business community.

## The GDFT Financing Chain

The GDFT financing chain is illustrated in the diagram (Figure 17b). Concerning the interest rates throughout this financing chain, it should be noted that the concessional loan offered by IFAD to the Wholesale Banks (8.5 percent annual interest rate including fee) apply for Rwanda, Tanzania and Uganda, and the less concessional (12.5 percent annual interest rate excluding fee) applies for Kenya. The interest rates charged by the Wholesale Banks to Governments to the GDFT Wholesale Banks, before final negotiations, is projected at 2.5 percent on local currency per annum. The interest rates offered by the Wholesale Banks to the Retail Banks would be in the range of 5-8 percent on local currency per annum.

The LCFY loans are expected to be provided with a grace period of up to three years and repayments below the lowest market-based interest rates charged by commercial banks for this type of loans in the target countries.

## Output 3.2: Capacity Building for Green Dairy Investments

197. GDFF will proactively use the TA facility to (i) build the capacity of the Wholesale Banks and Retail Banks to understand and implement green dairy sector projects, (ii) maximize both the impact and returns of the financed investments by ensuring that the projects are well designed, implemented in a manner that maximizes resource efficiencies, and achieve sustainable growth; and (iii) support the establishment of an effective M&E system and impact assessment framework. The TA facility will be managed by the Green Dairy Financial Lending Technical Assistance Facility. The TA facility would address non-financial barriers currently preventing greater climate-smart financing in the GDFF operations.

198. The technical support activities of Sub-Component 3.2 with both the participating banks and the SMEs will be managed and implemented by Technical Assistance Service Providers, to be identified and selected through regional competition. The TA facility will be managed by the Green Dairy Financial Lending Technical Assistance Facility (GDFF-TAF) through the standard no objection process. The TA is guided by a Green Dairy Financial Lending Toolkit.

199. Operating with a total TA budget of US$ 10 million (funded by GCF grant), output 3.2 consists three activities: (i) capacity building for Wholesale Banks and Retail Banks, (ii) capacity building for the Green Dairy Investment Support Services to SMEs; and (iii) M&E system and household surveys and evaluations.

## Activity 3.2.1 Awareness Raising and Capacity Development of Participating Banks in Green Dairy Financial Lending

200. Activity 3.2.1 aims at ensuring that the credit provided with GDFF funding will deliver real, effective, and transformative adaptation and emission reduction impacts on the dairy sector. The details of the capacity building activities for the beneficiaries can be found in the feasibility study (Annex A, section 5). The participating Wholesale and Retail Banks will receive a tailor-made technical assistance package that includes: (i) training; (ii) a demand-driven package of technical support and innovation services, with the aim to develop and offer appropriate green dairy financing products; and (iii) capacity building for M&E. The successful completion and submission of the Technical Assistance to the Retail Banks is a condition to access to GDFF financing for on-lending.

201. Before the GDFF lending operations commence, the Wholesale Banks will receive international and local TA support in order to develop their internal capacities to effectively manage green dairy financial products. Wholesale Banks also will receive systematic support to establish effective GDFF management systems, to coordinate the implementation of the GDFF project, and to ensure that the projects are well designed and implemented over term; the aim is that DaMA Wholesale Banks will strengthen their internal capacities to independently mobilize and

## B Implementation arrangements

### Accredited Entity

201. The IFAD will act as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the Programme. In this capacity, IFAD will be responsible for the overall management of the Programme, including: (i) aspects of Programme appraisal; (ii) administrative, fiduciary and financial oversight; (iii) monitoring and evaluation; (iv) reporting on progress against the approved Programme; (v) expected Outputs and Outcomes; and (vi) Programme closure and final evaluation. IFAD will carry out these responsibilities in close collaboration with the GCF and its Secretariat.

### Regional Programme Governance

207. The Regional Steering Committee (RSC) will be constituted by Permanent Secretaries from the Executing Entities. The RSC will consist of 5 members, who will appoint 4 members to the RSC, including 3 Government representatives and 1 or 2 representatives of beneficiaries and private sector or civil society. The RSC will meet once a year in person at the IFAD headquarters in Rome (Italy), and twice per year for the physical GCF meetings in Nairobi, Kenya and the IFAD Country Offices in Kenya, Rwanda, the United Republic of Tanzania, and Uganda.

208. To fulfill the AE functions, IFAD will set up a dedicated Programme Task Force (PTF) consisting of IFAD experts from its headquarters and country offices.

realized regional impacts of the Programme and that regional added value is achieved: (i) timeliness and quality of reporting to GCF, both consolidated annual work plans and budgets (AWPs), progress reports and M&E reports; (ii) output level reports are produced for reporting to IFAD and GCF; (iii) programme level reports are produced for reporting to GCF, both consolidated annual work plans and budgets (AWPs), progress reports and M&E reports; (iv) regional initiatives around MRV reporting; and (v) regional M&E knowledge management and reporting activities, to enhance the capacity of the four countries to report on their progress. The RSC will also monitor the quality of programme level reports being produced for reporting to IFAD and GCF. The RSC will specifically monitor progress against the following outputs: Output 1.3 (MRV GHG capacity building) and Output 1.4 (regional exchange platform, regional knowledge management and sharing). The RSC will also provide technical support on MRV reporting, M&E knowledge products and support the establishment of a knowledge platform specific to each country. This platform will be used for the exchange of information, best practices, lessons learned, development and sharing of studies, technical work, trainings, and financial initiatives between the four countries.

208. A senior regional coordinator and policy dialogue expert would be recruited in each country, with similar TDRs to those described above. The role of these coordinators will be to support the implementation of the Programme at national level, ensure that all elements within specified constraints, and escalate issues to RSC as needed (in case a national PSC decision has implications for the strategy of other countries). They would also provide technical assistance on MRV reporting, M&E knowledge products and support the establishment of a knowledge platform specific to each country. This role will be beneficial.

### National Programme Steering Committee

209. In each country, a National Programme Steering Committee (PSC) will be established. The PSC will:

(i) provide strategic direction for the implementation of the Programme at national level; ensure that all elements within specified constraints;

(ii) monitor progress and effectiveness of mitigation measures, and provide guidance on emerging risks of the Programme in the context of climate change;

(iii) review and approve the annual work plan and budget (AWP);

(iv) assess the progress and effectiveness of the Programme, and provide guidance and recommendations to ensure that the approach is appropriate for the country’s needs. The PSC will also:

(v) provide strategic guidance for its implementation; (vi) appraise the annual report on the implementation of the Programme;

(vii) review and approve the annual work plan and budget (AWP);

(viii) assess progress towards achieving the objectives of the Programme, including any achievements or shortcomings;

(ix) provide ad hoc advice and guidance in exceptional situations;

(x) identify lessons learned and good practices.

In each country, the PSC will have to meet formally at least once a year. The PSC may also hold meetings more frequently as required by its members. Extraordinary meetings may be requested by any of its members. The composition of each PSC is presented below:

The PSC will be chaired by the Permanent Secretary of the MUF, and additional members (e.g. Ministry of Environment, NEMA, Meteorological Authority, private sector, civil society) may be added.

## National Programme Coordination and Management

217. In the four countries, the Programme will be managed by national Programme Coordination Units (PCUs), either within the MoEF or in a separate unit, depending on country context. The PCU will comprise competitively recruited Government staff with appropriate project management skills, subject to FAO No Objection at the key stages of recruitment and appointment. The PCU will be responsible for overall coordination and day-to-day management of the Programme, as well as facilitating a conducive environment, specifically: (i) ensuring that all partners are aligned on the objectives and activities implementation among EEs and implementing agencies and partners; (ii) the development of operational plans and budgets for the Programme; (iii) the monitoring and evaluation of progress towards achievement of the overall management of the Programme; (iv) the mobilization, contracting and coordination of implementation partners (v) the monitoring and reporting on the performance of the Programme in line with the approved work plan and budget; (vi) the encouragement of best practices and good practices and providing guidance for effective Programme implementation; (vii) the management of the communication strategy to ensure that all stakeholders are informed about the Programme’s goals and deliverables, as well as its progress; (viii) the coordination with financing agencies and donor counterparts on financing arrangements; (ix) communication and visibility of the Programme.

218. In Kenya (Figure 18), at national level, a PCU will be established within SEDD in the MoEF. To steer implementation of the Programme, a Country Programme Coordination Committee (CPC) and a Country Programme Implementation Team (CPIT) will be established with the respective representatives from the relevant departments. The CPC will be chaired by the Director General of the national PCU. The CPC and CPIT will also ensure coordination with other Projects, for example synergies with the “Transition to Renewable Energy in Sub-Saharan Africa” Project and the “Agriculture and Rural Development Region Economic Bloc” Project proposed by FAO to the GCF. At the sub-county level, a Subcounty Programme Implementation Team (SPIT) will be established within the relevant departments, as outlined for the CPIT.

219. In Rwanda (Figure 19), the Programme will be an integral part of the RDRPE-2. It will be implemented through the SPPA and the PCU will be established in the MoEF. The PCU will be responsible for overall coordination of the Programme and will create partnership with the Local Government Authorities in each targeted District, building on the existing structures and mechanisms for engaging local communities.

## Programme Co-financing

The co-financing (see Table 3.1) is applicable for reporting on its financing activities and the amount of their disbursement to the AE in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the GCF policies as well as in AMA, the Fund’s Financial Rules and Regulations. The co-financing will be provided by IFAD and Government Funds, depending between the co-finance and IFAD in its capacity of AE. The amounts of co-financing are detailed in Section C.1.

232. In Kenya, the Programme will be co-financed by INRMP to the amount of USD 74.5 million from IFAD; FAD funds will be used to finance Component 1 and as well for Programme management and M&E. This includes USD 9.0 million for the development of veterinary extension services, dairy consumption and food safety aspects under Component 1, activities related to the development of veterinary and extension services, dairy consumption and food safety aspects under Component 2, and USD 65.5 million for the development of cooperative development and business linkages. Under Component 3, credit lines will be provided to Finance Output 3.1.

233. In Rwanda, the Programme will be co-financed by RDP2 for USD 84.61 million from IFAD; in addition, Government of Rwanda will provide USD 14.75 million channeled to Outputs 2.1 and 2.2. FAD funds will be used to contribute towards financing Component 1 and as well for Programme management and M&E. This includes USD 9.0 million for the development of veterinary extension services, dairy consumption and food safety aspects under Output 2.1, the activities related to the development of veterinary and extension services, dairy consumption and food safety aspects under Output 2.2, and USD 65.5 million for the development of cooperative development and business linkages. Under Component 3, credit lines will be provided to Finance Output 3.1.

234. In Tanzania, the Programme will be co-financed by C SD-TDP in an amount of USD 50.81 million from IFAD; these FAD funds will use to finance Components 1 and 2 as well as for Programme management and M&E. This includes USD 9.0 million for the development of veterinary extension services, dairy consumption and food safety aspects under Output 2.1 the activities related to the development of veterinary and extension services, dairy consumption and food safety aspects under Output 2.2, and USD 38.5 million for the development of cooperative development and business linkages. Under Component 3, credit lines will be provided to Finance Output 3.1.

## In Uganda

The Programme will be co-financed by the IFAD and RACF/IF for a total amount of USD 94.27 million from IFAD; these FAD funds will be used to finance Components 1 and 2 as well as for Programme management and M&E, in addition, IFAD will cofinance Output 3.1.

## B.5 Justification for GCF funding request

The Programme will be implemented in four countries in East Africa, three of which are classified Least Developed Countries (LDCs) and one is a Small Island Developing State (SIDS). These countries face the greatest challenges to implement this Programme at scale due to their economic vulnerability and limited capacity. The LDCs are among the most vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change, as their economies depend heavily on agriculture, which is highly sensitive to weather patterns.
The wars in Ukraine, Lebanon, Palestine and Syria have deeply affected these countries, through conflict-induced displacement, loss of livelihoods and infrastructure damage. This has had a significant impact on all four countries, with GDP growth falling considerably in all four countries. Half of public debt is external. Between 2015 and 2021, external debt relative to gross national income increased from 31.3 to 37.9 percent in Kenya, from 30 to 48.3 percent in Uganda, from


---
**Page 66**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 63

41.2 to 87.9 percent in Indonesia, and from 39 to 45.2 percent in Tanzania**. High debt limits their ability to finance the investments to meet climate change mitigation and adaptation needs in the dairy sector.

## Financial barriers

### Real and perceived high risks of financing projects with low returns and/or high costs of borrowing pass onto the borrower, leading to higher cost of capital requirements.
- **GDF’s solution**: Impact-focused investment loans with a mandate to invest in dairy value chain actors**. Call for proposals is open until 31 March 2024.

### Financial products are standard and not tailored to the needs of small entities with low value chain returns.
- **GDF’s Green Dairy loans are developed in collaboration with businesses to ensure debt burden is manageable, with adequate loan terms and grace periods**.

### Enterprises are operating at low margins with cashflow constraints. They cannot project investments with low ability to repay.
- **GDF’s solution**: Call for proposals is open until 31 March 2024 to reduce immediate burden of repayment for small entities with low value chain returns, loans for shorter-term investment and longer-term financing.

### Internationally financed operators often do not offer a competitive cost of FX hedging. Smaller entities cannot absorb the costs of FX hedging if they are operating in different markets.
- **Lending in local currency to all SMEs**. Call for proposals is open until 31 March 2024 to allow the GDF’s absorbing the costs of FX hedging.

## Technical barriers

### For businesses prioritizing the use of debt capital, investments into climate-smart practices and technologies are often not fully compared with fast productivity-enhancing investments.

#### Local presence by local implementing financial institutions (see implementation) can provide technical assistance to support businesses in identifying potential co-financing opportunities. This will also help unlock the productivity benefits of climate-smart practices and technologies for smallholder farmers and borrowing SMEs about CC options that are available from their banks and other lending institutions, while providing value chain actors by providing affordable finance in local currencies.

### 240. A central offering that is critical to the delivery of the GDFF’s impact is lending in local currency to dairy SMEs. This is a significant challenge faced by enterprises in the sector, and it poses risks to SMV viability and sustainability**. The proposal seeks to address this challenge through the provision of technical assistance grants (TAG) for dairy SMEs, with a two-fold justification: (a) without FX hedges, it allows for lower interest rates for all the borrow SMEs; and (b) provides cross-border investments into smaller, domestically oriented SMEs that are able to borrow in foreign currencies.

#### 241. Justification for technical assistance grant: The proposal seeks to provide a US$ 10 million technical assistance grant (TAG) to support dairy SMEs in the GDFF region to become more financially effective and competitive through the adoption of smart technologies. This equates to 28 percent of the loan capital allocated to GDFF. With this funding, GDFF will provide grants to dairy SMEs for: (a) capacity development of key institutions, including training and technical assistance to help farmers understand and finance green dairy sector projects; and (b) maximise both the impact and returns of the financial investments in the dairy sector by improving operational efficiencies, and achieve sustainable growth. In doing so, the TAG facility will address non-financial barriers currently hindering greater climate smart financing in the GDFF target sources.

### 8.6 Exit strategy

#### The design of the Programme includes several elements aimed at improving the sustainability of its results and ensuring that they are not lost when the programme ends. These include: (i) a strong focus on engagement and ownership of governments, regional institutions, national dairy boards, and other stakeholders in guiding the implementation of the Programme; (ii) a focus on building capacity for sustainable private sector investments in the dairy sector, in line with NGOs and NAMAs**; (iii) capacity development of key institutions, including training and technical assistance to help farmers understand and finance green dairy projects; and (iv) management that effectively reduce GHG emissions and build climate resilience, without compromising milk production. The Programme will also focus on: (a) strengthening the financial sector through the creation of MRM systems**; (v) value chain strengthening to enhance service delivery, avoid losses and increase dairy consumption for improved nutrition; and (vi) ensuring that the benefits from the GDFF are sustained beyond its life.

#### In terms of engagement and ownership, the Programme was designed with the participation of a wide range of stakeholders in the dairy sector. The Programme will be implemented through a series of partnerships. The validation of the GDFF Project Preparation Facility (PPF) at the COP21 also raised awareness of the Programme’s potential to support the implementation of NAMAs and other climate change initiatives. The Programme’s Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex 7) provides detailed information on how these stakeholders have been engaged in the development of the Programme, including their roles and responsibilities. The GDFF will ensure that lessons learned, documents prepared, and activities financed during the implementation of the Programme are shared with other stakeholders (e.g., through a knowledge management system), disseminated and replicated for the benefit of similar Programmes, including in Latin America and Asia.

** https://acumen.org/blog/new-renewable-energy-solutions-in-africa-now/
