244. **Regenerating environment**, by deeply integrating low carbon, climate-resilient dairy interventions into national and regional policies and regulations, the legacy of Component 1 will be an enhanced enabling environment for the dairy sector to contribute significantly towards achieving the UNFCCC’s goals on impact and sustainability at scale. In particular, the policy and regulatory review (Output 1.1) ensures an enabling framework for the dairy sector to adopt climate-resilient practices. The provision of improved veterinary and extension services (Output 1.2) strengthen the capacity of public services to continue to support similar extension services in the future. The establishment of a market-based approach to GHG emissions reduction, increase in MRV capabilities (Output 1.3) will enable Governments and private operators to effectively monitor GHG emissions from dairy operations and reporting to the UNFCCC, and be responsive to public and private interest to address climate change.

245. The Programme will strengthen the capacity of dairy cooperatives to provide dairy farmers with consistent and stable access to outlets for their milk at fair prices and timely payment (Output 2.1). They will also receive support to build a strong business case for investment in low carbon, climate-resilient practices. By ensuring that dairy farmers have access to a stable market for their milk, they will be less risk averse and more willing to adopt the technical innovations required to reduce GHG emissions from their operations. The Programme will use the well-established L-IFS approach to encourage farmers to adopt practices in the long term. L-IFS is one of FAO’s flagship tools for promoting sustainable intensification of smallholder agriculture, and has been used successfully since 2013 by many projects supported by the IFIs and development partners such as FAO. The L-IFS methodology will be adapted specifically to dairy production systems in the context of climate change adaptations, so that dairy farmers have direct and technical knowledge of the different low carbon practices being promoted.

246. The GDFF will mobilize long-term capital for additional climate financing together with related top end technical support (Output 3.1). This will be achieved by building on the success of the Dairy Action for the Environment programme in supporting the adoption of climate-smart technologies and practices beyond the DaME programme period. By using GDFF resources, the Programme will also provide a platform to attract private sector investment and catalyze additional private capital into the sector. The GDFF will bridge the gap between demand and supply of finance for dairy projects in developing countries by providing technical support to project developers and investors across other value chains and regions. The GDFF’s embedded MAE function (see Annex 2, Chapter 10 for detail on GDFF’s MAE) will ensure that learnings are captured throughout delivery, which will facilitate replication and scaling.

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This document outlines expected performance of the programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Total Invested Framework.

## D.1. Impact potential

248: The Programme will guide smallholder dairy producers and private sector value-chain operators on a best-practice approach to climate-resilient sustainable milk production, which will increase productivity and value chains will be streamlined for maximum efficiency; financial services will be made available, and the sectors' technical capacity will be strengthened. This will result in increased milk yields per cow, improved daily productivity, climate resilience of the sector, reduced emissions intensity of milk, and reduced total emissions.

249: To achieve mitigation targets, the Programme will provide technical assistance for the establishment and improvement of dairy farms, including access to quality feed, water management systems, improved animal health services, processing facilities, as well as the promotion of adaptive livestock breeding, integrated rangeland and pasture management practices. The Programme will also support the development of a market for climate-resilient milk by aiming to blend GCF proceeds with other investors, including IFAD's. In this way, businesses and dairy farmers receive access to finance at competitive rates.

250: The Programme objective of promoting climate resilience and sustainable livestock production will be achieved by supporting local private sector actors, such as dairy cooperatives, aggregators and milk processors, to overcome barriers to adoption of climate-resilient practices. By providing technical assistance for the establishment and improvement of dairy farms, including access to quality feed, water management systems, improved animal health services, processing facilities, as well as the promotion of adaptive livestock breeding, integrated rangeland and pasture management practices. The Programme will also support the development of a market for climate-resilient milk by aiming to blend GCF proceeds with other investors, including IFAD's. In this way, businesses and dairy farmers receive access to finance at competitive rates.

251: The total number of direct beneficiaries will be about 2.3 million individuals and each one has the potential to influence up to five additional people (indirect beneficiaries). This means that a total of approximately 9.4 million indirect beneficiaries are expected to benefit from this Programme. The Programme will invest in a total of 84 districts/counties (12 in Kenya, 7 in Rwanda, 26 in Tanzania and 30 in Uganda).

252: The Programme will support the development of climate-resilient dairy farms through improved management practices, such as better feed management, water management systems, improved animal health services, processing facilities, as well as the promotion of adaptive livestock breeding, integrated rangeland and pasture management practices. The Programme will also support the development of a market for climate-resilient milk by aiming to blend GCF proceeds with other investors, including IFAD's. In this way, businesses and dairy farmers receive access to finance at competitive rates.

253: As a mitigation benefit, over a 20-year horizon and relative to the BAU, the Programme targets a decrease of 14.8 million tonnes CO2e (CO2 equivalent) per year, which is 98 in Kenya, 56 in Rwanda, 73 in Tanzania and 33 in Uganda.

254: Over the same period, the total number of dairy farms expected are expected to decrease (relative to BAU) by 1.0 million farms at the level of the Project, or by 1.9 million farms (61.9% CO2e in Kenya), (8.9 56.6% CO2e in Rwanda), (74.3% 60.4% CO2e) in Tanzania and, or by 3.2 million farms (52.2% 54.4% CO2e) in Uganda.

255: The Programme is also expected to generate 18.9 GWh of energy from solar power generation, which would be used for irrigation, cooling and heating purposes. This will result in a reduction of 30.6 million tonnes CO2e per year (CO2 equivalent), which is 47% in Kenya, 50% in Rwanda, 81% in Tanzania and 90% in Uganda.

256: The Programme will also support the development of climate-resilient dairy farms through improved management practices, such as better feed management, water management systems, improved animal health services, processing facilities, as well as the promotion of adaptive livestock breeding, integrated rangeland and pasture management practices. The Programme will also support the development of a market for climate-resilient milk by aiming to blend GCF proceeds with other investors, including IFAD's. In this way, businesses and dairy farmers receive access to finance at competitive rates.

257: The total number of direct beneficiaries will be about 2.3 million individuals and each one has the potential to influence up to five additional people (indirect beneficiaries). This means that a total of approximately 9.4 million indirect beneficiaries are expected to benefit from this Programme. The Programme will invest in a total of 84 districts/counties (12 in Kenya, 7 in Rwanda, 26 in Tanzania and 30 in Uganda).

# D.2. Paradigm shift potential

## The Program’s comprehensive approach to the paradigm shift in the dairy sector will reduce GHG emissions from the largest group of dairy producers in East Africa.

By implementing policy, institutional and service delivery environments, building climate adaptation capacity, and enabling access to markets and climate solutions for dairy farmers, the Program has the potential to create a win-win situation. The dual approach of the main pillar (refer to section B.3) is designed to address both the supply side and demand side of the dairy sector. This dual pillar has the potential to foster sustainable development in the dairy market by creating a lasting impact on the dairy value chain, while also providing an enabling environment for dairy farmers to adopt climate-resilient solutions.

The Program’s comprehensive approach will provide dairy farmers with access to a wide range of services and products that are essential for improving their livelihoods. These include:

- **Improved livestock management practices**: This includes better feed management, improved breeding techniques, and disease control measures.
- **Access to veterinary services**: This ensures the health and well-being of the animals in the dairy herd.
- **Diversification opportunities**: The Program will provide farmers with access to alternative livelihoods that can help them diversify their income streams.

The dual approach not only improves productivity but also facilitates the transition towards sustainable, low-carbon dairy production systems. This is achieved by:

1. **Improving livestock management practices**: This includes better feed management, improved breeding techniques, and disease control measures.
2. **Access to veterinary services**: This ensures the health and well-being of the animals in the dairy herd.
3. **Diversification opportunities**: The Program will provide farmers with access to alternative livelihoods that can help them diversify their income streams.

In addition, the Program plays an important role in creating a conducive environment for the adoption of climate-resilient practices by:

1. **Conducting awareness campaigns and improving access to information on climate change adaptation measures**: This includes providing farmers with access to information on climate change adaptation measures.
2. **Developing tailored financial products and technical assistance**: This will facilitate this process and improve access to financing and alternative livelihoods for dairy farmers.

The Program also aims to:

1. **Advance regional, national, and local legislative and regulatory frameworks**: This includes the development of a certification program for low-carbon production.
2. **Improve livestock management practices**: This includes better feed management, improved breeding techniques, and disease control measures.
3. **Develop climate change adaptation plans**: This will allow for the finalization of policies aligned with climate change priorities.

The Program will also contribute to:

1. **Improved livestock management practices**: This includes better feed management, improved breeding techniques, and disease control measures.
2. **Access to veterinary services**: This ensures the health and well-being of the animals in the dairy herd.
3. **Diversification opportunities**: The Program will provide farmers with access to alternative livelihoods that can help them diversify their income streams.

The Program’s comprehensive approach is designed to address both the supply side and demand side of the dairy sector, creating a win-win situation for all stakeholders involved.

Improving individual health of rural and urban consumers (sustainable development goal SGD: Good Health and Well-being). In addition, by strengthening veterinary services, the Programme aims to improve animal health, which will lead to better meat quality and increased livestock productivity. This will in turn increase food security.

The Programme contributes to ending hunger, achieving food security, and improving nutrition.

261. In addition, the Programme contributes to positive environmental impacts, in particular soil quality and carbon sequestration. The approach is based on maintaining biodiversity and combating climate change through management and restoration of rangelands. This approach is essential to maintain biodiversity and combat climate change by increasing carbon sequestration and improving soil quality. It also supports the circular economy value chain includes support for ranchland management and restoration.

262. The Programme is expected to generate significant positive economic impacts, including job creation, poverty reduction, and improved livelihoods. This will be achieved through a focus on smallholder dairy producers in the dairy sector. Smallholder dairy producers and local private sector value chain actors will have access to essential financial services, inputs, and markets. These include credit, insurance, training, and technical assistance for development.

263. The Programme reduces gender inequalities by actively promoting women's participation in LPIFs and leadership roles within the dairy sector. This is achieved through capacity building of women farmers on knowledge, social, and gender barriers by equipping male and female farmers with the tools to understand the impact of climate change on their livelihoods. The Programme also promotes the development of a gender action plan and promoting inclusive participatory planning for range land management.

264. The Climate Impact Potential Assessment conducted under the Programme indicates that East African countries such as Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania, and Uganda face significant challenges related to the dairy value chain. These include climate change impacts on livestock productivity, increased water scarcity, and emissions. Smallholder farmers make up most dairy producers, yet they face limitations due to low productivity.

265. Kenya is the driest among the four countries, with annual rainfall ranging from 400 to 600mm in some areas. It has the highest number of climate-related disasters, with 78 recorded since 1971. The central parts of Kenya have the lowest rainfall and are most vulnerable to droughts. However, exposure to drought is more significant in the southwest, especially around cities like Kisumu, Nakuru, and Arusha.

266. In Uganda, people are most affected by flooding in the northern, eastern, and central regions along the course of the White Nile. The annual average number of people affected varies from 10,000 to 25,000 per administrative sub-county. This is due to the high rainfall intensity and frequency during the rainy season, which falls into the "rain class" for most areas, indicating increased exposure and vulnerability of animals to climate extremes.

267. Rwanda is one of the countries with the highest total annual rainfall (1500mm to 1600mm). Due to the high rainfall intensity and frequency during the rainy season, this country has a high risk of flooding in the northern and western regions.


---
**Page 74**
---

Despite strategies based on centuries of exposure to droughts and floods becoming less effective due to difficulties in implementing coping strategies.

268. In Tanzania, the provinces of Tanga, Morogoro, and Pwani have the highest number of people affected by droughts. The impact is most severe where population density is high; however, the incidence of drought is comparatively lower in places such as the Kilogo Rungu and Unga Game Reserves, where population density is low.

269. Each of these countries suffers from a significant development deficit which negatively impacts their ability to steer the dairy sector on a path to low-emission and climate-resilient development. In all four countries, public fiscal space for investment in the dairy sector is limited due to competing demands for resources, including concessional financing to fund their dairy sector programs. As a result, vulnerable populations such as smallholders, women, and youth are highly exposed to risks related to technological, organizational, and policy constraints.

270. The lack of funding for climate-smart agriculture and climate-resilient practices is a significant barrier in the four countries. This results from limited public fiscal space for investment in the dairy sector, as well as in modern equipment, infrastructure, and better farming practices. This limitation exacerbates the vulnerability of the dairy sector to adverse impacts of climate change such as prolonged droughts and increased exposure to disease, leading to reduced productivity and environmental degradation.

271. In addition, poor communication between climate information suppliers and agricultural extension services, technological constraints in accessing climate information, and limited access to modern equipment and underdeveloped communication networks in East Africa hinder the effective dissemination of climate information and adaptation practices among dairy farmers.

### D.5 Country ownership

272. Country ownership of the Programme is ensured through alignment with national policies and ongoing GCF investments, as well as a participatory design process of the Programme.

273. Participating countries' NDCs focus on reducing GHG emissions and increasing resilience to CC. Support to the dairy sector in these countries will contribute to improved livelihoods, nutrition, soil fertility, rural livelihoods, and social inclusion. Therefore, the Programme aligns with the national strategies of the participating countries for climate change adaptation and mitigation, as well as their policies on agriculture and climate change. In Kenya, for example, the Programme aligns with the Agriculture Sector Development Strategy (2019-2023), the National Climate Change Action Plan (2017–2025), and the Kenya National Strategy on Climate Change and Low Carbon Development. In Uganda, the Programme will closely align with the National Climate Change Strategy in Rwanda, the Programme will closely align with the National Climate Change Strategy, and with Rwanda's National Climate Change and Environment Policy. In Tanzania, the Programme will align with the National Action Plan on the Agriculture Sector and the National Climate Change Policy.

274. In addition, the Programme aims to integrate climate change adaptation and mitigation by reviewing and developing national dairy policies, such as the Dairy Industry Strategy and Investment Plan, and the Dairy Industry Act (1980). The Programme will also support the development of a draft National Dairy Development Strategy at the national level; the draft Tanzania Dairy Development Strategy in Tanzania. In Rwanda, the Programme will work closely with the Ministry of Agriculture to develop a National Dairy Development Strategy.

275. At the regional level, the Programme aligns and supports ongoing GCF projects in East Africa. At the regional level, the objectives of the Programme complement the GCF regional project: Strengthening Climate Information Systems for Adaptation and Mitigation (SCIAMACHY). The SCIAMACHY project aims to enhance climate resilience in eight countries (GCF member states) through the establishment of early warning and climate dissemination systems. In addition, at the regional level, the Programme will support the development of a Regional Dairy Development Strategy.

276. The Programme was designed following an extensive consultation process with local stakeholders, ensuring that the needs of recipients are considered and ensuring full country ownership of the Programme. The NDCs in each

## D. Efficiency and effectiveness

The ex-ante financial and economic viability of the Programme is demonstrated by an economic and financial feasibility analysis (EFAA). The EFAA looks at the profitability of investments for the entire Programme from the perspective of society, while a financial analysis considers the profitability of these investments from the perspective of investors. The EFAA was conducted using the Global Livestock Environmental Assessment Model – Interactive - GLEAM (Annex 22).

The net cash flows are based on the incremental approach, which results from comparing the Win and Without scenarios. The Win scenario is defined as the situation where the Programme is implemented, while the Without scenario represents a situation in which no investments were made by the Programme. The incremental cost of the Programme was evaluated using the Global Livestock Environmental Assessment Model - Interactive – GLEAM (Annex 22).

At the regional level, the analysis shows that DaIMA represents an economically viable investment for the social and economic development of the region. The EFAA demonstrates a positive net present value and profitability.

The ERF is US$181.20 million under the base case scenario, while the ERR is 13.25 percent. Under the high economic performance (HEP) scenario, the ERF is US$479.60 million and the ERR is 20.55 percent.

At the national level, the analysis shows that DaIMA represents an economically viable investment for the social and economic development of the country. The EFAA demonstrates a positive net present value and profitability.

The ERF is US$181.20 million under the base case scenario, while the ERR is 13.25 percent. Under the high economic performance (HEP) scenario, the ERF is US$479.60 million and the ERR is 20.55 percent.

The net present value of the Programme is positive in all scenarios considered. The analysis shows that DaIMA represents a robustly economically worthwhile investment.

# LOGICAL FRAMEWORK

## E.1 Programme Focus

  - Increased resilience (adaptation)

## E.2 GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max 600 words, approximately <2 pages)

### Assumptions about the current state (baseline)
| Scale | Description |
|---|---|
| **Livestock, especially cattle,** are the main source of methane emissions in the East Africa region. They represent the largest livestock and the second highest source representing 58% of total GHG emissions from agriculture, forestry and other land uses. Through improved management practices and mitigation pathways across all scales, there is potential to reduce absolute and emission intensities with significant co-benefits for improving a growing demand and the growth of dairy markets. |
| **Exacerbated climate hazards are reported to cause direct damage in the livestock sector with direct and indirect effects on animal health, feed and feeding strategies, artificial insemination, manure management, renewable energy use, and water availability.** These vulnerabilities are exacerbated by changes in disease patterns, availability and quality of feed resources, and ecosystem services (degradation of pastures, erosion, reduced soil carbon sequestration). |

### Potential target scenarios
- **Paradigm shift will move a away from the current state of growing absolute emissions, low productivity, high emission intensities in the vulnerable dairy sector, with limited access to international markets except for export milk.**
  - Shift towards low production intensities and improved, more resilient systems at farm and system levels (including interventions that reduce methane emissions through better feed processing, formalize marketing chains for milk and products, and improve animal health).
- **Paradigm shift will be accompanied by measures that strengthen the enabling environment policies, regulation and institutional capacities.**

### How the project/programme will contribute
- Implementing a set of integrated and complementary interventions to improve animal health, feed and feeding strategies, artificial insemination, manure management, renewable energy use, water availability, and national capacities (vetinary extension and extension services, access to credit, etc.), ensuring better access to finance, at farm level.
- Improving the capacity for dairy farmers to make dairy food stuffs more efficiently and effectively through improved processing technologies.
- Scaling up of pasture management practices and animal health strategies (e.g., better manure management of grazing intensity) to reduce methane emissions and climate change impacts. Ensuring availability of high-quality feed resources, improving livestock productivity intensities, while sustaining production levels and ecosystem services (soil carbon sequestration).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 86

## Activity 1.4.2: Establish Regional Partnerships for Climate Change on climate-smart dairy sector transformation

The Programme will provide scientific support and capacity building to regional centers of excellence (regional centers of excellence) to expose them to best practices in the field of climate change and GHG emissions for the dairy sector.

### 1.4.2.1: Strengthen dairy groups and cooperatives on organizational management and service delivery

This activity aims to strengthen cooperative, organizational, and technical capacities of dairy cooperatives and MCGs to enhance their ability to adapt to climate change and gender awareness of their members. The activity will also support them in enhancing their roles as MGCS and MCGs.

### 1.4.2.2: Build and enhance productive alliances among the dairy value chain

This activity supports productive alliances between farmer cooperatives and enterprises along the dairy value chain, including milk processing plants, processors, and distributors to ensure affordable, high-quality inputs and services.

### 1.4.2.3: Promote renewable energy and water management technologies

The Programme will focus on resource use efficiency in dairy production systems and be fired by green energy sources. Solar collectors are applicable to relatively larger processes in the short-term given high upfront costs.

## E.7: Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

### 280 Each EME will implement and coordinate the overall M&E system building on the existing M&E systems of the related IGF-funded projects/finances, allocate resources for M&E tasks and deliverables, aggregate M&E data and present them in overarching reports and knowledge documents. verify and ensure quality and timeliness of M&E data and information, and provide regular updates to the GDFP Secretariat on progress towards achieving the expected outputs and achieve the anticipated outcomes, ensure the quality of Programme monitoring. Programme closure and final evaluation timelines are estimated at 12 months after the last disbursement of the IGF-funded project/finances. The M&E system will be designed in line with the overall methodology guidance on overall M&E implementation, as well as consideration of timely and high-quality reports to the GCF. M&E guidance beyond the PIM will provide further details on how the M&E system will be implemented. Specific responsibilities for different aspects of the M&E system and reporting aspects will be handled by the countries through their PMAs. IFAD will organize and lead annual supervision missions, including mid-term and end term evaluations.

### 281 The overall M&E for the GDFF will be built around its T&C and framework. The system will continuously be built through ongoing adaptive management so that the Programme can regularly assess its performance in supporting planning and reporting, learn and adapt to improve its climate adaptation outcomes. While PDAs are responsible for the M&E of their respective projects/finances for the GDFF, the Wholesale Banks in each country will have a partner on-site in data collection and analysis. Details on the MRE are proposed in Annex 11.

### 282 Specifically, for the GDFF under Component 3, a senior GEF Coordinator will be recruited at AFAD’s Regional Office to support the overall coordination of the M&E system. The GEF Coordinator will be supported by a team of experienced technical experts and financial officers. A team of highly experienced Financial and M&E Specialists will be recruited to assist the DaMpa PRA in developing an appropriate reporting, monitoring and evaluation system for

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 90

#### Work Plans and Budgets (AWPBs)

The M&E Officer in each of the national PMUs will prepare the national Programme's consolidated AWPBs, which will be submitted to GCF for approval. The AWPBs will include:

(i) a time line to complete each activity,
(ii) tabular summary information on the AWPBs of the overall project to easily assess progress,
and (iii) responsible reporting parties of each indicator.

As the Programme implements, the preparation of annual AWPBs will be a participatory process that will be based on the activities needed and proposed by the beneficiaries. The AWPBs will be prepared in consultation with the GCF representative and the national PMU. The M&E Officer will be responsible for all planning processes and for inclusion of key stakeholders to guarantee transparency, accountability while ensuring sufficiency of M&E information. The AWPBs will be submitted to GCF at least 60 days before submission to the National Planning Committee (NPC) as the next level of approval.

### Reporting System

Each participating country will facilitate preparation of each quarterly, six-monthly and an annual progress highlighting achieved successes, constraints, and lessons learnt; recommendations and action plans to address the identified constraints in the subsequent plans and implementation periods. The corresponding national reports are to be submitted to GCF level with technical support provided by ECO and other service providers.

### Monitoring and Evaluation

Responsibilities for monitoring and evaluation with the national E&G and Component 3 implementation team. Each country host team will be responsible for ensuring that all data collected is accurate, complete and timely. The M&E Officer from each country as the key implementer of the Programme, will play a key role in data collection and analysis and reporting. They will collect and report on all relevant indicators and progress against the approved AWPBs. GCF will provide technical support to the national PMUs for data collection and analysis, as well as learning and adaptive management events organized internally across projects or those supported by GDFG directly. The RSC, with technical support required from GCF, will monitor and evaluate the Programme's progress against the approved AWPBs and will report on all relevant indicators related to the Programme, and that regional added value is achieved and sustained beyond the programme life. Specifically, RSC will monitor progress with respect to the achievement of the national priorities and the regional added value. The GCF will provide technical support for data collection and analysis as well as reporting on the relevant indicators be disaggregated by (i) type of project; (ii) gender; (iii) age (youth vs non-youth); (iv) country at least and may further be required at project level.

### Supervision and Implementation

Support missions will be organized jointly by GCF, E&G and the Governments of Kenya, Rwanda, Tanzania and Uganda to assess and review progress and assist implementing partners and the PMUs improve on Programme implementation based on the mission's support.


---
**Page 94**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 81

and recommendation. The supervision missions will assess the overall physical and financial performance, identify implementation challenges and propose possible corrective measures to help address them. This should be done in accordance with all the fiduciary aspects of the financing agreement. The supervision missions will be conducted at least twice a year or more frequently if conditions may warrant, and according to IFAD’s GCF own policies and procedures including the performance monitoring framework. Supervision missions will provide feedback on the implementation progress against the approved project document and make recommendations, focusing on the achievements and constraints during the implementation. The support missions will monitor the implementation of the project and assess its impact in terms of achievement of the project objectives and constraints encountered during the implementation. The support missions will help identify and address emerging issues, build technical capacities, and to provide guidance for future projects. These missions may be conducted at any time as required by the Fund or the implementing agency. They can include, but not limited to: the Financial Expert, M&E expert, targeting specialist etc. It may also add value if the budget allows into a selection of PMU teams serving as facilitators and trainers for capacity building, knowledge sharing, continuous adaptation management and use of best practices aimed at strengthening knowledge management within the project and government set-up.

287. Baseline Survey. The Programme will conduct a baseline survey at the beginning of implementation in the targeted areas. The survey will be designed to collect information on the current situation of the targeted communities and households, and the baseline data collected from this survey will be included in the baseline survey that will be updated based on the missions by the Outcome Survey. The study will be contracted to an external service provider (ESP) with a proven track record in conducting such surveys. The ESP will provide technical assistance for the design of the survey, its implementation and target refinement. This will be done in a way that ensures data collection appropriates and for purpose in the context of Programme implementation.

288. Mid-Term Review and Final Evaluation. Internal and Final Independent Evaluations will be planned and managed by IFAD according to the requirements of the GCF. The evaluation will be conducted by an independent team, including IFAD staff, and will be carried out at least once during the project period. The evaluation will include a comprehensive household/business survey including both quantitative and qualitative data collection and analysis. IFAD will lead evaluation process following IFAD’s own policies and procedures for evaluations. The evaluation will be based on the results of the missions by the Outcome Survey, the MTR Group, made up of IFAD, GCF and the other co-financers. The MTR and final evaluation will assess an element of the Programme's contributions to the project.

289. Knowledge Management, Communication and Learning. The Programme will elaborate and implement a KM and Communication Strategy that will: (i) provide Project beneficiaries with the necessary material to sustain the technical knowledge acquired with the support of the Project (production of training materials and communication tools); (ii) share knowledge gained from the project implementation among all stakeholders involved in the project, including IFAD, GCF and other co-financers; (iii) disseminate and current Project in various fields and based on the information collected as part of the monitoring of results or from specific thematic missions; (iv) share knowledge generated by the Project with other projects and programmes implemented by IFAD, GCF and other co-financers; (v) provide a platform for learning and visibility. In achieving the Policy Component, the KM and Communication Strategy will strive aim to develop and create channels of policy influence and dialogue among stakeholders involved in the project. The KM and Communication Strategy should also establish a Community of Practice (CoP) to support the development and coordination of regional implementation creating harmonized programme requirements in all levels

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## Selected Risk Category: Institutional Capacity for Implementation and Sustainability

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Medium |

### Description:
290. Limited skills exist in the dairy value chain in the four countries, particularly in areas of social inclusion and gender, dairy technicians, extension services, M&E and functional Dairy LFF specialists in the implementing partners (IPs). In Uganda, the IPs have limited capacity to manage their own projects (e.g., project management, financial management, procurement, and human resources) due to their mandate (e.g., technical assistance, capacity development). Further, limited M&E skills within PMUs and government staff in general is bound to affect the implementation of the project. The lack of a strong institutional framework for data collection, harmonization and reporting at the regional level may also be a challenge.

## Selected Risk Category: Financial Management and Financial Risks

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | High | Medium |

### Description:
292. The Programme involves multiple implementing partners (IPs) such as Project Management Units (PMUs), districts county governments, all of which hold fiduciary responsibilities. Although the PMU will consist of qualified personnel with experience in financial management and reporting, there is a risk that some IPs may not have adequate capacity to manage these finances. The GCF, IFAD and the GoG. The districts county governments will receive funding from the Ministry of Finance and report to it. There are also risks associated with the lack of adequate audit coverage for the project due to the involvement of multiple financial institutions (e.g., banks, credit unions) in disbursing funds to the IPs. Additionally, there is a possibility that some high-risk expenditure categories may not be adequately addressed during the audit.

### Mitigation Measure(s):
293. Formal agreements will be established with the Ministry of Finance and the participating districts county governments. These MoUs will clearly outline the requirements for financial reporting with periodic reviews by the GCF, IFAD and the GoG. The IPs will also have to submit monthly financial statements to a finance staff recruited through a competitive process. This training will focus on: (i) Familiarization with GCF and IFAD policies and procedures; (ii) Financial management and reporting practices for project implementation across different project components, and (iii) Overall financial management practices related to the project. The risks associated with inadequate audit coverage of the project due to the involvement of multiple financial institutions will be mitigated by implementing a dedicated finance staff at each IP. A Fund will be maintained in dedicated project accounts at the Central Bank of each district county government. These funds will be subject to regular internal and external audits, including monthly bank account reconciliations and financial reports generated for these dedicated accounts to ensure transparency and accountability. (ii) Each district/county receiving project funds will be required to establish sub-project

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 93

**Selected Risk Factor 3 Sector Strategies and Policies**

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | High | Description |

295. Each of the four countries recently developed a Livestock Sector Transformation Plan, including a Dairy Development Strategy. The governance structures for these strategies are described in the respective country profiles and are formulated in an inclusive manner, involving all organized stakeholders. These policies are aligned with the national development plans and are supported by the government. However, there is a risk that the sector will not be able to implement these strategies due to the lack of professional organization of this category of actors. Policy dialogue is thus dominated by the private sector, which has a strong influence on policy formulation and implementation in all four institutions.

296. During the design process, the four Governments committed to adhere to IFAD's environmental safeguards and targeting policy, to maximise positive social and environmental impacts, and ensure that DaMilk is in line with IFAD's strategic objectives. The Programme will be designed as a multi-year project, covering all stages of the entire design process: a) The Programme will support the organisation of smallholder farmers at regional, national, and sub-national levels; b) The Programme will support the development of a market-based value chain approach to dairy production in each country; and c) The Programme will also support the review and update of sector regulations, but also their enforcement. This will be supported by the National Dairy Boards, District milk inspectors, and strengthening of laboratory facilities for milk control.

**Selected Risk Factor 4 Project Scope and Approach**

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Description |

297. The Programme strategic approach responds to the sector priority needs, characterised by a lack of milk supply, dominance of the informal raw milk value chain and inadequate access to services and finance. However, the selected strategy is not without risks. The Programme design could be revised or adapted in response to changes in any one of either the marketing aspects, or production. The DaMilk design covers a large range of domains due to the overall complexity of the dairy sector. These include: i) supply chain development; ii) market access; iii) consumption; iv) market access, low consumption, price gaps. The Programme must address all these challenges at the same time and in parallel. This is a complex task that requires a high level of coordination and a moderate level of complexity of the programme design, which is however common in similar value chain projects.

298. In case the situation of the value chain evolves during implementation, some adjustments may be needed on project activities, including budget allocation, without modifying the project structure, theory of change, objectives and indicators. These adjustments could be made at any stage of the project cycle, but would require a reduced emphasis on production and productivity. These adjustments could be made at the MTR stage. Some of the interventions will be more flexible than others; for example, those related to market access may have more flexibility in terms of partners that have experience and comparative advantage in these domains. In addition, Design should be flexible and not

## Selected Risk Factor 5 Regional Context

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Government | Medium | Medium |

### Description
Despite recent protest by the youth, the four countries are characterized by a good socio-political stability and a rather low occurrence of inter-ethnic tensions. However, in some rural communities, there is growing concern because young people have become disenfranchised with agriculture and often do not have access to high quality input and credit services. The government has been working on the issue for several years but has not yet achieved significant results.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
As the population grows in the country, agriculture will remain an engine for job creation, including livestock and poultry production. Small scale farmers are key actors in reducing poverty, increasing food security, improving nutrition, and enhancing resilience. DAFFA will strengthen the livelihoods of the most disadvantaged rural communities by providing them with access to quality inputs and credit services.

## Selected Risk Factor 6 Limited adoption/reversion to BAU practices by beneficiaries

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Government | Medium | Medium |

### Description
Small dairy farmers show a low adoption of new approaches or practices or revert to business-as-usual practices. The use of participative approaches for capacity development such as farmers field days, demo farms and training sessions is limited in the four countries.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The use of participative approaches for capacity development such as farmers field days, demo farms and training sessions will be supported by sector specific organizations (e.g., SQUAD and KOB in Uganda; Tana River Dairy Board (TRDB) and the Tanzania Cooperative Development Commission (TCDC); FDC in Kenya). This will help to increase farmer’s access to better livestock breeds and veterinary services, reduce disease prevalence and lower emissions but also at a better economic return for farmers, which creates economic incentives.

## Selected Risk Factor 7 Insufficient access to markets to incentivise the paradigm shift

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

### Description
The four countries have been successful in reducing rural poverty and consequently inequality and social tensions. There are however limited links between the agricultural sector and other sectors.

# Other

## Low Medium Description

303. Small dairy farmers are interested in improved practices but they do not have sufficient access to market to meet the economic return they need.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

304. The Programme has a strong focus on developing access to market for small holders under Output 2.1: Smallholder Dairy Farmers’ Access to Market. This will be achieved through direct marketing, producer cooperatives, MCCs and MCFs. It will also build productive alliances and linkages along the dairy value chain.

305. Establishing four relatively small-sized feedlot operations in each of the sub-sector dairy sites poses several challenges. Relatively low funding-base can constrain the interest of banks to participate in the scheme. Lack of understanding of the business model, particularly the feedlot operation, and lack of awareness about the financial advantages of green dairy investments along the dairy businesses can reduce loan demand. Finally, lack of recognition of the value chain by small businesses can result in low recovery rates (particularly in more technical) aspects of green dairy investments.

**Selected Risk Factor 8: Risk of low demand for and repayment of GDF Funds**

## Medium Description

306. To address these risks, GFPT aims to include several design features and instruments. Biostatistical technical support provided by GFPT aims to substantially increase the borrowers’ understanding of both the banks and borrowing SMEs. The loan terms will be flexible (e.g., longer maturity, lower interest rates), performance-based long grace and repayment periods, will support successful cash flow management in the borrowing companies and assist them in building up their business. In addition, GFPT aims to build positive credit histories for small and medium dairy companies, providing a soft start for a permanent client relationship with existing banks and other financial investments for participating dairy businesses.

## GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### GOAL 1: IMPROVE THE CLIMATE RESILIENCE OF SMALL-SCALE DAIRY FARMERS

307. The Programme has been reviewed in line with IFAD’s Social, Environmental and Climate Assessment Procedures (SECAP) and classified as Moderate for the categorization of Environmental & Social risks as well as associated mitigation measures. This classification is based on the fact that the project will not significantly increase risk of harm to people or the environment and will be located away from environmentally or socially sensitive areas. In addition, the project will not have significant adverse effects on human health (e.g., primary or secondary) and have a low likelihood of causing serious adverse effects to human health or the environment.

308. Recognizing that moderate adverse impacts may range on the environment and human health during its implementation, the Programme has developed an Environmental & Social Management Plan (ESMP). The ESMP is part of the ESPM. The ESMP entails potential environmental and social impacts associated with project activities and their mitigation measures. To keep track of the implementation of the ESMP, there will be two types of monitoring to be conducted: a) Monitoring of the ESMP by the Project Team; b) Monitoring of the ESMP by the GCF Social Safeguard Specialist will conduct at least one field visit at an interval of six months to verify reports submitted to the GCF. The ESMP will be reviewed and updated as required on the basis of the project’s progress, and as required based on the risk level of the sub-project. As regards Reviews, there will be annual reviews of the ESMP and all other relevant documents by the Project Team and the GCF Social Safeguard Specialist. These reviews may involve programme stakeholders at various levels from the sub-project level to the national level in the four countries involved.

## 309. During proposed development phase, consultation of potential stakeholders in selected sites was done to solicit their views and concerns regarding the proposed Interventions. For a continuous engagement during implementation of the program, a stakeholders engagement strategy has been prepared as part of the EMIF. The strategy provides a framework for regular communication with stakeholders on the progress of the project and any emerging issues so that the programme is carried out effectively. As part of the stakeholders engagement and monitoring mechanism, a Green Climate Fund Reporting Mechanism (GRFM) will be established in each country. The GRFM will address queries from the public and provide an avenue for feedback about the program; respond to problems, complaints, and suggestions and ensure GCF also remains transparent.

## 310. The EMIF will be disseminated on the GCF or IFAD Board meeting date which is earlier: it will be posted on the IFAD website, on the GCF website and shared with relevant stakeholders including government counterparts, civil society organizations, and other institutions. The Programme Coordination Teams at the county level will make and distribute copies of the EMIF to all stakeholders in their respective counties. The EMIF will also be made available through the GCF’s Freedom of Information Act and the IFAD’s Public Disclosure Policy. Therefore various communication tools will be utilized for the engagement process, such as Project Notes, project briefs, newsletters, press releases, social media, seminars, workshops, e-mails, phone conversations with stakeholders.

## 311. The gender assessment (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 311.1. The gender assessment and action plan of the Programme provides an overview of the gender inequalities that hinder women, youth, and other vulnerable groups participation in and benefiting from dairy production and other livelihoods activities. It also provides a summary of the findings of the Gender Equality Analysis (GEA) and Social Inclusion (SI) assessment analysis and sample. Part 1 gives a brief GESI analysis of smaller-scale dairy farmers, women, youth, and other vulnerable groups in Tanzania, Uganda, Kenya, Rwanda, and Ethiopia. Part 2 provides an overview of the gender action plan for each country.

## 312. The methodology for the gender assessment and action plan was informed by in-country missions in Tanzania, Uganda, Kenya, and Rwanda. The field missions included consultations with key stakeholders and in-depth interviews with women dairy farmers, youth, and other vulnerable groups. The GESI analysis is based on the findings of these missions and field examples to inform the needed interventions. How resources (cattle, water, money, and family land) are allocated within households and communities has a significant impact on how men and women, young people, and other vulnerable in dairy production and the barriers they face Part 2 provides an overview of the GESI action plan for each country.

## 313. To address these barriers, the Programme will: (i) Increase women economic empowerment through dairy production by providing access to productive resources; (ii) Reduce time and labor burdens and balancing workloads activities; (iii) Increase their voice and decision making at individual, household, group, and community levels and in local government institutions. The GESI action plan is designed to address the barriers that women face as men and women of different groups, disabilities, ethnicity, and religion are targeted to access productive assets, services, and information. The GESI action plan will be implemented through a combination of interventions by Providers with experience in community-centric approaches promoting integration of young men and women, vulnerable groups, and other marginalized groups into the dairy value chain. This will be done in collaboration with academics and researchers. The GESI action plan will be monitored and evaluated through the project M&E system.

## G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### G.3.1 Disbursements/Fund Flow: Designated accounts (DAIs) will be administered following “receiving fund” principles. The DAIs will be established in each implementing partner country (IPs). The IPs will notify their Partners (PIs) before any funds transferred to the IPs, except Tanzania where Mcids signed with the IPs only when they are not included in the financing agreement. Each project will open and maintain separate DAIs at UCO to receive


---
**Page 100**
---

GCF funds will be disbursed through IFAD. Similarly, separate project accounts in local currency will be opened by PCUs, district offices and IPs to receive funds from the project designated/operating accounts. Countries where single treasury account is established will open a GCF sub-account in the single treasury account. In case of fund issues to ILR, direct payments to ILRs may be considered based on the MoUs signed between the Borrower and ILR. However, all Withdrawal Approvals and IPs need to be submitted by the PCU of each Country.

### 315. Disbursements will be based on submission of quarterly Interim Financial Reports (IFRs) within 90 days after the quarter end. The IFRs will be prepared in accordance with the GCF financial reporting framework, and submitted by PCUs through IFAD’s Client Portal and will include a cash forecast for the next two quarters. As per GCF rules, there will be no disbursements beyond the approved budget.

#### 316. Accounting and Financial Reports (Results (GDP) and Inputs (Irr)): Accounting and reporting will be performed through Country’s IFM system and will follow national regulation, which is in a gradual process of harmonization with GCF standards. The following countries have adopted the GCF financial reporting framework: Tanzania (C-SOTF), Kenya (RiMIF), and Uganda (GCF). On-the shell accounting software will be used, and adequate client support provided by IFAD. Financial statements for each quarter will be prepared in accordance with IPSAS accrual basis and IPSAS Cash Basis of accounting respectively.

#### 317. Quarterly interim financial reports will be submitted to IFAD within 90 days after each quarter end and unsolicited semi-annual reports on the use of funds by source of funding, category, components, sub-components and activities will be internally audited quarterly by the Independent Internal Audit (IA) Unit under the auspices of the Internal Auditing Division (IAD) at the Ministry of Finance. The Borrower is required to prepare the financial reports with standard templates and submit them to the executing agency’s PCU. (Please refer to Annex 10 for details).

#### 318. Internal Controls: Executing agencies will have the oversight responsibility over IPs, PCUs and all IPs will have adequate internal control arrangements in place to ensure that funds are used for intended purposes. Rwanda (C-SOTF), Tanzania (C-SOTF) and Kenya (RiMIF) will submit their quarterly interim financial reports and unsolicited semi-annual reports on the use of funds by source of funding, category, components, sub-components and activities to the Independent Internal Audit (IA) Unit under the auspices of the Internal Auditing Division (IAD) at the Ministry of Finance. The Borrower is required to prepare the financial reports with standard templates and submit them to the executing agency’s PCU.

#### 319. External Audits: Financial statements of Rwanda (RiDOP), Tanzania (C-SOTF), Kenya (RiMIF) and Uganda (GCF) will be externally audited by the Auditor General of each country in accordance with International Auditing Standards, and the audits report will be submitted to IFAD within six months of the Borrower’s fiscal year-end. The Borrower is required to provide an opinion on GCF funds.

### G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

- No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not contain any confidential or sensitive information.
- X with confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may contain confidential and/or sensitive information. In this case, the Borrower is required to provide a redacted copy of the funding proposal to the Secretariat along with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the GCF rules.
- Redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website: The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential info.

We refer to the programme titled Dairy Interventions for Mitigation and Adaptation (DaIMa) as included in the funding proposal submitted by International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) to us on 12 September 2024.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of The National Treasury, the National Designated Authority of the Republic of Kenya.
