# Secretariat's assessment of FP269

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

### Strengths

* The first GCF dedicated dairy programme, developed in response to climate risks affecting the sector, particularly drought and increasing temperatures and reduced availability of water, while reducing greenhouse gas emissions, especially methane.

### Points of caution

* Multi-country programme, which might lead to unsynchronized implementation across the four countries. Mitigating measures have been included to decouple implementation in the case of delays.
* Sectoral programme designed to build resilience across the dairy value chains across the four countries through implementation by public and private actors, including financial institutions.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms listed in the term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE) and if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in annex II to document GCF/B.42/02.

## Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### 2.1 Programme background

* The dairy sector in East Africa, including Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania, faces significant challenges due to climate change. Dairy farming is a major contributor to greenhouse gas emissions but also vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change threats on its long-term sustainability. Inefficiencies in dairy farming lead to high production costs and low productivity. The sector is characterized by smallholder farmers with limited market access, challenges in sourcing local feed and fodder, inadequate extension and financial services, and poor animal health management. These are exacerbated by low livestock genetic potential, herd management issues, animal diseases, insufficient extension support, limited awareness of climate-resilient and low-carbon practices and technologies, rangeland degradation and gender inequalities.


---
**Page 115**
---

### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Strengthened national capacities and enhanced enabling environment for low emissions climate resilient dairy sectors (total cost USD6.04 million, GCF cost USD5.82 million, GEF cost USD7.24 million)

- **Component 1.1:** The Green Dairy Financing Facility (GDFFF) will provide two loan products to support the development of a national dairy financing platform and the establishment of a revolving fund for low-emission technologies in the dairy sector.


---
**Page 116**
---

10. Currently, we will create a supportive environment for transforming East Africa’s dairy sector, driving higher productivity, enhancing climate resilience and reducing emissions intensity. It will focus on four key outputs: establishing a supportive policy and regulatory framework (output 1.1); enhancing public service delivery in the dairy sector, particularly through specialized livestock extension and veterinary services (output 1.2), strengthening the capacity to monitor GHG emissions and climate vulnerability monitoring in the dairy sector (output 1.3) and improving knowledge management (output 1.4).

- Output 1.1 (Strengthening supportive policy and regulatory environment established) aims to establish a climate-responsive policy and regulatory environment to support the sustainable transformation of the dairy sector in East Africa. Activity 1.1.1 focuses on reviewing and formulating national policies, strategies, and regulations that promote sustainable development, adaptation and mitigation. This includes aligning national policies with nationally determined contributions (NDCs) under the Paris Agreement, harmonizing existing policy frameworks, revising regulations and fostering private sector engagement. Sub-activities 1.1.1.1 and 1.1.1.2 will support the review of existing policies and strategies to ensure alignment with NDCs and revise regulations and enhance technology transfer. Sub-activities 1.1.3.1 and 1.1.4.1 will strengthen dairy value chains by promoting sustainable practices, such as improved feed management, raising awareness on the nutritional benefits of milk and food safety, promoting increased consumption of high-quality animal products, and supporting the development of a national dairy strategy.

- Sub-activity 1.1.2.1 will focus on awareness initiatives for climate-vulnerable populations, while sub-activity 1.1.2.2 will enhance dairy inspection systems to ensure quality standards and reduce disease transmission. Sub-activities 1.1.3.1 and 1.1.4.1 will support the development of policies for sustainable dairy farming, identifying tenure rights and supporting land-use planning to enhance dairy production in East Africa. Sub-activity 1.1.5.1 will focus on awareness-raising campaigns and capacity-building efforts to secure land rights, particularly for women and youth. These activities are expected to create a more climate-resilient, inclusive and sustainable dairy sector in East Africa.

10. Output 1.2 (Enhancing public service delivery in the dairy sector, particularly through specialized livestock extension and veterinary services) aims to strengthen public service delivery in the dairy sector through improved veterinary and extension services. Activity 1.2.1 focuses on strengthening livestock extension services by providing training for extension officers and developing tools to address challenges caused by climate-induced diseases. This will involve expanding veterinary outreach, investing in diagnostic facilities, and supporting the development of a national animal health information system and community animal health workers with better tools and training. Sub-activity 1.2.1.1 will support the deployment of veterinarians and paraveterinary professionals, particularly young and female extension officers, to provide specialized services. Sub-activity 1.2.1.2 will focus on the development of veterinary laboratories and improve feed quality testing. Activity 1.2.2 focuses on strengthening livestock extension services by providing training for extension officers and developing tools to address challenges caused by climate-induced diseases. This will involve expanding veterinary outreach, investing in diagnostic facilities, and supporting the development of a national animal health information system and community animal health workers with better tools and training. Sub-activity 1.2.2.1 will develop training modules on climate mitigation and adaptation, including topics like feed production and management, disease prevention and control, and livestock breeding. Sub-activity 1.2.2.2 will train village master trainers and facilitators to scale up climate-smart practices, with a focus on women and youth participation. Sub-activity 1.2.2.3 will promote innovative extension tools, including digital platforms for information dissemination and training materials. These initiatives are expected to increase access to knowledge, improve productivity and enhance climate resilience in the dairy sector.

10. Output 1.3 (Strengthening the capacity to monitor GHG emissions and climate vulnerability monitoring in the dairy sector) aims to strengthen the capacity of East African countries to monitor GHG emissions from livestock activities using mobile-based technologies (MBVTs), including handheld devices, and improve climate vulnerability monitoring in the dairy sector. Activity 1.3.1


---
**Page 117**
---

### Activity 1.3.2 (Enhancing regional cooperation and improving management)
- **Objective**: To enhance knowledge management and regional cooperation to promote the replication of climate-resilient and low-carbon dairy practices across East Africa.
- **Activity 1.4.1** will support the development of a regional knowledge platform for sharing best practices, lessons learned, and innovative solutions in GHG measurement, livestock productivity, and climate adaptation.

### Component 2 (Sustainable intensification and cooperative capacity development)
- **Total cost**: USD 238 million; GCF cost: USD 109.7 million
- **Output 2.1 (Stakeholders' and cooperative capacity development strengthened)** aims to improve market access, strengthen dairy cooperatives and promote sustainable practices for smallholder dairy farmers.
- **Output 2.2** will reduce farm-level GHG emissions and enhance productivity through climate-smart breeding, improved feed management, and digital tools.

### Activity 2.2.1 (Breeding)
- Will scale up climate-resilient dairy breeds by scaling up artificial insemination services for smallholder farmers.
- Focuses on disease prevention, improved herd management, and digital tools for animal health monitoring to improve the quality of milk production and certified seed multiplication to improve feed availability.

### Activity 2.2.4 (Feeding)
- Promotes climate-smart feeding practices by improving livestock feed management and nutrient recycling for soil health and emission reduction.
- Will scale up biogasifiers to convert manure into clean energy, reducing deforestation and providing a sustainable source of renewable energy in the dairy sector.

### Activity 2.2.6 (Nutrient Recycling)
- Focuses on increasing milk production and contributing to climate change mitigation by improving nutrient recycling for soil health and emission reduction.


---
**Page 118**
---

19. Activity 2.3 (Pasture and grasslands management improved) aims to improve pasture and grassland management to enhance climate resilience, biodiversity and carbon sequestration while reducing GHG emissions from dairy production. Activity 2.3.1 will strengthen rangeland governance by improving the legal framework for land tenure rights and securing land tenure rights and resolving resource conflicts. Activity 2.3.2 will focus on restoring degraded rangelands through rotational grazing, integration of leguminous trees for carbon sequestration and improved water management practices. Additional activities in this programme will increase water availability for dairy cattle by investing in water harvesting systems, such as micro-dams, rainwater harvesting tanks and irrigation systems. These interventions are to be implemented in extensive dairy systems. These interventions will support sustainable livestock production, reduced greenhouse gas emissions and increased resilience of the dairy value chain.

Component 3: Green Dairy Financing Facility (Total cost: USD 109 million; GCF cost: USD 17.5 million out of USD 12 million grant)

20. Component 3 is designed to provide access to finance for the four target countries through access to finance for dairy value chain actors, including farmers, cooperatives, SMEs and technology providers. By addressing financial and technological barriers, GFF will support the adoption of climate-smart technologies and sustainable dairy practices in the dairy sector. The Green Dairy Loan Facility (GDLF) is structured into two parts: output 3.1 (Effective green financing for dairy value chain actors) and output 3.2 (Green finance for dairy technology providers).

21. Under output 3.1, GFF will offer two loan products to support the adoption of climate-smart technologies and sustainable dairy practices. The Green Dairy Investment Partnership Loan is a three-year term loan with an interest rate of 5% per annum. Loans under this facility have turnover above USD 800,000; loans between USD 250,000 and USD 500,000 with tenor of two years at 6% per annum; and loans below USD 250,000 with a tenor of one year. The Green Dairy Loan Product for Small and Medium Enterprises (SMEs) is a three-year term loan facility with a global loan up to USD 250,000 (average USD 50,000) with a three-year tenure. Both loan types require businesses to demonstrate clear climate-smart investment ambitions and strong sustainability practices. The GFF will provide technical assistance to help dairy value chain businesses to prepare for investment plans and optimize their operations post-financing.

22. Under output 3.2, GFF will offer two loan products to support the capacity of banks and dairy sector businesses in implementing climate-smart projects. The technical assistance facility will address financial and non-financial barriers, ensuring effective green financing for dairy value chain actors. Financial institutions (banks) that are part of the pilot banks will receive training and support to integrate green dairy finance products, improve risk management practices and enhance their capacity to provide green financing. Dairy businesses will receive tailored assistance in business planning, investment readiness, financial management, climate-smart technology adoption, sustainable supply chain management processes; and (iii) regional coordination and knowledge-sharing - best practices, lessons learned and technical assistance for the dairy sector. This initiative aims to scale up climate-smart investments, improve operational efficiency and support sustainable growth in the dairy value chain.

Monitoring and Evaluation: GFF will be monitored by the Green Climate Fund (GCF cost: USD 4 million)

25. Each enacting entity (EE) will manage and coordinate the overall M&E system, leveraging existing project frameworks financed through the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD), the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and other partners. The EE will verify data, producing reports and organizing learning events to enhance sector-wide knowledge. The Learning Steering Committee, supported by rotating national EEIs, will ensure harmonized


---
**Page 119**
---

reporting and methodological guidance. International Livestock Research Institute (ILRI) will provide ongoing technical M&E support, such as templates and methodologies, while data capture and reporting will be handled by country-level project management units (PMUs). ILRI will oversee the process of ensuring quality assurance.

3. A senior coordinator will be recruited at IFAD Kenya Regional Office to oversee GCF coordination, M&E, reporting and knowledge management. During the start-up phase, focus will be on developing a monitoring, evaluation and learning system for GCF, aligned with policy of IFAD, the NEMA PMU and wholesale banks. GCF financing and reporting will be integrated into the overall GMA regional M&E framework.

Project management (total cost: USD 13.9 million; GCF cost: USD 0.649 million)

25. ILRI will serve as the AE for the programme, overseeing its overall management. This includes ensuring that all project activities are aligned with the approved work plan to ensure the achievement of the expected outputs and outcomes, as well as programme closure and final evaluation. The AE will also be responsible for coordinating the implementation of the programme with GCF, coordinating efforts through its headquarters in Rome, the East and Southern Africa Regional Office (ESARO) in Nairobi, Kenya, and the Country Office in Dar es Salaam, Tanzania.

26. Additionally, the Regional Sustainable Livestock Development Programme will provide alignment and value addition, while governments in each country will implement the programme through designated national focal points. The AE and the GCF project management unit will handle coordination. International and national entities, including ministries of agriculture and specialized organizations like ILRI, will offer technical support to national teams.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High
27. The programme will impact 2.5 million direct and 15.4 million indirect beneficiaries across all four countries. As detailed in paragraph 6 above, the programme aims to reduce the milk emissions from dairy production by 80% per ton of milk produced, which is equivalent to a reduction in total GHG emissions on the targeted dairy farms while increasing milk production by 34 percent.

28. The programme is projected to reduce total GHG emissions from milk production by 4.01 tCO2e per year per farm. In Kenya and Tanzania, methane emissions from cattle populations are anticipated to drop by 46 per cent, while methane from manure management is expected to decrease by 57 percent in Ethiopia and 38 percent in Uganda. This will result in a reduction of 54.435 tCO2e per year through improved energy generation and access.

29. The programme will directly contribute to the results of the updated Strategic Plan for the GCF (2016-2020) in three ways: by supporting climate-smart agriculture, local private sector early-stage ventures, micro, small and medium-sized enterprises innovation by supporting resilient market chains and GEF.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High
30. The programme takes a holistic approach to reshaping the dairy sector in East Africa, aiming to lower GHG emissions while enhancing the well-being of dairy producers. By


---
**Page 120**
---

3.3 Sustainable development potential

Scale: High

18. BioMa contributes to several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) by enhancing financial access, increasing productivity, improving food security and nutrition, fostering innovation in climate-smart technologies, promoting sustainable dairy production and supporting the transition of smallholder farmers into more resilient livelihoods.

24. The programme contributes to SDG 3 (Good health and well-being) by promoting safe, high-quality dairy consumption and raising nutrition awareness. Strengthening veterinary services also helps improve animal health, increasing productivity and reducing emissions. Additionally, by ensuring better food safety and quality control, the programme supports the fight against malnutrition.

25. It also supports environmental sustainability by enhancing soil conservation, biodiversity protection, and ecosystem restoration. The project promotes agroforestry, land rehabilitation, and biodiversity conservation, combating climate change and maintaining ecosystems in a sustainable manner (SDG 13). By integrating circular economy principles, improving manure management for biogas production, and enhancing the use of renewable energy sources, it creates green jobs, reducing poverty (SDG 1) and enhancing productivity through climate-smart technologies. It investigates ways to improve access to credit and market access for women, creating a more resilient carbon-free dairy sector while promoting innovation and infrastructure development (SDG 9).

3.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: Medium to high


---
**Page 121**
---

### 3.5 Country ownership

#### Scale: High

The NDCs and other national strategies define each country’s approach to reducing GHG emissions in response to climate change. The proposed programme aligns with these national strategies by incorporating various measures aimed at enhancing climate resilience in the dairy sector, including the promotion of climate-resilient livestock breeds, the development of climate-resilient feedstocks, the introduction and expansion of climate-resilient crops that can withstand extreme weather conditions, the development of climate-resilient livestock feed production systems, the establishment of a national database for monitoring crop and livestock insurance schemes to protect farmers against climate-related risks.

Overall, the programme will provide technical and policy support to the ministries responsible for agriculture and rural development in each country. The programme also includes financial resources and regulations that are critical for the sustainable transformation of the dairy industry. These efforts will help countries achieve their NDCs by enabling them to shift towards low-carbon, climate-resilient dairy production that benefits both the environment and rural communities.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Scale: High

The programme’s economic and financial analyses assess profitability from both a societal and a participant perspective, using the Livestock Sector Investment and Policy Toolkit to model different scenarios for each country. The analysis considers various business models reflecting different production systems, were developed per country based on national herd structures, feedstock availability, and existing infrastructure. These include dairy processing units (all solar-powered) and service providers. The programme’s GHG mitigation impact is estimated at 175 kt CO2e per year.

The overall financial analysis of the programme indicates the profitability of the programme's investments for the farmers and cooperatives. It uses a 17.5 per cent discount rate


---
**Page 122**
---

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

The DaM4A programme is being implemented across four countries in East Africa and is assessed as category B with a moderate environmental and social risk rating. The programme has been screened against IUCN Social, Environmental and Climate Assessment Procedures and the GCF Safeguards. It includes an environmental management framework and stakeholder engagement plan as part of the funding proposal. Distribution of the baseline data to the beneficiary countries is ongoing.

The project will be implemented in close collaboration with the national governments concerned, with the intensification of dairy production, lack of appropriate waste management and child labour associated with small-scale farming being identified as key risks. The project aims to improve pasture productivity and quality by enhancing the composition of forage varieties, fodder collection systems, better pasture management, coupled with low-carbon sources of energy in milk processing.

During the review process, information was limited on the resilient seed variety fodder crops to be used in the project. This will be addressed through a series of workshops for agriculture and livestock for the beneficiary countries and information on envisaged infrastructure development such as silos, cold storage facilities, feed mills, veterinary centres for artificial insemination, water harvesting systems such as micro-dams and cattle sheds. Following the GCF approval process to issue in March 2025 and various review iterations, the project will be implemented with a revised environmental management plan for the programme and the promotion of locally proven fodder varieties to eliminate the potential of introducing invasive species.

#### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and environmental and social safeguard 7 (Indigenous Peoples)

An Indigenous Peoples planning framework is provided as an annex to the environmental management plan. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with the Indigenous Peoples planning framework will not be confined to one or two pastoralist groups and that all indigenous peoples will benefit from the project. An Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AE and EEs. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the AE has confirmed that the Project Board will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

#### Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment

The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy (ESP) includes a new safeguard on sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) in GCF-financed activities. The AE provided SEAH safeguards to the project team as part of the environmental management plan. Given that construction work is likely to be important for the project to ensure there is a comprehensive approach to addressing gender-based violence (GBV), especially construction-related GBV, it will be important for the project to ensure that there is a comprehensive approach to addressing gender-based violence (GBV). The AE has therefore highlighted potential SEAH/GBV risks for all activities, including activity risk assessment in alignment with the GCF SEAH safeguards requirement.

### 4.2 Gender policy

- In line with the GCF Green Climate Policy, the AE prepared and submitted a gender assessment together with gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) action plan.
- The IFAD gender analysis approach, the assessment covers key areas required for gender mainstreaming in the project, including: i) the identification of gender gaps and inequalities targeting criteria and provide sex and gender/social disaggregation targets for its interventions. The GESI action plan will be developed to address these gaps and inequalities. ii) the selection of service providers included in the action plan. These may include youth organizations, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), women’s groups, etc., which are often underrepresented as key during implementation of the GESI action plan as these already have gender focal points.
- In addition to the above, the AE will also ensure that all project activities and outputs and their indicators are gender-sensitive. The GESI action plan will be developed in close collaboration with the GCF and will be procured under GFDRF and technical assistance sub-activities to ensure implementation of the gender action plan.
- Specific technical assistance will include (i) capacity strengthening for the AE, project team members, and service providers to deliver the gender action plan; (ii) the recruitment of experienced service providers to support outreach activities in line with the GESI action plan; and (iii) provision of ongoing GESI technical support during implementation.

##### 4.3.1 Overall project/programme assessment (high risk)

    - IFAD is an international AE with more than 50 years of development work in global agriculture and rural development. IFAD has provided approximately US$24 billion in grants and loans for agricultural and rural development projects in 109 developing countries.

    - Sovereign credit risk: The GCF loan will be extended to the governments of four host countries, which will then provide the funds to commercial banks or microfinance banks and to retail banks or microfinance banks, which will on-lend to small dairy farmers and

**Page 124**

# GCF credit exposure will be to the Governments of Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania with low sovereign credit ratings between B- and B+.

## Programme implementation risk

Within the four target countries, there is limited institutional capacity and skill set in the dairy industry including dairy technicians, social / gender equality awareness, project management skills, and financial management. This may lead to project delays. This is being mitigated by competitive recruitment of qualified project management staff and a robust monitoring system.

## Programme's concessional pricing

To ensure that full benefit of concessional loan pricing under Component 3 is passed on to end borrowers, the facility agreements will include provisions for interest rate floors. The interest rate floor will be set at 5% per annum. This will be reduced by the lowered funding cost from this programme compared to the retail banks' own funding costs.

## Compliance risk (medium risk)

The inherent money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF) risk associated with certain programme activities, such as the launch of a Dairy Green Financing Facility to provide green loans, is relatively elevated.

The AE will implement the programme through its regional office in Kenya, as well as its country offices in the four target countries. The four target country governments will act as EEs for Component 3 and will be responsible for ensuring that the programme complies with relevant financial institutions. The AE's financial management risk assessments of the recipient governments did not lift elevated risks related to ML/TF. The ministries of finance in the target countries have established a dedicated unit within their respective national development or Public Development Banks in each target country to execute green dairy sector financing operations.

## Anti-money laundering/countering the financing of terrorism (AML/CFT) controls

To be implemented by the AE, which is responsible for overall fiduciary management, include training conducted with the four target countries' financial institutions. The AE will also implement counterparty integrity risk testing a risk-based approach, incorporating integrity due diligence measures to identify and mitigate ML/TF risks associated with programme beneficiaries, and procurement countermeasures, to identify ML/TF and other prohibited practices. In addition, the AE will ensure that all counterparties are subject to enhanced due diligence as an additional safeguard against financial management and fiduciary risk. In addition, the four target countries' public development banks will be required to establish a dedicated unit within their respective national development or Public Development Banks in each target country to execute green dairy sector financing operations.

Based on the elevated ML/TF risk posed by some of the proposed programme activities and the various controls and mitigation measures to be applied during project preparation and implementation, the overall AML/CFT risk is determined to be medium.

### GCF portfolio concentration risk

In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal within monitoring thresholds on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of results area, AE concentration and single proposal.

## Recommendation

It is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.

## 4.4 Fiduciary

IFAD serves as the AE and is responsible for overall programme management, including appraisal, fiduciary and technical oversight, supervision and closure, in line with the accreditation requirements of the GCF. The programme will be implemented through its headquarters in Rome, regional office in Nairobi and country offices in Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). Country offices are managed by national EEs in each country, including key ministries, public development banks and agricultural agencies.

Programme coordination units will be established or integrated within existing structures and staff will be recruited through a competitive process. The recruitment of staff for the programme coordination process through a no-object mechanism. The programme coordination units will serve as focal points to manage the implementation of the project in each country.

Component 3 centres on operationalizing GDF, a regional initiative to expand inclusive green finance for climate-smart dairy investments. Each country will implement a dedicated line of credit (LoC) with IFAD and its national EEs. The LoCs will be replenished by IFAD regularly to the programme coordination units and IFAD. These banks will use it to lend retail loans (commercial and microfinance) to dairy farmers in each country. The banks will extend loans to end beneficiaries using their existing frameworks and digital platforms.

Disbursement will follow a revolving fund arrangement, with each country operating separate accounts for disbursements and repayments of the GDF line of credit. Where treasury single account systems are used, GCF funds will be tracked via a dedicated chart of accounts. The banks will submit quarterly progress reports on the status of loan applications, conditional on 70 per cent utilization of prior tranches. Rwanda and Uganda will use integrated financial management systems (IFMS) in line with the International Public Sector Accounting Standards, while Kenya and the United Republic of Tanzania will apply consolidated financial statements with GCF-specific coding. Projects will be required to consolidate financial results quarterly and submit them to IFAD. Internal controls will be enforced through national audit systems, with Rwanda using a project-specific auditor for each project. Annual external independent performance and financial audits will be conducted by each country’s Auditor General, with specific audit opinions on GCF funds due within six months of fiscal year-end.

## 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

The HuMa funding proposal presents a well-structured results architecture anchored in a robust theory of change that is designed to deliver transformative impact in the East African dairy sector through a combination of mitigation and adaptation measures across Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and Tanzania. The proposed project activities are clearly articulated and supported by a coherent results framework that links activities and outputs to expected outcomes and long-term impact, in line with the GCF Integrated Results Management Framework.

## GCF.R42/02/MAR/17

### Section 59
The Board is recommended to consider whether disbursements of GCF proceeds should only be made after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in those countries, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities by the host country.

### Section 60
With regard to the activities being financed by the GCF Loans, the Accredited Entity has proposed that it will enter into a financing agreement with each host country, acting through its ministry of finance (MoF), in accordance with the terms of the MoU. The MoU will be executed on behalf of the wholesale bank in that host country under the terms of an on-lending agreement between MoF and the wholesale bank. Under this arrangement, the wholesale bank will act as the executing entity for each host country, while the Accredited Entity will not have a direct contractual relationship with each wholesale bank. The MoU will be subject to the same conditions and requirements for implementation as the AMA and FAA. For the Accredited Entity to pass down and enforce the relevant requirements of the AMA and FAA to the executing entities, the terms sheet has agreed that the executing entity will ensure that each MoU passes down monitors, supervisors, and other mechanisms required by the AMA and FAA. The terms sheet also allows the Accredited Entity to comply with the AMA and FAA requirements as if it had a direct contractual relationship with the executing entities.

### Section 61
Since the Accredited Entity's direct counterparty is a sovereign state (as one of the executing entities), the Secretariat has agreed to disapply the provisions of the AMA allowing GCF to pass down and enforce the relevant requirements of the AMA and FAA. As such, Le each MoF as, due to the sovereign nature of the counterparty and the contract, it is not feasible for the Accredited Entity to pass down and enforce the relevant requirements of the AMA and FAA relationship with the executing entities under subsidiary agreements. Accordingly, and similar to the arrangements in PT142, PT162, PT908, PF236, SAV122 and PT157 as a condition for obtaining the FAA, the Secretariat has agreed that the MoU will be signed by the National Designated Authority (NDA) of each host country of this disapplication and its implications. An express reference to this agreement is required in the NDA.

### Section 62
To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Submission of a certificate or legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, within 120 days after Board approval, confirming that the host country has obtained all final internal approvals needed by it and has the capacity and authority to implement the proposed programme.

(b) Signature of the FAA in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within this time period. The Secretariat will also require a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and

(c) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s review of FP269

## Proposal name: Dairy Interventions for Mitigation and Adaptation (DiMA)

### Accredited entity: International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)

#### Countries:
- Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania

#### Project/programme size: Large

## I. Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

1. This funding proposal is for a large public sector cross-cutting programme in environmental and social subcategories category B as submitted by the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) as accredited entity (AE). The Dairy Interventions for Mitigation and Adaptation (DiMA) project will be implemented across four contiguous East African countries: Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania (see figure 1 below). The total funding proposal is US$208.56 million. This amount is proposed as GCF grant finance and USD 208.26 million as co-finance from IFAD and two of the governments (see section I.7 below).

   - The programme has three entities (EIs) for the implementation of components 1 and 2 are as follows: in Kenya, the Government of Kenya through its National Treasury, the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries; in Rwanda, the Government of Rwanda through its Ministry of Finance and Economic Planning, Rwanda Agriculture Board within the Ministry of Agriculture and Animal Resources and Development; in Uganda, the Government of Uganda through its Ministry of Finance, Planning and Economic Development, the Ministry of Agriculture, Animal Industry and Fisheries, and the National Dairy Development Board; and in Tanzania, the Government of the United Republic of Tanzania through its Ministry of Finance, the Ministry of Livestock and Fisheries, and the National Dairy Development Board. The third implementing entity is identified for component 3 in the funding proposal, section A.20, but this is understood to be IFAD.

   - The aim of the proposed programme is: “to reduce methane and other GHG emissions from the dairy sector while increasing the resilience of livestock-dependent communities. The programme will support a shift from traditional dairy production systems towards more sustainable and climate-resilient ‘UC’ (nationally determined contribution)-aligned practices that reduce emissions at farm and farm-level, increase productivity and income for smallholder farmers, improve milk collection, decentralize processing with renewable energies, formalize dairy value chains, and promote the development of new markets. The programme will also support initiatives that promote circularity, and increase rangelands ecosystems resilience and services (including carbon sequestration) in grasslands” (see grant proposal, para 2).

5. The programme’s interventions, geographically targeted as shown in figure 1 below, will address climate change adaptation and mitigation issues across the varied dairy production systems of the four countries involved.

   - This assessment is based on the full and final funding proposal package received by the ITAP on 24 March 2015.

# Impact potential

The programme will target two of the GCF results areas for adaptation, aiming to enhance the resilience of (i) most vulnerable people and communities (adaptation result area [ARA] 1); and (ii) dairy production systems (ARA 2). The programme also aims at enhancing dairy productivity and production, which is a key component in achieving resilient and sustainable dairy production. This will be achieved through value-chain linkages and improved productivity.

## Scale: Medium to high

The programme will target two of the GCF results areas for mitigation, aiming during the project period to reduce emissions from dairy farming (mitigation result area [MRA] 1); and (ii) forests and land use (MRA4), which includes livestock farming, both by reducing emissions from dairy farming, particularly release of methane; and by increasing the capacity of forests and other land uses to sequester carbon.

### Climate-change risk and impacts

The funding proposal highlights the climate context of the four countries, and undertakes a systematic analysis of climatic hazards. This covers observed changes in temperature (increasing annual average maximum temperature), declining volume of precipitation, increased annual average maximum temperature, declining relative humidity, and increasing frequency of dry days, very wet days, and very warm days. Future projections on rainfall are uncertain but suggest that the coastal areas may experience less rain than at present; increased frequency of dry days, very wet days, and very warm days. Future projections on rainfall are uncertain but suggest that the coastal areas may experience less rain than at present; increased frequency of dry days, very wet days, and very warm days. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) notes that the All chose not to include analysis of duration hot days, given the complexity and variety of definitions of a heatwave, and the difficulty in attributing such events to climate change. This is reflected in declines in animal health, milk production, fertility, and calving complications.

The funding proposal demonstrates how these observed and projected changes in climate can impact on dairy farming systems through increased frequency of droughts and floods, and increased vulnerability to zoonotic diseases (compounding direct anthropogenic impacts). The programme will address the risks associated with this by strengthening agroecosystems on which it depends to these climate change and other challenges, with limited access to quality inputs, limited water availability, frequent disease outbreaks, inadequate


---
**Page 131**

### The Green Climate Fund

#### Adaptation Impact Potential

The intended adaptation impact of the programme is not stated clearly in section B.1, which speaks broadly about efficiency, productivity and resilience of dairy systems. Instead, the proposal provides a clear analysis of how specific exposure and vulnerability discussed above will be addressed through various interventions. These interventions are designed to reduce vulnerability to particular climate hazards scattered throughout the funding proposal. The EAP would have expected to see a clearer statement in the funding proposal regarding how these interventions will build resilience against drought - increasing vegetation cover and enhancing water infiltration, as well as setting aside fodder reserves.

Many of the proposed interventions related to increased heat, humidity or combined heat and humidity, are not fully covered in the funding proposal, and are mentioned only once in response to a question from the AE about this. The AE indicated its intention to include a range of measures:

- To attenuate heat stress effect on animal's reproduction and production parameters (e.g., by providing shade, cooling systems, etc.)
- To reduce the exposure of livestock to heat stress / solar radiation in grazing fields; water harvesting and recycling for cattle watering; crossbreeding with heat-tolerant disease-resistant


---
**Page 132**
---

The ITAP recommends that this aspect be brought out strongly in the project implementation manual to be developed, with practical recommendations for management protocols and monitoring.

Overall the programme aspires to directly benefit about 2.5 million individual dairy farmers and dairy value chain actors in 94 districts/counties that are vulnerable to climate change. This includes 103,786 households (HHs) in Kenya; 1,000 HHs in Rwanda; 1,000 HHs in Uganda; and 758 HHs in the United Republic of Tanzania. Of the beneficiaries, at least 40 per cent will be women and 20 per cent will have a disability (including those with visual impairment or physical disabilities). An estimated 13.4 million indirect beneficiaries will benefit from strengthened veterinary services, improved access to credit, increased livestock productivity through better animal health extension and breeding services, and improved access to climate information. A total of about 2.7 million hectares of land in the project area is expected to be under improved management. About 50 per cent of existing natural pastures will be brought under improved management. The funding proposal shows the following combination of criteria used to select intervention areas: (i) climate-risk hotspots; (ii) absolute vulnerability to climate change; and (iii) livestock production potential. These are further divided into pastoralist, agropastoralist, mixed farming, and rangeland-based livelihoods.

Mitigation results from the programme interventions will be achieved through total mitigation results of 2,619,458 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2e) over a 20-year lifespan. This results from a combination of emissions reductions (from the introduction of renewable-energy solutions and improved livestock management practices) and avoided emissions (through the rehabilitation of pastures and rangelands).

Emission reduction results were calculated using the best practice Global Livestock Environmental Assessment Model interactive (GLEAM-i) developed by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO). GLEAM-i uses an embedded herd dynamic model to estimate livestock production, emissions from enteric methane and manure, based on digestibility and nitrogen content. Indirect emissions from the use of energy for feed production are also included in this calculation. Using this methodology, milk production is projected to increase by 34 per cent (through producing more milk per cow) while reducing the volume of methane generated per kg of milk by a significant 29 per cent and reducing the total feed consumption per litre of milk by 10 per cent compared with the without project scenario.

The reproduction calendar, performance recording using technology, an enhanced female-to-male ratio through sexed semen and reduction/cull of subproductive animals, as well as production efficiencies (through breeding, feeding and animal health), and interventions to strengthen herd management practices are all aimed at increasing the quantity of milk from livestock to the milk.* The ITAP recommends tracking herd sizes (as a combined result of births, deaths and acquisitions of new livestock) during project implementation.

A key challenge in planning for climate change mitigation and adaptation, is the uncertainty around how climate-change impacts will evolve during the programme period. It should be noted that the projected increase in average annual temperatures resulting from the combined effect of high temperature and high humidity may turn out to be even higher than projected. It is well-documented that high TL conditions lead to higher mortality rates among dairy cows, which can have a significant impact on herd size. 3. Monitoring and reporting framework (MRF) for the project as set out in annex 22 to the funding proposal for rangeland restoration interventions, with clear indications of responsibilities for site-level ground-truthing and data collection: (i) GPS coordinates of each site; (ii) a list of key indicators of land degradation and rehabilitation practices were implemented; (iii) surveys on species composition and improved biomass yield; (iv) the implementation of agroforestry, intercropping, mixed farming, sustainable pastoral grazing, agropastoral, improved pasture management, management of grazing intensity, etc.; and (v) the use of remote sensing analysis through high-resolution satellite images (using, for example, the Sentinel-2 satellite imagery) to assess changes in vegetation cover under restoration. On the mitigation side, a system is also in place for measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) of soil carbon sequestration in areas under restoration, including modelling for estimation of soil organic carbon stocks and other indicators such as soil moisture, carbon stocks, soil texture, carbon inputs from vegetation and manure, as well as climate [all collected through the use of remote sensing analysis]. The system will also include a baseline and annual results. The team will also leverage data from the national MRV systems enhanced under component 1.3.1.2 (e.g., with flux towers such as eddy covariance towers – see feasibility study for more details).

Overall, the impact potential is rated as medium to high.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

Paradigm shift relates to the degree to which the proposed programme can successfully catalyse a transformation in the dairy sector investment of US$ 150 million over five years with complementary IFAD projects. The programme, which will work with approximately 6.5 per cent of the total dairy herd in Tanzania and 23 per cent of the total dairy herd in Kenya and 14 per cent in the United Republic of Tanzania, aims to facilitate the transition from a dairy sector characterised by rising emissions, low production efficiency, market instability and climate vulnerability to one that is more resilient. The programme will focus on improving soil carbon, climate-resilient sector. By improving policy, institutional and service-delivery arrangements, it seeks to transform the dairy sector in Tanzania into an efficient, sustainable and climate-smart solutions for smallholder dairy farmers, the programme seeks to transform all aspects of the dairy value chain.

* Written questions from the ITAP with IFAD responses, 11 April 2025.


---
**Page 135**
---

29. **Enabling environment for scaling up:** Component 1 of the programme focuses on creating a climate response policy and regulatory environment in the four countries, through work to review and formulate dairy-sector policies, strategies and regulations. Awareness campaigns will be conducted with stakeholders at all levels.

The programme also seeks to improve land-tenure security and management for sustainable dairy farming. Public-sector capacity for livestock support will be increased, enhancing veterinary services and animal health systems in the four countries. The programme will strengthen extension services as well. At a regional scale, GCF financing supports the transformation of the dairy sector through the establishment of national dairy development strategies and action plans; creation of centres of excellence; cross-country cooperation; and knowledge exchange. MIV systems using computerized information management tools will be established in all four countries for insurance assessments; and provision of finance through the Green Dairy Financing Facility (GDFF).

30. **Focus on national MVP:** The programme has the potential to make an important contribution towards achieving the objectives of the GCF, including strengthening institutions, for both climate risk and vulnerability assessment, improving greenhouse gas emissions accounting and reporting systems, and supporting the development of SMEs. The programme also aims to facilitate the development of a carbon certification system for the dairy sector, and reduce barriers to technology dissemination and strengthening dairy value chains.

In response to a question from IFAD, the programme will address the issue of benefit-sharing of potential proceeds from the sale of carbon credits in the future, in response to a question from IFAD. To ensure that smallholders have access to fair and inclusive access to carbon finance under pilot Activity 3.4, the Programme will support this by establishing transparent mechanisms for beneficiary identification, benefit-sharing, and participation in decision-making.

31. **Retail bank uptake:** The intention of the programme is to de-risk private investment and accelerate the adoption of low-emission climate-smart technologies and solutions by smallholder farmers through a combination of public-private partnerships (PPPs), catalytic financing engagement and scaling up climate finance flows. The programme is based on a thorough dairy market analysis, which has identified that there are currently significant financial barriers to technology dissemination and strengthening dairy value chains.

At the same time, there is a need for banks to be more involved in providing credit to smallholder farmers than was anticipated, limiting the partners shifted ahead in financial markets. In the experience of the TAP, it took several years before banks were willing to lend to smallholders, with loans only drawn upon, because the banks fail to actively engage new customers in unfamiliar market sectors. The programme will seek to address this by providing a risk-sharing mechanism that is essential to broaden their lending portfolio to include higher-risk clients or enterprises with new business models. Risk participation agreements, which share a portion of credit losses between a concessionary fund and the bank, are one such mechanism.

32. **Risk sharing:** The programme will establish a risk-sharing mechanism that will work with the wholesale and retail banks as well as the financial sector more broadly (including long grace and repayment periods), but it is unclear what the programme can/will do to incentivize these retail banks to take on this risk, given the current low levels of lending in the dairy sector. The inclusion of Selected Risk Factor 8 in the funding proposal, which acknowledges that "[establishing four reliable financial institutions] will be challenging and may require a phased approach," is appropriate.

Relatively low funding base can constrain the interest of banks to participate in the scheme. Lack of experience with smallholder farmers and dairy farming in particular could also reduce bank interest. In response to a request to the AE to explain the approach to mitigating this risk as it moves forward, the programme will seek to engage with the AE to provide further evidence or proven success concerning the level and quality of the GDFP pilot/implementation data.

# Sustainable development potential

## Social and health co-benefits.

The programme will promote improved food safety and nutrition through a range of interventions including formalization, awareness campaigns, and product traceability to improve nutrition and health awareness, with a focus on the increased consumption of fruits and vegetables (SDG 2: Zero hunger; SDG 3: Good health and well-being). The programme contributes to ending hunger, achieving food security and improving children's nutritional status (SDG 2: Zero hunger). In addition, by strengthening the capacity of smallholder farmers to adopt climate-smart agricultural practices that lead to higher milk productivity and reduced losses, as well as decreased GHG emissions and increased farmers' income, it contributes to ending poverty (Sustainable Development Goal SDG 1: No poverty) and animal health and welfare (SDG 12: Responsible consumption and production).

## Gender and social inclusion co-benefits.

The programme includes interventions targeting the involvement of women, youth and marginalized groups (see paragraph 5.13 of this assessment). Project activities that promote gender equality in agriculture (e.g., involving women and girls/youth) play a rearing cattle, milk cows and producing dairy products in households and small businesses. The social and economic impacts of these interventions are analyzed in annex B (Gender assessment and action plan). The programme aims to reduce the gender gap by increasing women's participation in livestock farming activities, their participation in livestock farmer field schools, and opening spaces for women and youth in traditionally male-dominated roles – including milk aggregation, animal health services, manure management, and marketing. The Gender Equality Assessment Tool (GEAT) and the Gender System Plan will be used - a participatory tool that engages households and communities in reflection on gender equality issues and promotes gender-sensitive planning and decision-making by reducing socially acceptable roles through gender change and peer influence.

## Environmental co-benefits.

The programme's interventions to restore and better manage degraded lands, coupled with climate-smart agriculture and mitigation results, but will also produce environmental co-benefits - such as an increase in biodiversity, with native species being restored. The use of indigenous grasses and legumes in combination with exotic grasses may be used more extensively in humid and semi-humid grasslands. In the United Republic of Tanzania, for example, farmers will be allowed to regenerate in arid and semi-arid grasslands/regions in Kenya and the United Republic of Tanzania. The programme's interventions are designed to follow circular economy principles, improving resource management for biogas production and nutrient cycling, which will contribute to soil health (SDG 12: Responsible consumption and production) and climate change mitigation that is aligned with SDG 13 on Climate Action.

*Written questions from the ITAP with IFAD responses. 11 April 2025.*

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

- Three of the four LaRMA countries are classified as least developed countries<sup>25</sup>, and all of them are recognized as being highly vulnerable to climate change. Observed and projected climate-change scenarios for the region indicate significant changes in precipitation and temperature, with increased frequency of extreme weather events such as droughts and other extreme weather events. The effects will negatively impact the dairy sector, resulting in:

# Substantial impacts on dairy livestock and loss of productivity of pastures and rangelands

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) emission inventories for Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda, and the United Republic of Tanzania highlight that smallholder farmers and pastoralists dominate the GEF emission inventories. The expected increase in milk yields per cow is projected to be 10% by 2030. However, intensities will continue to rise if no action is taken to reduce emission intensity per litre of milk produced, and to enhance productivity such that yields can increase without growing herd sizes.

The urgency for climate change adaptation and mitigation in the dairy sector is highlighted in national policies and plans, more particularly in the countries' NDCs. As pointed out by the GCF, there are several opportunities for adaptation and mitigation that recognize "efficient livestock systems" as a feasible adaptation option that has synergy with mitigation.

### 1.5 Country ownership

The Governments of Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda, and the United Republic of Tanzania are fully supportive of the proposed GEF programmes to strengthen to improve resilience to climate change in dairy production and value chains and to reduce methane and other GHG emissions from livestock systems. These countries have multiple benefits for the environment, nutrition, livelihoods and social inclusion. Partners supporting the programme design and development process include IFAD, FAO, the Global Dairy Platform, and the CGIAR Research Program on Climate Change, Agriculture and Food Security (CCAFS).

According to annex 10 to the funding proposal, the programme was initially conceptualized as a project under the GEF Small Grants Programme (SGP). This project idea and the concept of net zero itself, however, were perceived as too complex, unclear and difficult to understand by many government and development partners. In addition, country ownership is an important principle in the SGP. The programme was therefore adapted for smallholder farmers and pastoralists and their adaptation to climate change, leading to the shift to a cross-cutting project under the GEF Small Grants Programme (SGP).

The word "daima" means "always" or "forever" in Swahili, a language spoken in all four countries, highlighting the importance of sustainability and a regional identity to the programme.


---
**Page 139**
---

### Alignment with policy

The ItaMa programme aligns with the national strategies of the participating countries by supporting and complementing existing plans, policies and strategies related to agriculture, livestock and climate change.

In Kenya, the proposed programme will be aligned with the National Climate Change Action Plan (2016–2027), the National Climate Change Action Plan, Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture Implementation Framework (2018-2027), and the Kenya National Strategy on Climate Change and Low Carbon Development. In Rwanda, it aligns with the National Climate Change Strategy and Investment Plan, National Adaptation Plan, and National Climate Change Strategy.

### Engagement with stakeholders

The stakeholder engagement plan (annex 7 to the funding proposal) provides a record of an extensive consultation process over the course of 2021 and early 2022. The programme has been designed in close collaboration with key stakeholders involved in all stages of programme initiation, conceptualization and preparation.

In each country, national steering committees (NSCs), comprising representatives from ministries of agriculture/livestock and dairy boards) were established in 2023, at the regional level, a regional steering committee was established in July 2023. Regional workshops (July and September 2023), stakeholder engagement meetings were organized to involve a wide array of national and regional public institutions, private sector entities, civil society organizations, and other key stakeholders and partners in designing the programme.

### Capacity of accredited and executing entities

As AE for the programme, ItaMa will be responsible for ensuring that all implementing entities are accredited by ItaMa with 11 other GCF projects/programmes under implementation. This role includes programme approval, project design, project preparation, project implementation, reporting to the GCF, and overseeing final evaluation, engaging ItaMa headquarters in Rome and regional office in Nairobi, as well as its country offices in Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania.

The implementing entities will be accredited by ItaMa (senior civil servants) from the EEA. 12 government entities listed in paragraph 2 above - will remain responsible for implementation at national level. The programme will work with each country overseeing implementation: international and national entities, including ministries of agriculture/livestock, dairy boards, and other relevant public institutions; and regional implementation teams, with preference given to those that have both proven capacity and regional presence.

### Component 1

Component 1's work on policies, awareness and vulnerability assessment will be implemented by the relevant EEs in each country, through a wide range of additional government entities. The programme will also engage with civil society organizations which the EEIs will have oversight responsibility, which will include local governments, national government institutions, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), international organizations, and other non-governmental organizations, ILOs, FAO and private-sector entities.

The same applies to work in component 2 on cooperative strengthening, promoting renewable energy and waste management, and improving the resilience of smallholder farmers and livestock producers. The programme will build on the fiduciary capacity of the EEI in each of the four countries.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Medium

With a total budget for the proposed DaIMA programme of USD 358.26 million, IFAD is seeking USD 150 million from GCF—67 percent of the overall budget. IFAD is investing a total of USD 94.5 million in concessional loans to producers for renewable energy-driven productivity enhancements, with a total of USD 45 million in grants and USD 37.5 million in tenure fees over four existing IFAD-to-government loan programmes: in Rwanda—the Rwanda Dairy Development Project, phase 2, in the Philippines—The Philippine Dairy Sector Support Project, in Ethiopia—the Green Climate Fund (GCF)-funded project in Kenya – The Integrated Natural Resource and Environmental Management Project, and in Uganda—the GCF-funded project in Uganda.

The proposed financial structure for the DaIMA programme includes slightly concessional loans to dairy producers (12 per cent of GCF investment), 63 per cent of the total grant amount, and 25 per cent of the total tenure fees. The loan facility is a maximum of USD 88 million (per cent of the proposed GCF investment, 37 per cent of total programme). Maximum concessional loans are for renewable energy-driven productivity enhancements enabling environment and national capacities for low-emission climate-resilient dairy sectors, and also for capacity building in the value chain, including training, advisory services, and grants.

Loan facility: In addition to the four complementary IFAD-government loans serving as co-finance to the GCF investment, a programme-wide investment facility is being set up through commercial banks or public development banks (PDBs) within their respective countries. Following this, the wholesale banks provide local currency wholesale loans to retail banks operating at the national or local level. The wholesale banks will be responsible for repaying the full loan amounts to the retail banks will operate. An annual survey of market interest rates will help to define appropriate interest rates that will be applied by the wholesale banks. The retail banks will operate. The entire amount revolves across the IFAD-financing unit until the loans in USD 175 million are fully repaid. The wholesale banks will be responsible for repaying the full loan amounts to the retail banks, regardless of whether the retail banks have been able to repay the full loan amounts to the Ministries of Finance for repayment back to IFAD and GCF.

Figure 2: Green Dairy Financing Facility financing chain (from funding proposal, para. 144).

### Abbreviations: EE = executing entity; IFAD = International Fund for Agricultural Development; LCV = local currency value; SME = small and medium enterprise; SMF = social mobilization fund

The programme thus includes a complex on-lending structure across four steps (1) from GCF to the IFAD, 2) from IFAD to wholesale banks in EE, 3) from wholesale banks to participating retail banks, and 4) from retail banks to borrowers. The IAP acknowledges the value of the host governments taking the hard currency risk and thus enabling on-lending in local currencies. However, this is a complex structure that could be difficult to implement, potentially undermining the consensual nature of GCF funds provided, and seems likely to make coordination with other donors (who may have different conditions for their funding implemented) very challenging.

## Economic analysis

The ex-ante financial and economic viability of the programme is demonstrated through an independent financial analysis conducted by the GEF ( Annex 3 to the funding proposal). Economic performance indicators for the four countries individually confirm the economic feasibility of the project. The project has a positive net present value (NPV) at the countries' incremental net benefit streams, discounted at a rate of return of 12 per cent. At this rate of return, the NPV is US$305 million with an economic rate of return (ERR) of 25 per cent for the baseline scenario. Two additional economic performance indicators are also used to assess the project's financial and economic balance with a low vs a high carbon market price assumption: Under the low scenario, the ERR is 17.9 per cent at US$305 million; under the high scenario, the ERR is 30.3 per cent and the NPV is US$254.6 million. The economic analysis concludes that the GEF/IFAD project represents a robust economically worthwhile investment.

## Financial viability

The financial viability of the project was assessed from the perspective of direct participants. The Livestock Sector Policy Toolkit was used to develop dairy production models in all four countries, using five different herd types: (i) large-scale intensive system/cow grazing with exotic breeds; (ii) small-scale intensive system/cow grazing with exotic breeds; (iii) semi-intensive with cross-bred cows and local breeds; (iv) semi-intensive with purebred cows and local breeds. Combinations of these herd models with likely investments through dairy farmers, milk collection companies, and wholesale banks were used to assess the financial viability.

The financial analysis used the financial discount rate for each of the four countries (i.e., the rate at which commercial banks can borrow from the central bank): 11.25 per cent in Kenya.


---
**Page 142**
---

6.5 per cent for Rwanda, 9.3% per cent in Uganda and 5.4 per cent in the United Republic of Tanzania. The analysis indicates that the financial internal rate of return (FIRR) is above the opportunity cost of capital in all four countries and thus that the proposed investments can be profitable. The FIRR for Rwanda is 19%, with an NPV of USD 172.82 million; for Uganda, an FIRR of 19 per cent and NPV of USD 41.62 million; for Tanzania, an FIRR of 17 per cent and NPV of USD 47.40 million; and for the United Republic of Tanzania, an FIRR of 15% and NPV of USD 38.90 million. The financial internal rate of return is also calculated for the proposed programme as a whole, using an average discount rate of 8.2 per cent over the period 2014-2026, with an FIRR of 15% and NPV of USD 37.90 million.

Mitigation cost-effectiveness: The estimated cost per tonne of carbon dioxide equivalent for the programme, defined as total investment cost/expected lifetime emission reduction (LTER) in tonnes of CO2e, is USD 48 per tonne of CO2e avoided (including existing IFAD loan programmes), and USD 10 per tonne when considering only the 50 per cent of emissions that are directly associated with the proposed mitigation results (see funding proposal, section A.4).

Given the high unit cost of the mitigation results and the complexity of the on-lending structure that reduces financial efficiency, efficiency and effectiveness are rated as medium.

## Overall remarks from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

This proposed faMaS programme is expected to make a significant contribution to helping countries in sub-Saharan Africa (SSA) achieve their national development goals by enabling them to reduce their GHG emissions and enhance carbon removals, while simultaneously building resilience against climate change impacts. The programme will also contribute to enhancing the robustness of adaptation actions related to heat stress.

To enhance the robustness of adaptation actions related to heat stress, the ITAP recommends that IFAD include, during the inception phase and early implementation, systematically integrate or pilot a range of interventions that are likely to be effective in improving livestock adaptation monitoring frameworks. This will refine the targeting of livestock heat stress interventions (e.g., housing, shade, water management, breed selection) across intervention areas.

The ITAP further recommends that IFAD include in the programme implementation manual:

(a) A clear statement of the intended adaptation impact of the project – in reducing vulnerability or exposure, or enhancing adaptive capacity, in relation to key climate change impacts (e.g., increased temperatures and humidity; increased development of pathogens, diseases, parasite and vectors in dairy cattle; (iv) decreased availability of feed resources for livestock; (v) increased heat stress in livestock; (vi) a decrease in the quantity and quality of pasture; and (vii) deterioration of fodder in unsupervised grazing areas).

(b) A detailed section on measures to be implemented for heat stress reduction in cattle, taking into account the combined effects of temperature and humidity, including both the breeding of heat-tolerant breeds and crossbreeding with heat-tolerant breeds, and also a management protocol for very hot days involving shade, water access, feeding, and spraying. This could include grazing cattle under a shade roof with solar-powered fans, and spraying cattle with water.

An addition to the project's monitoring and evaluation plan to track herd size (including dairy and beef) over time will provide valuable information on the programme's implementation, in order to test the assumption on which the programme's mitigation


---
**Page 143**
---

results depend (i.e., that biodiversity will remain stable), and to put in place adaptive management measures where growth trends are emerging.

The IAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.
