# INTRODUCTION

This annex sets out the main parameters of the Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) assessment and action plan for implementation of DuMAYA interventions in Tanzania, Kenya, Rwanda and Uganda. Part I provides a contextual GESI analysis of dairy value chains for dairy farmers and MSMEs (dairy cooperatives and processors) in each respective country. Identifying the key challenges for gender equality and women empowerment, youth employment, nutrition, marginalization of smallholder dairy producers and access to finance, it proposes interventions through associated services enterprises and proposes potential interventions. Part 2 provides the gender action plan setting out proposed actions to achieve the Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) targets. These will be further reviewed and revised during implementation.

The DuMAYA theory of change is that 10 technological solutions to reduce methane and GHG emissions and financial services to efficiently sustain production and marketing processes, which increase revenue and/or reduce production costs, are supported for dairy farmers and milk processors to adopt in East Africa. These farm-level methane emissions and supply chain emission reductions will be achieved through the following key objectives: (1) to make these solutions be economically attractive to farmers, pastoralists and dairy sector processors; The key objectives of DuMAYA are: (a) To strengthen systemic and institutional capacities of the livestock sector to enable smallholder dairy producers and local value chains to adopt low-emission technologies and practices; (b) To provide access to finance and other tools for smallholder dairy producers and private sector value chain operators for a low-emission, climate-resilient, and sustainable pathway; (c) To ensure that the benefits of these solutions are shared equitably among stakeholders with access to required financial services while making the necessary transition to low-carbon, climate-resilient, and sustainable livestock production and value chain development.

## PART I: GESI ANALYSIS

Agriculture plays a significant role in the economy, providing employment and income to millions of people. As a result, the impact of rapid changes on agriculture may have long-reaching consequences that extend well beyond individual farmers and their families. According to the United Nations, this output is mostly generated by family farms, where women supply around 60 to 80 percent of the labor input. East Africa has demonstrated the positive effects of dairy production in terms of poverty reduction and gender equality. The region has a strong policy environment with international frameworks and progressive national legal and policy frameworks promoting gender equality and women empowerment. However, women in agriculture face gender-specific barriers, including limited access to land, financing, markets, technology, extension services, and decision-making power.

In Rwanda, maternal and child health has improved significantly over the last two decades and exceeded Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) ambitions. Maternal mortality reduced by 80% between 2000 and 2014 while infant and child-mortality decreased by over 70% in the same period. *Free universal basic education was initiated and scaled up


---
**Page 150**
---

to 12 years. This resulted in a net enrolment of nearly 100% in primary school for both boys and girls. Gender parity was achieved with more girls than boys in primary school.## Uganda

Women outnumber men (52% compared to 47%, as per the 2012 population census) in the rural population working in agriculture. About two-thirds of all households are headed by women and working age youth (ages 15–
34) constitute 27% of the rural population. Women account for only 24% of the agro-dealers in Rwanda. Women and men farmers in dual households are characterized by unequal power relations, and women have limited decision-
making authority over resources and income. In many cases, women are responsible for household chores and care for children and elderly family members. In mixed farming systems, such as those found in the GINRMA program, women are responsible for feeding the animals, cleaning the stalls and milking utensils, while men are more involved in the milking of cows as well as the transport and sale of milk. These social and gender dynamics relate to the fact that women have less access to resources than men do. In some cases, this may reduce the effectiveness of such programs, or worse, increase gender inequalities within the household. Such cases may occur, for instance, when men take over decision-making and control of income from a livestock product, such as dairy, that has been produced primarily following the intervention. Additionally, women in agriculture are more vulnerable to climate change and land degradation because they generally do not have other livelihood options to support their families.

### Women's Empowerment

The Government of Uganda has identified gender equality and women’s empowerment as critical to achieving sustainable development. The government has made significant progress towards this goal through policies and commitments and has put in place a legal framework to promote Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment (GEWE). The GEWE Act, 2013, provides for the establishment of a National Gender Equality Unit within the Ministry of Finance, Planning and Economic Development. The National Gender Equality Unit is mandated with the responsibility of monitoring and evaluating progress towards gender equality in Uganda. The government has also established the Gender Equality Commission (GEC) to monitor and evaluate progress on GEWE. The GEC provides an annual report on the state of gender equality in the country, and reports to Parliament.

### Women's Participation

The proportion of women in parliament rose from 9% in 1996 to 32.8% in 2003 but decreased to 14.4% in 2006. However, most women in government are in lower-level positions and have limited access to resources such as land, credit, and information. In many cases, women do not hold decision-making positions within their households or communities.

### Women's Role in Agriculture

Women constitute 52 percent of the population in Tanzania. Seventy-five percent of these women live in rural areas. Of these,
80.4 per cent work in the agriculture sector; 0.1 per cent in the missing sector and 2.7 per cent in small and medium enterprises (SMEs). 44.1 per cent of women in the urban centres work in SMEs and mainly sell clothes, food and non-
## Tanzania

### Women's Participation in Agriculture

Women outnumber men (52% compared to 47%, as per the 2012 population census) in the rural population working in agriculture. About two-thirds of all households are headed by women and working age youth (ages 15–
34) constitute 27% of the rural population. Women account for only 24% of the agro-dealers in Rwanda. Women and men farmers in dual households are characterized by unequal power relations, and women have limited decision-
making authority over resources and income. In many cases, women are responsible for household chores and care for children and elderly family members. In mixed farming systems, such as those found in the GINRMA program, women are responsible for feeding the animals, cleaning the stalls and milking utensils, while men are more involved in the milking of cows as well as the transport and sale of milk. These social and gender dynamics relate to the fact that women have less access to resources than men do. In some cases, this may reduce the effectiveness of such programs, or worse, increase gender inequalities within the household. Such cases may occur, for instance, when men take over decision-making and control of income from a livestock product, such as dairy, that has been produced primarily following the intervention. Additionally, women in agriculture are more vulnerable to climate change and land degradation because they generally do not have other livelihood options to support their families.

### Women's Empowerment

The Government of Uganda has identified gender equality and women’s empowerment as critical to achieving sustainable development. The government has made significant progress towards this goal through policies and commitments and has put in place a legal framework to promote Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment (GEWE). The GEWE Act, 2013, provides for the establishment of a National Gender Equality Unit within the Ministry of Finance, Planning and Economic Development. The National Gender Equality Unit is mandated with the responsibility of monitoring and evaluating progress towards gender equality in Uganda. The government has also established the Gender Equality Commission (GEC) to monitor and evaluate progress on GEWE. The GEC provides an annual report on the state of gender equality in the country, and reports to Parliament.

### Women's Participation

The proportion of women in parliament rose from 9% in 1996 to 32.8% in 2003 but decreased to 14.4% in 2006. However, most women in government are in lower-level positions and have limited access to resources such as land, credit, and information. In many cases, women do not hold decision-making positions within their households or communities.

### Women's Role in Agriculture

Women constitute 52 percent of the population in Tanzania. Seventy-five percent of these women live in rural areas. Of these,
80.4 per cent work in the agriculture sector; 0.1 per cent in the missing sector and 2.7 per cent in small and medium enterprises (SMEs). 44.1 per cent of women in the urban centres work in SMEs and mainly sell clothes, food and non-


---
**Page 151**
---

alcoholic and alcoholic beverages. Among the challenges experienced by women doing business include lack of capital, materials, markets, expertise / skills, knowledge and physical spaces to conduct business. The constitution recognizes equal rights for men and women. Women are expected to have better access to land and control over the resources and information needed for dairy production, value addition, and marketing. Men generally own cattle while women have ownership over the milk. Women are hindered in dairy development by a lack of ownership of livestock and limited access to credit. The level of education is also an issue; many women at household or farm-level women's decision-making power is low as also seen in farming groups, in which they seldom hold leadership and decision-making positions.

Mostly this varies within various cultural contexts, and this diversity should inform analysis and interventions.


In traditional dairy production systems, gender roles vary with geographies. For example, in the Southern Highlands of Tanzania, milking is a man's activity, but not a taboo for women; whereas in Tangas both women and men can milk. In some cases, women are more involved than men because they have better access to livestock. Women also lose their role when the youth too often have limited rights over household assets, control and ownership of commodities. As value chains commercialize and markets become sophisticated, moving further away from the farm gate, women's roles in dairy production decline. In some cases, women are not even allowed to enter the markets rudimentary, milk remains a woman's commodity. Once the productivity increases to profitable magnitudes and the value chain becomes commercialized the ownership of milk is likely to change to men. To address these issues, various interventions have been implemented such as involving women in decision-making processes, partnering, using participatory sensitization meetings with women and men at household and farmer group level to develop agreed upon dairy production systems, providing training on animal husbandry and business management, gender equality training, etc.


The Gender Learning System (GALS) where women have multiple livelihood options, such as dairy production, crop residue preparation for fodder production and fodder harvesting "cut and carry" from communal lands, raise poultry, collect firewood, sell milk at the market, and engage in other income-generating activities (e.g., weaving and other uses). With support for the various livelihood options, the women can still survive the worst-case scenario of losing milk business men. The DAAD and the IAD Climate Smart Smallholder Dairy Transformation Project (C-SSTP) will support additional geographical areas such as peri-urban dairy communities in the coastal region as well as some pastoral communities not included in the C-SSTP.


Kenya


Women have a right to own land according to law, however socio-cultural practices favour male inheritance. Only about 7 percent women own land alone. Without secure tenure to land, women's participation is an ill benefit from land uses such as dairy is inhibited. Yet women provide up to 75% of agricultural labour in addition to heavy workloads related to household chores and child care. Women are not paid for their contribution to the farm and provide labour for commercial farming where they are paid a pittance. Women also have less access to information, extension services and climate smart technologies. The situation is compounded by the delineation of agriculture as a function of the county government necessitating coordination and cooperation between the national and county governments.


For the various activities within the livestock sub-sector, women contribute between 60-80 percent of the labour-force, especially in ASAL areas. On average, women work longer hours (12.9 hours) than men (8.2 hours), yet they earn less than half of their male counterparts and are not paid for their contribution to livestock and other agro-human economic activities. Enrolment of both boys and girls in primary and secondary schools has been increasing from 2009


---
**Page 152**
---

to 2018 and is almost at par with 49.3 percent boys and 57.3 percent girls. Females also account for 41.3 percent of public and private university students, 43 percent of technical and TVET enrolments, and 69.2 percent adult education enrolment. The prevalence of girls in school remains remarkable irrespective of their parents’ income, manufacturing, wholesale finance and insurance. Generally, boys perform better than girls, but girls also perform well.

Maternal mortality is improving; the Kenya Demographic and Health Survey (KDHS) 2014 the ratio stood at 362 deaths per 100,000 live births compared to 579 in 2008. The KDHS 2014 also revealed that the overall prevalence of HIV is higher among women compared to men. In 2017, the National adult HIV prevalence rate had reduced and was estimated at 4% with prevalence higher among women (6.2%) than men (4.5%). Retrogressive and harmful cultural practices such as female genital mutilation (FGM) and early marriage are still prevalent in Kenya. The prevalence of FGM is 31% in women aged 18–29 years, 70% for women aged 30–39 years, 65% for women aged 40–49 years, and 21% in women for ages 18–29. Prevalence of FGM varies among regions; it was highest in the Somali – 94%, Samburu - 80%, Bodi- 84%, Kisi-Kaasai – 76%.

Climate change is one of the greatest environmental and development challenges facing the world today. Women bear the brunt of climate change because they are more vulnerable to its effects, have less access to resources such as land and water, and have limited ability to adapt.

Youth empowerment. Youth in East Africa accounts for over 40 percent of those between the ages of 15–35. Youth unemployment remains high and is higher than among young women than men. In rural areas youths are informally employed (and underemployed) in subsistence agriculture and family-based livelihood activities. This demographic dividend can be harnessed to drive economic growth, but this requires a conducive environment for youth employability. As the largest employer in the country, the agriculture sector remains a key entry point for job creation, inclusive growth and poverty reduction.

However, many small-scale farmers participate in dairy due to hope capital and operational investments required. Involving the youth in dairy farming is crucial as it provides a platform to tap into the potential of a young, educated population that can be trained and skilled to meet input and service delivery gaps necessary to develop sustainable livelihoods for them. The youth are also involved in other value chain activities such as marketing and dairy sector. Youth’s openness towards new practices can be key in development for, for example, new and environmentally responsible dairy practices and to fully utilise the potential of new technologies such as AI for the dairy sector. For example, the use of AI in livestock management has been shown to increase milk production by 10%. This has resulted to many employment opportunities along in support activities for agriculture. The youth mainly involved in the informal jobs, which are largely self-employment opportunities, include mobile milk traders, milk bars and shops and tea stalls. These enterprises are usually small scale, with limited capital, poor infrastructure, limited financial capital, limited entrepreneurial skills, poor rural infrastructure, mind barriers and chagrity of farm labour due to limited education and training. Removing these constraints will raise the industry’s productivity, which in turn would create more jobs for the young women and men.

The 2003 Rwandan Constitution enshrines gender equality and social inclusion and sets out the obligations of the State. In 2019, MINAGRI launched its Gender and Youth Mainstreaming Strategy aimed at providing women and youth with equal opportunities to access resources and services in order to achieve their full potential on a sustainable path.

The youth population in Uganda comprises of both the educated/skilled and the unskilled/illiterate majority in rural areas. Two-thirds of the labour force is below 35 years. Although 65-75 per cent of the population works in agriculture, its contribution to GDP has been declining over time. The youth are increasingly becoming more entrepreneurial. Access to a market, having an opportunity for access to credit and finance, and working with modern and digital technologies are important. Specializing, creating jobs and decent employment for youth in dairy and beer value chain, can reduce rural


---
**Page 153**
---

poverty, improve food security and nutrition in the country. In Tanzania: Youth (15-35 years) and children (0-15 years) account for 75% of the population, with a median age of 18 years old. Two-thirds fall below the poverty line; those younger than 15 years represent half of the 16 million youth who are currently unemployed in Tanzania (off farm agricultural). Young men more often than young women find non-agricultural employment, approximately 60% versus 40%. The Government of Tanzania (GoT), specifically the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA), is implementing the Build a Better Tomorrow (Babt) programme to address these challenges. Babt aims at: i) improving access and utilization of youth through implementing behaviour/attitude-changing communication strategy; ii) empower youth through training mentoring and coaching; iii) engage youth in profitable and sustainable management of agribusinesses; iv) enable youth-led agricultural innovations and entrepreneurship; v) provide youth with financial services; vi) create support initiatives for synergy and efficiency. Through partnerships between the private sector, NGOs and government institutions, the MoA anticipates achieving 12,000 new agribusiness enterprises in villages across the country, for which 75% of the beneficiaries are women. The Babt programme is also targeting youth who are interested in livestock specifically, youth involvement is less well documented. There are eight Youth Incubation Centres for beef and goat fattening that will take 240 and 1,300 women in 2023/24 and 2023/24 respectively. Collective action for the youth is also being promoted through the Livestock Agribusiness Support Programme (LASP) project. Community Cowshed Concept for dairy are a type of Livestock Farmer Field School (L-FFS) that provides social cohesion from which youth learn and farm dairy together. The Community Cowshed Concept could serve as an incubation incubator for youth, training the young farmers passing through and starting their own dairy business from there.

Kenya's population is largely young, with 35.7 million Kenyans (75.1%) below the age of 35 years and offers a dynamic workforce. However, the country faces several challenges including: high youth unemployment at over 40%, a high unemployment rate of 35%. The key challenges for youth participation in agriculture include negative perception and attitude to agriculture, inadequate skills, knowledge, and information such as in dairy farming, limited agricultural innovations and entrepreneurship opportunities. There are various national and international initiatives, strategies, institutional and policy frameworks to empower the youth at national level and county levels. However, most counties are yet to implement youth policies.

Marginalized indigenous groups. In Rwanda, the 2003 Constitution does not recognize the economic, social and cultural rights of the Batwa and it only refers indirectly to them under the term "historically marginalized communities", a term that during the constitution-making process was generally understood to refer to the Batwa. The Constitution recognizes the right to self-identification and the right to belong to one's community. Thirdly, there is no compatibility with the definitions of indigenous people under international and regional human rights law – in particular the emphasis on the right to self-identification - and can therefore not be interpreted as a similar legal protection for Batwa as that provided by the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples (UNDRIP). However, The National land policy does make considerations for pastoralists such as the Karagasia community living in the North. Tanzania consists of over 100 ethnic groups (majority indigenous Batwa and Bantu origin) and with a population of about 56 million people. There are also many non-indigenous communities in rural areas. Ethnic controversy and conflict are rare in Tanzania compared to neighbouring countries. Tension between peoples of different ethnicities is more likely to be related to land, water resources, natural resources, religion, gender, Sector transformations, private financial investments, and policies favouring sedentary over pastoral livelihoods.

Protection of pastoral people's rights can be difficult, since they often migrate (in marginal areas) across national borders and are not seen as indigenous to a nation, region or district. In the Southern Highlands and Zanzibar, indigenous people are sedentary smallholder farmers combining agriculture, horticulture and livestock farming from.


---
**Page 154**
---

Tabara and Singida, indigenous pastoralists migrate to Mbuya and Vinga in search for grazing. In Tanganyika pastoralists and smallholders, both indigenous to Tanga, coexist with their green grasslands, sharing common pastures.

Cross border migration from Kenya is another important reason for the shift all cattle owners have been assisted by the National Ranching Company to new farmers (including pastoralists deciding to become farmers) through tripartite loans. Consultation with selected members of the Masaii community in Matetebe Village, Mbarara district in Mbeya region revealed that they were not consulted about the land allocation and that they were not informed when the government ranched the land in the 1970s. The beneficiaries of this land, however, still move their cattle to other areas in search for food and water. This has led to conflicts with farmers who are trying to safeguard indigenous people's territories and will not alter their culture nor marginalize them, neither should DUMA.

An estimated 75,000 people identify as indigenous in Kenya, and are found among the pastoralist communities of Turkana, Rendille, Borana, Masai, Samburu, Itihamu, Somali, Gabbra, Pokot and Endemic Communities. Some of these groups have been displaced from their ancestral lands due to government policies that have led to land alienation and resource tenure insecurity, poor service delivery, weak political representation, as well as general discrimination and exclusion. It is also important to recognize that they are valuable partners and holders of local traditional knowledge that can be harnessed for the benefit of all Kenyans.

People living with Disability: The EAC Secretariat has developed a regional EAC Policy on Persons with Disabilities (PWDs) which was adopted by the EAC Summit in 2013. The policy recognizes that persons with disabilities are among the most vulnerable groups in society. They are often undereducated, untrained, unemployed or underemployed. They are also likely to be extremely poor – especially women, youth and those living in rural areas. In the region, disability is not well understood by many people and there are no national laws well established. In the four countries, different disabled persons' organisations and the umbrella organisation regularly take part in discussions with the government on issues affecting the lives of people with disabilities. Therefore, there is need to ensure that all policies and programmes are inclusive of people with disabilities.

Nutrition: There has been modest progress towards achieving global nutrition targets in the East African region. However, not a single country in the subregion is on course to meet the targets for anemia in women of reproductive age (aged 15 to 49 years), low birth weight, diabetes among men, diabetes among women, obesity among men, and obesity among women by 2025. The prevalence of overweight or obesity among adults aged 18 years and above is 33%. Some 13.6% of infants have a low weight at birth in the Eastern Africa subregion. East Africa still experiences a malnutrition burden among children aged under 5 years. The average prevalence of overweight & obese - the second lowest in the region - was 20%, compared to an average of 48% for the entire African continent, which is significantly higher than the global average of 21%. Conversely, the East Africa subregion's prevalence of wasting is 3.2%, which is lower than the regional average of 5.6%. In addition, the region's adult population also faces a malnutrition burden: an average of 7.1% of adults (aged over and above) are obese with diabetes, compared to 9.5% of women. Meanwhile, 12.1% of women and 3.5% of men live with obesity.

In Rwanda, anemia among children went down from 52% in 2005 to 38% in 2010 but this prevalence is still high. Children under one year of age, six out ten are suffering from anemia. Among pregnant women, [20%] were found to be anaemic. Seven percent of Rwandan women are too thin and 16 percent are overweight or obese.

Overweight and obesity are higher in urban areas with a 30% prevalence for women in the City of Kigali. Vitamin A and iodine deficiency has reduced more than anemia, which mainly affects pregnant women and children; six to 24 months old children are most affected by stunting, wasting, and growth and more so in infants. The malnutrition cannot be met by diet alone. In 2010, anaemia prevalence in Rwanda was 25% during pregnancy and 17% among women of reproductive age. More than 70% of children 6–24 months were found to be anemic. Malnutrition of young children is a major cause of death and disability. The economic burden of malnutrition on the economy’s productive capacity estimated at up to 10% over a lifetime. Cumulating these negative effects can result in economic losses of up to 3% or more.

In Uganda, gender relations play an important role in food security and nutrition management in the community. Women control most of the food and with limited resources, but they have no control over this food. Food and nutrition data available for Uganda are now two decades old and need to be updated. Even so, in 2003, the infant mortality rate was at 88.4 per 1,000 live births while under the five mortality rate was 152 per 1,000 live births. The Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR) is estimated to be around 670 deaths per 100,000 live births. Women are illiterate, illnne and hunger exist and childhood undernutrition still persists. 40 percent of deaths among children were due to malnutrition. Over eighty-eight per cent of the children below five years were stunted; a 40 per cent were wasted and had 22.5 per cent were underweight. The prevalence of anemia in women was estimated at around 38% while for children such a prevalence rate of 5%. Iron deficiency anaemia was slightly more than 50%, while 10% of the woman population were iron deficient. In addition, 42 percent of pregnant women and 67 percent of lactating mothers were anemic. Nearly children less than 5 years old, school-going age children, adolescents, women in the reproductive age, internally displaced persons and prisoners.

Malnutrition in Tanzania is high. Over 34% of children under the age of five are stunted and nearly 41% of women aged between 15–49 suffer from anemia. The most affected regions, with a prevalence of stunting exceeding 40%, include the semi-arid areas of Ruvuma (41%), Iringa (37%), Rufiji (48%), Kilombero (42%), Njombe (54%), and Songwe (43%). In Pemba North West, 39%, and in Pemba South, 33% of children are stunted. In Zanzibar, stunting rates range from 17–60%. The prevalence of anemia is high among pregnant women (52%) and lactating mothers (48%). Being thin for age), inadequate care and feeding practices, low nutrition education on dietary needs of different age groups (young children, adolescent girls, women of reproductive age, pregnant and lactating mothers), etc. remain an issue. Studies have shown that the majority of households in rural areas do not have access to a variety of foods; they only access to milk, a nutritious high protein animal source food, dairy farmers need to be trained on the importance of allocating some of the milk (mainly evening milk) for household consumption and the importance of making other animal source foods available. The government has initiated several programs such as the National School Feeding Program, promotion of women’s livelihood options such as poultry, and collective action in the milk cooperative will empower these households to have a better impact on nutrition. DAGMA partners e.g., GAF, IFNPs can support school milk feeding programs by applying lessons from other successful interventions such as in Kenya whereby providing school milk increased nutrition, attendance and enrolment.

In Kenya, lack of access to adequate and diversified food results in various forms of protein nutrition problem. In 2011, over two million children (33%) were reported to be stunted. Children under five years are particularly affected by deficiencies in nutrient-rich foods such as milk, eggs, fish, meat, legumes, fruits and vegetables. Women aged between 15–49 years are most vulnerable with a high risk of iron deficiency (60% among pregnant woman) and vitamin A. An estimated 16% of adult

Gender Based Violence (GBV) and Sexual Exploitation and Harassment (SEAH). In Rwanda, cultural and patriarchal stereotypes and attitudes perpetuate GBV. Negative gendered attitudes, roles and expectations such as a culture of silence, lack of education on GBV, and the belief that women are inferior to men, are factors for survivors. Men and boys are often left out of the dialogue and seem as perpetrators rather than allies in GBV prevention. For male survivors, service seeking is limited by the fear of ridicule, loss of influence and space in the community if news of abuse spreads. In Uganda, the culture of silence, lack of education on GBV, and the belief that women with gendered power imbalance, the culture of silence and the high levels of tolerance and non-reporting, the practice of GBV in Rwanda has created an internal self-sustaining cycle which can only be broken through combined efforts of transitional justice, legal reforms and social change.

In Uganda, the 2016 Demographic and Health Survey revealed that seven in ten women who had suffered sexual violence neither sought help nor told anyone. Factors that contribute to under-reporting of GBV include social stigma and discrimination, fear of retaliation, lack of knowledge about services available for survivors, and a high tolerance for violence. Most Ugandans think the police take GBV cases seriously, but majorities also see domestic violence as a private rather than a criminal matter and think that women who report GBV to the authorities will face negative consequences such as being ostracized by their community or losing custody of children. In addition, early marriage, FGM, and in some cases rape as normal societal occurrences. 13 Sexual gender-based violence predisposes survivors to HIV, unwanted teenage pregnancies, and mental illnesses like depression. This leads to dropouts among adolescents who are unable to continue with their education. The prevalence rate for GBV is high among women aged 15-49 years old compared to child pregnancies. Uganda has a high prevalence of SGBV (22%) among women in ages 15–49 years. While SGBV among AGYW does not necessarily lead to pregnancy, an analysis of District Health Information System (DHIS2) data indicates that at least one-third of all cases of GBV reported by women aged 15-49 years old resulted in pregnancy.

Tanzania has a supportive policy and legal framework for GBV. However, many forms of GBV, including intimate partner violence and rape, are tolerated by both men and women—although the justifications for acceptance differ. Women/girls are most frequently blamed for causing or provoking GBV. In such cases, in addition to domestic violence and girls’ education, other factors include lack of economic independence and social norms. For example, President Museveni publicly stated that GBV should be included as one of the Millennium Development Goals. Furthermore, Tanzania’s constitution recognizes women’s rights to equality and non-discrimination, but it does not define GBV as an act of its realization or poverty—which is rare among poverty reduction strategies in other countries.

The Kenya Demographic and Health Survey shows that 60% of percent of women aged 15-49 have experienced either physical or sexual violence, while nearly half (47) percent of those who ever married experienced either emotional, physical or sexual violence. Women are also larger at risk of FGMAC; the prevalence rate in Kenya being 27 percent. Furthermore, women are more likely to be victims of GBV than men and are usually perpetrated by persons they know.

## Programme beneficiaries

DoTMa will benefit about 500,000 households (HHs) of which 145,000 HHs will be involved in dairy value chains. The project will support smallholder farmers to increase their milk production by 2-3 kg/day per cow and 70% HHs. The majority are dairy farmers in intensive and semi-intensive systems who currently sell or aim to sell milk in formal and informal markets. These smallholder dairy farmers have generally between 1 to 5 dairy cows (6 to 10 kg of milk/kg/day) and are mainly targeted through Component 2 of the Programme. They will benefit from technical support of producers who need to be scaled up to support the formalization of the dairy sector and foster sustainable intensification in semi-intensive systems (see Table 3.1: Dairy Farmers’ Characteristics and Market Studies). Dairy value chain support will also benefit medium and large commercial farmers (5 or more dairy cows, 5-9 kg of milk/kg/day) in semi-intensive systems, 12 or more kg/milk/day in intensive systems). Under Output 2.3, the Programme will provide technical assistance to smallholder farmers who are not yet connected with dairy farmers through the rehabilitation/ improved management of 268,000 hectares of rangelands. Entry points to reach dairy farmers will also benefit from the Programme's support, including dairy cooperatives and milk collection centres (MCCs). Smallholder farmers will be mainly targeted through Component 1 and Component 2. Beneficiaries of Component 3 will be the larger formal stakeholders in the value chain (dairy cooperatives, private enterprises, processors, input suppliers) who have been identified as key actors for the implementation of the Programme. They will benefit from technical, financial funding applications, and manage implementation. These formal stakeholders will provide backward and forward linkages, extension services and agro supplies to the smallholders.

The target beneficiaries for DoMA will include:

- smallholder dairy farmers/ entrepreneurs, pastoralists,
- input and service providers including dairy processors and their communities, including women, youth, children and vulnerable groups such as people living with HIV/AIDS.

Others who will benefit in order to facilitate services to smallholder dairy farmers will include:

- financial service providers: Participating Financial Institutions (PFIs) which will be offering financial services to the small holder dairy entrepreneurs and MMAT markets e.g., dairy cooperatives and SACCOs, and other micro-finance institutions (MFIs).
- other service providers such as advisory extension staff, input suppliers, agrovetts, value chain actors;
- program management: DoMA Facility Coordination Unit at FAO and implementing partners or focal points in the districts.

### Targeting strategy and approaches

The targeting strategy will consist of:

(i) geospatial targeting to identify participating Districts and regions and cities in Uganda, Kenya, Tanzania and Rwanda, where dairy has been developed and there is potential to develop the identified VC interventions, and where mature clustered farmers exist for entering market potential. The programme intervention areas are identified based on the integration of (i) the climate risk hotspots; (ii) absolute methane emissions per district/country; (iii) the current level of dairy production per district/country; (iv) livestock herd production by district/country; and (v) complementarity with other international financing institutions (IFI)-funded interventions, more particularly from FAO (see figure 1 below). (ii) The target groups are smallholder dairy farmers, including those who have not yet reached the 40 per cent and 30 per cent quota for youth (iii) in targeting, with activities geared towards the needs of small and medium producers that are at various stages of development. (iv) Building and capacity-building measures to develop the capacity and self-confidence of those with less of a voice or limited power so that they can articulate their needs and participate in planning, decision-making and project activities.

## 2. GENDER AND SOCIAL INCLUSION ACTION PLAN

DoMA will target 40 per cent women; 30 per cent youth; and 10 per cent comprising of persons living with disabilities (PWDs). The gender action plan is designed to ensure that women are integrated with technological solutions to reduce methane and GHG emissions, and the financial support services to sustain efficient production and marketing processes. Indigenous women will be protected by ensuring that they will not be displaced from their ancestral land; children will benefit through school feeding programs. Activities include milk provision through the school feeding program and other interventions such as mother nutrition education.

The objective of this DoMA GES action plan is to ensure that women, youth, people with disabilities and marginalized groups have equal opportunities to participate in, and benefit from, the program as well as in these communities. Social inclusion will be achieved by ensuring that all participants are provided with access to land, credit facilities, seed varieties, economic empowerment opportunities, access to information and skills as well as linkage to market and value added. In this regard, the action plan primarily proposes to integrate GES actions within the project activities and sub-activities. The DoMA will mainstream GES actions such as those in favor of GGE and GEE where a direct fit is not guaranteed with core DoMA activities.

# Purpose

The purpose of the DaIMA Programme is to increase smallholder dairy production and associated input and service providers' revenue, and/or reduce their production costs through reduced methane and other GHG emissions by adopting low emission reduction technologies and financial services to efficiently sustain production and marketing processes.

## Objectives of the Programme

I. To strengthen systemic and institutional capacities of the livestock sector to enable smallholder dairy producers and local value chain actors to reduce CH4 and other GHG emissions.
II. To increase access for women, youth and vulnerable groups in the dairy sector to inputs and private sector value chain operators for a low-emission, climate-resilient, and sustainable pathway
III. To enable smallholder dairy producers and local private sector value chain actors across to require financial services and support to transition to low-carbon, climate-resilient, and sustainable livestock production and value chain development.

## Theory of change, assumptions and risks

The programme will integrate GESI considerations into DaIMA Components and activities, which will enable targeted interventions for women and youth to overcome the specific barriers identified. This approach will provide opportunities for women and youth to participate in and benefit from dairy production. Therefore, the integration of GESI activities within the Programme Components is critical. Component 1 is about strengthened national capacities and enabling environment for smallholder dairy producers, Component 2 is about low-emission and climate-resilient primary dairy production and Component 3 on Green Dairy Financial Incentive (GDF).

DaIMA aims to address the following challenges: (i) strengthening climate resilience; (ii) reducing emissions across the dairy value chain; (iii) the reduction of GHG emissions per kg milk, and (iv) improving the livelihoods of dairy farmers in East Africa.

A holistic approach is needed for a more sustainable dairy sector. A combination of policy, institutional and service delivery elements will build the capacity of smallholder farmers including women, youth and vulnerable groups to adopt low-emission practices and technologies that are climate-resilient through mitigation and adaptation benefits. As a result, female and youth dairy farmers and processors will be motivated to adopt more efficient, climate-resilient practices that will increase incomes, reduce production costs, and help combat climate change. This will also lead to improved access to markets for smallholder dairy sector inputs and services within the dairy sector. This integration aims to foster market growth while promoting emission reduction and/or GHG emissions.

To address these gender barriers, DaIMA will contribute to gender equality and women empowerment, women by: (i) increasing women's economic empowerment through access to improved dairy breeds, better access to productive resources and services, marketing and value addition and access to finance by targeting women dairy smallholders in dairy farms; (ii) improving the capacity of women dairy farmers to adopt low-emission practices and technologies, climate-resilient crop and sustainable production and greater efficiency; (iii) increased women participation, voice and decision-making in dairy farmer organizations and cooperatives (iv) increasing positive gender and social norms such as joint benefits sharing through the GDFs methodology and (v) contributing to policy

### COMPONENT 1: STRENGTHEN NATIONAL CAPACITIES AND ENHANCED ENABLING ENVIRONMENT FOR LOW EMISSIONS CLIMATE RESILIENT DAIRY SECTORS

#### Output 1.1: Climate-responsive policy and regulatory environment established.

**Activity 1.1.1:** Review and formulate Dairy Sector policies, strategies and regulations for climate change adaptation and mitigation. In the four countries, the Programme will support the Ministries, Departments and aligned institutions to review and formulate dairy sector policies, strategies and regulations that are climate-resilient, gender-based and socially inclusive and essential for a sustainable transformation of the dairy sector, in line with the NDCs and MDPs. This includes aspects related to mainstreaming CC adaptation and mitigation, and adopting export and import regulations that support the development of resilient value chains. The review will also consider the entire value chain (importation of dairy produce, supplemental equipment, inputs such as germplasm, fodder seeds, feed additives, veterinary medicines, coolers, etc.). Resources will be provided to (i) mobilize national and international expertise for policy review workshops; (ii) ensure that the policy review and dialogues are inclusive; (iii) validation processes; and (iv) the preparation of policy briefs and communication materials for popularization.

**Sub-activity 1.1.1.1:** Support the review and preparation of national dairy policies, strategies, and regulations to mainstream climate change adaptation and mitigation. Priorities in the four countries will be as follows: In Kenya, (i) the National Dairy Development Investment Plan; (ii) the policy framework for the National Dairy Board; (iii) the draft National Strategy and legal framework for Animal Feed and Fodder; (iv) A National Strategy for Animal Breeding and breeding regulations (the draft Dairy Act India 1958); (v) the Kenya Agricultural Sector Extension Policy (KASEP), to be aligned with the NDCs and MDPs. In Tanzania, (i) the review of the National Dairy Development Investment Plan; (ii) quality in Rwanda, the upcoming Strategic Plan for Agriculture Transformation 5 (PSAT5) provides a framework for a low carbon and climate-resilient agriculture sector. Within this framework, the Programme will support the review of (i) national dairy policies, strategies and regulations to mainstream climate change adaptation and mitigation after Rwanda Food and Drugs Authority (RFD&A) and Rwanda Standards Board (KSL), for milk quality, standards and safety, feed formulation, energy efficiency, etc.; and (iii) The 2016 Ministerial Order to address new dairy value chain realities, including the need to improve the quality of raw milk production and processing, especially by women and young men. Support will be given to the formulation of a national regulatory framework for the private veterinary practice including for Community Animal Health Workers (CAHWs) and Livestock Farmer Fields Schools (LFFS) facilitators with consideration on the need to improve the quality of raw milk production and processing, especially by women and young men. Support will be given to the formulation of a national regulatory framework for the private veterinary practice including for Community Animal Health Workers (CAHWs) and Livestock Farmer Fields Schools (LFFS) facilitators with consideration on the need to improve the quality of raw milk production and processing, especially by women and young men. Support will be given to the formulation of a national regulatory framework for the private veterinary practice including for Community Animal Health Workers (CAHWs) and Livestock Farmer Fields Schools (LFFS) facilitators with consideration on the need to improve the quality of raw milk production and processing, especially by women and young men. # Sub-activity 1.1.1.3 National Dairy value chain governance and awareness

The aim of this activity is to enhance national dairy value chain governance as a key element of climate change adaptation process. At national level, the national dairy boards (KDB, TDA, TDR, NPDPI) and other relevant professional bodies of private stakeholders, will be supported to create gender awareness in the dairy policy dialogue, to create awareness and education/capacity building on existing and new policies that can potentially affect women's health and the vulnerable.

# Sub-activity 1.1.1.4 District level dairy value chain governance

Multi-stakeholder platforms involving delegates from dairy value chain stakeholders will participate in the annual planning and review of Programme activities. DaMiA will ensure participants women, youth dairy cooperative members, milk collectors, milk processors, service providers of artificial insemination (AI), veterinary services, extension staff and local governments.

## Activity 1.1.2 Launch awareness campaigns on nutritional benefits of milk and food safety aspects

This will be a targeted support to milk consumption and nutrition awareness, accompanied by nutrition education and social behaviour change among smallholder producer and consumer households, informal dairy value chain actors and milk handling institutions and nutritionally vulnerable groups. The programme will promote the consumption of milk rich in calcium, thus enhancing their individual health and nutrition. The Programme will support milk consumption and nutrition awareness campaigns at district level, involving women, youth dairy cooperative members, milk collectors, milk products, accompanied by nutrition education and social behaviour change and communication (BCC) to reach not to informal dairy value chain actors, milk handlers at home and institutions and nutritionally vulnerable groups. This


---
**Page 162**
---

activity will target 40 per cent women ensuring male engagement to increase the participation of men in improving household nutrition. Women will be trained as Tots and facilitated to roll out community outreach campaign sessions for food security.

### Sub-activity 1.1.2.1 Public awareness on the contribution of milk to nutrition and resilience, with focus on vulnerable population.
The awareness raising will use various community platforms such as extension, LIFS, MCC/cooperatives will include women and other vulnerable and marginalized groups meeting quotas. Topics will include: importance of milk in nutrition and resilience; benefits of consumption of fresh milk over pasteurized and processed milk. In Rwanda, it will include participation in dairy fairs/expos under the auspices of RINDP to showcase climate-friendly and energy efficient local processing and consumption of dairy products. In Tanzania and Uganda, the programme will scale up school milk programs for improved nutrition of vulnerable children especially those under five years.

### Sub-activity 1.1.2.2 Build capacity of dairy inspection to ensure milk quality and safety and reduce waste.
Daily Inspection is essential for sector-level milk quality and safety, thus encouraging formal market growth and reduction of milk rejection and waste (less GHG emission intensification). The Programme will provide technical training and equipment to inspectors in the provinces. The programme will also conduct a baseline survey on the level of inspections to ensure development and enforcement of milk quality and safety standards. Women and youth will be considered among the inspectors to increase their capacity development and execution of inspection, creating jobs.

### Activity 1.1.3 Improve land tenure security for sustainable dairy farming.
The Programme will adopt a fit-for-purpose approach in developing a land tenure security framework that is gender-sensitive and inclusive of all types of land, type of governance and sex and age of the person accessing and controlling the land. For pastoral and agropastoral systems, the focus will be on the identification of use rangelands by gender, their protection and supporting gender and youth participation in decision-making processes for sustainable dairy farming. The programme will also focus on optimizing land productivity through land consolidation and land use planning. Activities will include capacity building and support to participatory planning processes to create local-level capacities.

### Sub-activity 1.1.3.1 Mapping of local tenure rights and preparation of training materials.
The Programme will mobilize specialized technical assistance to (i) implement a rapid tenure governance capacity assessment, including a tenure risk analysis; (ii) develop a land tenure security framework that is gender-sensitive and inclusive of all types of land, type of governance and sex and age of the person accessing and controlling the land. The focus will be on increasing local authorities’ capacity to secure gender and social inclusive tenure rights for dairy production and the use of rangelands; (iii) develop training materials for the national tenure committee, local dairy production system’s tenure in terms of gendered access, use of secured off-farm land will be identified and assessed, and appropriate gendered mitigating strategies will be proposed.

### Sub-activity 1.1.3.2 Developing capacity on land tenure at decentralized level.
The focus will be on capacity building of local authorities and raising public awareness on options to incrementally increase tenure security for women and men in the dairy sector. The programme will also support the development of a gender-sensitive framework to support public awareness campaigns on tools available to secure tenure rights for women, youth and the vulnerable.

### Sub-activity 1.1.3.3 Participatory planning of tenure for dairy farms on communal and customary land at local level.
The Programme will scale-up best practices in gender and social inclusive rangelands/tenancy participatory planning and management approaches to secure tenure rights for women, youth and the vulnerable. The focus will be on the planning of tenure for dairy farms on communal and customary land (for example ensuring the community committees include 40 percent women.

2. Output I.2 Improved service delivery to the dairy sector

Activity I.2.1 Strengthen veterinary services for better animal health: higher milk yields and reduced losses. A key barrier to reduce GHG emissions and sustainably increase the productivity of dairy animals is animal health and welfare. Poor animal health leads to increased susceptibility to disease, such as mastitis (caused by Staphylococcus aureus – ECF), helminths infection, etc.). Healthier animals are more resilient to climate hazards and more productive, with less milk to be discarded, leading to a reduction of “unproductive” GHG emissions. The higher revenues provide increased household income for the dairy sector. Animal health services will be provided by trained personnel, including among the women and youth in the household. To expand the outreach and capacity of veterinary services, the Programme will invest in primary animal health support, as well as in national diagnostic capacity of veterinary laboratories to reduce the unequal access to these services.

Sub-activity 1.2.1.1 Support the installation of basic private sector public veterinarians and veterinary parapharmacies. The Programme will implement the animal health support and disease control system using, the “One Health” approach. This approach will have three levels: (i) support private veterinarians and para-veterinarians, in particular young female and male practitioners; (ii) recruit and support livestock extension workers, while ensuring gender balance among them; and (iii) provide training to veterinary students and young female and male trainees and youth farmers from the I-FFS for coaching their peers and assist private female and male para-veterinarians and livestock extension workers. In Kenya, veterinary education, and training programs to women and men from both private and public sectors will be provided by the University of Nairobi (UON) and the Moi University. In Rwanda, support will be provided for the installation of young female and male veterinary doctors and veterinary technicians as private veterinarians in selected districts. In Tanzania, female and male Main Livestock Extension Workers (LEWs) will be equipped and trained, and private rural and urban veterinarians will be trained to ensure the last mile animal health and extension services. In Uganda, The Programme will build capacity of female and male veterinary doctors and veterinary technicians as private veterinarians in selected districts. In all three countries, Health principles, disease diagnosis, emerging and re-emerging diseases because of CC.

Sub-activity 1.2.1.2 Strengthening diagnostic capacity of veterinary laboratories and capacity to test quality of animal feed. In Kenya, the Programme will improve disease control (surveillance and monitoring, contingency planning, and treatment) of targeted diseases including modern diagnostic equipment in regional centres (located in Nairobi, Eldoret, Kisumu, Mombasa, and Kitui). In Tanzania, the programme will provide diagnostic equipment in Arusha. In Rwanda, four (4) satellite laboratories will receive additional equipment for serology (ELISA) and molecular diagnostic PCR to enable rapid detection of infectious diseases, as well as equipment for handling biological waste and the establishment of a laboratory information management system (LIMS). The Programme will also establish a veterinary laboratory (VLAB) to handle advanced diagnostic capacity for viral diseases affecting livestock. In Tanzania, the programme will provide diagnostic equipment in Arusha. In Uganda, the Programme will establish an Animal Health Investigation Laboratory in Zanzibar; To support the establishment of district mobile laboratories that will serve LEWs (local paravets); (c) train female and male laboratory technicians for each mobile laboratory. In Uganda, veterinary laboratories will be equipped with modern diagnostic equipment to enable rapid detection of diseases and a laboratory facility for detection of the diseases related to CC. It will also establish a national data management system on emerging and re-emerging diseases because of CC (TAD and zoonosis). In each country, The Programme will: (i) enhance the capacity of veterinary laboratories in terms of equipment, personnel, and infrastructure; (ii) provide an all-CAVM in Rwanda, NARDI in Uganda, TALID in Tanzania); this includes equipment, training of young female and male researchers, and inputs for testing feed samples; (iii) undertake on-farm feed sampling and laboratory quality testing (e.g., mycotoxins, aflatoxin, etc.); (iv) establish a national animal health information system.

Activity 1.2.3 Strengthen extension services for low-carbon and climate resilient dairy sector: To reach out to men, women and youth dairy farmers, raise awareness and build their capacity in the field of ECC (mitigation and adaptation), the Programme will provide training on ECC technologies, tools and methods, and enhance the quality of extension services. This activity will involve improving delivery of public and private extension services at farm and cooperative aggregation levels, by female and male local (district/county) livestock officers who coordinate the community-based extension service providers.

The Programme will provide support through Livestock Farmer Field Schools (L-FFS), affirmatively ensuring the involvement of women, youth, and vulnerable farmers, youth in mixed crop-livestock systems and Vulnerable Field Schools (VFS) in grazing and pastoralist systems.


Sub-activity 1.2.3.1. Preparation of training modules for extension on climate mitigation and adaptation measures If required, specific topics that are part of the proposed pathways under Outputs 2.2.3 and 2.3 can be added to the modules of the L-FFS (or other extension approaches). The topics in the training modules related to climate change adaptation include: (i) livestock management practices for improved feed security such as use of conserved silage and urea-molasses multi-nutrient blocks (UMMB); supplementation (use of non-conventional feed resources e.g. sweet potato vines or tannin-rich plants reducing and inhibiting CH4 production) reproductive management, vaccination, deworming, breeding, selection, and genetic improvement; (ii) land and manure management, use of drought-resistant fodder, application of crop residues, seed production (certification, traceability, quality control); (iii) climate-smart technologies such as the use of feed additives (e.g. rumen inoculation software), use in the innovative SNV-developed Rumex® tool), and animal welfare, including housing in zero-grazing practices. The guidelines will be prepared on improved fodder culture and climate-smart technologies; innovative management practices for livestock production systems (e.g., mixed crop-livestock systems, agroforestry trees as standalone or line trees). The L-FFS modules will be developed with the support of national or international technical assistance. The L-FFS modules will include gender modules with strategies and guidelines to support women, youth and vulnerable groups participation. SNV will support the selection of female and young trainers.


Sub-activity 1.2.2.2 Training of master trainers (MTs) and facilitators on climate change-related topics: The training will be conducted in three phases: (i) MTs and the establishment of new L-FFS (aiming at 40 percent women and 30 percent youth). To support the sustainability and scaling-up of the L-FFS, existing L-FFS MTs and female master trainers and facilitators will be trained on the specific topics listed below. The training modules will include both general and related topics.


Sub-activity 1.2.2.3 Development of innovative extension tools: These innovative extension tools could include (i) participatory extension approaches, such as the Participatory Integrated Climate Services for Agriculture (PICSA), which has been piloted in Kenya, Rwanda and Tanzania; (ii) e-extension services by using bulk SMS and internet to reach more farmers; (iii) mobile applications for livestock management and climate change adaptation; (iv) training of young youth in the development of content and capacity and to improve their ability to access and share knowledge and skills on farming technologies; (v) TV, radio and social media networks. In addition, other innovative extension tools could include: (i) a mobile application for livestock management and climate change adaptation; (ii) a mobile app for K-MaP, a digital platform developed through a joint initiative between the Kagame and the KMD, designed to provide weather information to support decision-making in the agricultural sector in Kenya to develop capacity of extension officers and other stakeholders on climate-smart agriculture. The guidelines will be prepared on innovative extension tools that will be developed and promoted. In Rwanda digital agriculture solutions such as the smart-ukungurembe project provides services to farmers.

3. Output 1.3: Improved GHG MRV capacities & monitoring of climate vulnerabilities

To reliably track progress on the ambitious climate goals of the national NDCs, state-of-the-art systems of Measurement, Reporting and Verification (MRV) of GHG emissions are needed within the participating countries, along with a clear understanding of their institutional arrangements for implementation.

This Output will enable countries to report with accuracy on emissions in the dairy sub-sector in their national GHG inventories at Tier 2 level, and address the institutional, planning and knowledge barriers identified in the ToC. It will also enable countries to capture the full range of climate change mitigation actions that are being implemented as part of their NDCs or other state commitments. Improved MRV systems will ensure that participating countries can conduct their own GHG calculations with a high degree of confidence, and will provide a basis for reporting on progress towards national and international climate finance, including through carbon credits.

This Output will also address the issues related to the data for climate vulnerability assessment and national feed balances, all contributing to improved climate resilience. Countries will receive implementation support from GEF (Mainga Centre and research stations) to execute Activities 1.3.1 and 1.3.2.


Activity 1.3.1: Developing capacity in the country on MRV systems

The Programme will identify critical capacity needs at national and local levels to improve the operation of MRV systems by young and older women and men. This will help developing consistent MRV systems for the dairy sub-sector that allows system updates, so that the impact of interventions within programmes, and beyond, can be monitored over time, and progress can be assessed against an established baseline.


Sub-activity 1.3.1.1: Capacity assessment and development in capturing, recording, tracking, and reporting data on daily GHG emissions

This sub-activity will carry out in depth capacity assessments of existing MRV tools and frameworks for dairy sub-sector at national level. The results from the capacity assessment component will be provided to design activity data questionnaires for Tier 2 GHG emissions in dairy (e.g. animal numbers, weights, productivity, feed rations, animal health interventions, manure management and grassland management) and develop a data collection protocol. A training manual will also be developed based on the results of the capacity assessment involved in the MRV work. IRI’s Mainga Centre will provide training to young women and men from the countries on data collection protocols adjusted to their needs with relevant stakeholders (technicians, university staff, staff of national research stations, etc.). The training will also include the use of a range of generic dedicated emission estimation tools (e.g. ODKs), data quality control, and calculation of GHG emissions for inventory reporting, following established protocols.


Sub-activity 1.3.1.2: Developing MRV systems for the livestock sub-sector

This intervention will include baseline, measurement, monitoring and evaluation activities to develop a national MRV system for dairy sub-sector at Tier 2 level, including institutional arrangements. To ensure sustainability and impact at scale, the sub-activity will update of national livestock census questionnaires to include activity data requirements for GHG inventories outlined by IPCC on other cattle sub-sectors. This will enable countries to track livestock productivity and to report on enteric CH4 and manure CH4 and N2O emissions, and soil carbon storage in grasslands/rangelands, following International inventory guidelines, using IPCC Tier 2 methodology.


Activity 1.3.2: Improve GHG emission measurements in dairy value chains

On-form data like animal live weights and milk yields are necessary to calculate Tier 2 GHG emissions. Emission factors also need to be improved to reflect local conditions (e.g., animal metabolism, feed composition). This activity will comprise the following sub-activities:


---
**Page 166**
---

Sub-activity 1.3.2.1: Collect activity data on farms to calculate Tier 2 dairy emissions under various land, feed, animal health and management scenarios. To validate results, the collection of farm activity data for baseline and with project situations using digital tools (e.g., livestock Tier Expert, GEM) will be done. This will focus on animals numbers, weights, production, feed rations and manure management system. The measurements will be disaggregated by sex and age of the owner if the animal. This sub-activity will be performed annually with the support of all the project partners. The data collected will be used to develop a baseline scenario for Tier 2 emissions to ensure sustainability and replication through the transfer of capacity (see output 1.4). This activity will inform the Programme’s risk assessment and provide basis for baseline and with-project situation.

Activity 1.3.4: Climate risk and vulnerability assessments. Together with improvements to extension services under output 1.2, the Programme will seek to improve the accuracy and use of information (climate, feed availability) by last mile-users in the dairy sector especially women as livestock caretakers. This will include building the capacity of development partners to provide climate information services and disseminating and discussing the usage of information services by different groups, including (i) impacts of climate change on the dairy sector; and (ii) information available on feed availability through tools like national food balance sheets.

Sub-activity 1.3.4.1: Climate risk and vulnerability assessments, including mapping on the dairy sector at local level. It will consist mainly carrying out climate risk and vulnerability assessments at local level, based on historical and projected climatic data. This will involve the development of agro-climatic zone maps and Dairy Sector suitability maps based on the current climate (1995-2020 period). The assessments will include gendered aspects related to vulnerabilities faced by women and other groups such as pastoralists etc.

Output 4.1.6 Knowledge management and regional cooperation enhancement. The Programme will facilitate transfer of knowledge through regional and South-South and Triangular Cooperation (SSTC) platforms, including the East Africa University for Agriculture (EAU-IBAR). This will include low-cost and non-carbon dairy adopted by the Programme. The EAU-IBAR will contribute to the implementation, by facilitating regional platforms, sharing experiences, exporting best practices, and spearheading policy dialogue. EAU Managing Centre, as a State-of-the-Art environment for knowledge management, will be used to store, manage and disseminate knowledge. To achieve the institutional, planning and knowledge barriers identified in the TCF. Shared knowledge will enhance future low-carbon planning in the dairy sector, minimize climate risks and enable participating countries to take advantage of available climate change adaptation technologies.

Activity 4.1.1 Support to Regional Centres of Excellence for technology validation and for maintaining a regional database on practices, GHG emissions and adaptation indicators. To ensure sustainability and transfer of capacity to national institutions, the Programme will provide scientific support combined with capacity development targeting locally relevant research questions. This will include the establishment of pilot projects in Kenya for CC and GHG emissions for the dairy value chain. National Centres of Excellence (or “knowledge hubs”) will include national agricultural research institutions (e.g., KALRO in Kenya, NARO in Uganda, TALIRI in Tanzania, RAB in Rwanda), local universities and other relevant stakeholders. The Centre of Excellence will be hosted by the East Africa University of Agriculture (SUA) in Tanzania). It will build on the legacy of the East African Agricultural Productivity Programme (EAAPP; World Bank-funded), which has built Regional Centre of Excellence (RCoE) in the dairy sector in Kenya.
