# Sub-activity 1.4.1.1: Support for regional Centres of Excellence in technology validation and dissemination.

The Programme will assist the above-named institutions with (i) technical training and protocols for GHG measurement and reporting; (ii) capacity building on biochar production, soil carbon sequestration and enhancement, and other technologies for crop residue management, for research, MRV, and technology validation linked to climate change mitigation; (iii) research capacity for conducting innovative research such as optimizing the biogas processes for enhanced energy and nutrient recovery and building efficient biodigesters; (iv) development of a curriculum for training on biochar production, soil carbon sequestration, and other technologies for crop residue management; (v) development of curricular materials and guidelines integrating young women and youth; and (vi) knowledge, information and visibility products related to the focus areas feeding, animal health, resource management and biogas, and grassland management and soil carbon sequestration.

# Sub-activity 1.4.1.2: Develop and maintain a regional databases' repository on practices, GHG emissions per sources and systems, and climate change adaptation.
This sub-activity will consist in developing, maintaining, and promoting a regional database on best practices, and their impact on GHG emissions per source and livestock production system, and climate change adaptation. The database will be developed using the “Agricultural Carbon Footprint (ACF)” platform, which will be leveraged, and made available to all actors in the Programme, with the support of the EAC.

# Activity 1.4.2: Establish regional cooperation platforms and participation to regional/ global networks for knowledge exchange and policy dialogue on climate-smart dairy sector transformation.
The regional collaboration platforms will consist of a network of stakeholders from the East African Community (EAC), the East and Southern Africa Dairy Association (ESADA), local government partners, centres of excellence, international organisations and NGOs, and involve private sector platforms and networks (e.g. One Health Research and Education Centre (OHREC) in Kenya; National Dairy Development Board (NDB) in India; Dairy Innovation Circle Network; National Biogas Stakeholders Network in Kenya). The participants will include women and youth.
The Inter-Governmental Authority on Development (IGAD), through its Center for Pastoral Areas and Livestock Development (CPLAD), will be invited to share its experience of facilitating appropriate and agile knowledge, research, and technology development, including their methodologies, lessons, and transfer through regional platforms.

# Sub-activity 1.4.2.1: Regional platform for knowledge exchange and policy dialogue on climate-smart dairy sector transformation.
The regional platform will be organized on a rotational basis in each of the countries involved with the above-named entities and will be organized on a rotational basis in each of the countries involved with the above-named entities and will be organized on a rotational basis in each of the countries involved with the above-named entities and will be organized on a rotational basis in each of the countries involved with the above-named entities. The platform will function as the knowledge management mechanism for the Programme M&E system - on the GHG efficiency gains (from breeding, artificial insemination) herd management, feed, animal health, resource management and biogas, grassland management and soil carbon sequestration.
The platform will consist of a network of stakeholders from the East African Community (EAC), the East and Southern Africa Dairy Association (ESADA), local government partners, centres of excellence, international organisations and NGOs, and involve private sector platforms and networks. The participants will include women and youth.

# Sub-activity 1.4.2.2: Participation in regional and global climate policy meetings.
In practice, the sub-activity will consist of a joint participation of the 4 countries in global and regional events (global science and policy mechanisms on dairy and climate change) such as the Greenhouse Gas and Animal Agriculture Conference – GGAA 2015, the African Climate Change Summit (ACCS), etc. The objective is to ensure that the Programme can share its progress and results from the Programme for scaling up and replication, increase its visibility and engage with other countries


---
**Page 168**
---

# Sub-activity 1.4.2.3: South-South and Triangular Cooperation (SSTC)

This sub-activity will support the emergence of a global network of experience and expertise on reducing GHG emissions, especially methane in dairy production and value chains, through a financing mechanism for SSTC projects. This sub-activity will finance the participation in knowledge-exchange programs, including through FAO's "Learning Routes" with e.g., Argentina, Brazil (Embraz), Costa Rica, India, Indonesia, Kenya, Malaysia, Mexico, Peru, Philippines, South Africa, and Vietnam. The SSTC will be identified and assessed by the Regional Steering Committee in collaboration with the EAC. It will finance study tours and gender and socially inclusive country dialogues to workshops to share innovations and scalable practices presented by SSTC partners.

## COMPONENT 2: LOW EMISSION AND CLIMATE RESILIENT PRIMARY DAIRY PRODUCTION

### Output 5.1: Smallerholders' and cooperative capacity development strengthened.

#### Activity 2.1.1 Strengthen dairy cooperatives on organisational management and service delivery
This activity aims to strengthen cooperative, organisational, and technical capacities of dairy cooperatives, and raise their climate change and gender and social inclusion awareness of their management team. Also enhance the enrolment of women and youth value chain actors in the cooperative membership and leadership. Support will also be provided to establish a formal dairy cooperative structure.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.1.1 Strengthen the governance and management structures of dairy cooperatives
This sub-activity will include: (i) training on cooperative governance and management practices for all management, supervisory and general committees of dairy cooperatives; (ii) the business coaching of cooperatives by business development service providers where desirable, assistance for the registration as a formal dairy cooperative, and/or participation in national and regional events that are essential for dairy value chain governance, promoting inclusive policy, and business dialogues.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.1.2 Strengthen capacity to reduce milk losses and ensure milk quality and safety
At the aggregation stage, up to approximately 4 percent of the milk is lost via spillage and spilling. Among a range of non-comprehensive SHG simulations, it was found that the use of insulated milk containers (IMCs) and mobile cold rooms (MCRs) would increase their technical capacity in milk quality control covering raw milk testing at milk collection, hygiene, and initial processing before the sale of raw milk, in both national and regional food safety standards. The support will assist women and youth to participate as drivers, mechanics, and technicians of IMCs and MCRs (including transport and milking equipment), (ii) insulated milk transit vehicles (demonstration purpose); and/or training on milk quality, food safety, feed management, and hygiene; (iii) women and youth value chain workers, transporters and technical staff of cooperatives, MSFs and MCs ensuring women and youth participation.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.1.3 Expand the outreach and capacity of dairy cooperatives, IMCs and MCRs
The targeted MSFs and MCs will be supported to develop gender and youth response action plans to ensure opportunities for youth employment such as inclusion and equal employment opportunities ensuring women can work such as "zero tolerance" on GEI in all aspects of work and inclusive policies and practices. This may include training or technical assistance for such institutional capacity building.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.1.4 Create gender awareness and roll out GALS among households of dairy farmers
Gender transformative approaches will be integrated in the Programme. Capacity building and trainings for stakeholders on


---
**Page 169**
---

# Sub-activity 2.1.2 Build and enhance Productive Alliances along the dairy value chain

The Programme will provide support to productive alliances between farmer cooperatives and enterprises with farmer-aligned engagement models, such as the generation of inputs for specific dairy clusters. This will enable farmers and young farmers to access affordable, high-quality inputs and services.

## Sub-activity 2.1.2.1 Strengthen cooperatives' capacity in business and financial management

The aim is to ensure that dairy cooperatives and their MCCs are run as viable business enterprises. Therefore, the Programme will support cooperatives in developing a business plan and financial management system. The programme will also offer member farmers (e.g., facilitated feed and AI service purchases checked-off against milk sales) – serving as “dairy hubs”. These dairy hubs will build capacity of young farmer leaders as mentors to increase youth engagement. For implementation, the Programme will support cooperatives in developing a business plan and financial management system for their MCCs and MCCs will receive: (i) financial literacy training; (ii) financial management training and equipment (software and IT equipment); (iii) gender-sensitive and inclusive business management and business plan development training; (iv) mentorship and coaching to young farmers in cooperatives; (v) facilitation of access to credit for trained young people in participating MCCs and MCCs as part of increasing their employability and creating jobs. Further, youth trainees will receive business mentoring/coaching on business and financial management in well-performing cooperatives and enterprises and be signed up as agreed upon after completing their training.

## Sub-activity 2.1.2.2 Build capacity for dairy value chain actors to negotiate off-takes

The Programme will support the development of a database and data base building for negotiation of off-take agreements between (cooperatives) and private off-takers (Productive Alliance model), as well as public procurement (e.g., school or hospital procurement) for better milk prices guaranteed by contract. The aim is to support dairy cooperatives in developing their business models, including the development of key components of their farmer engagement models, such as quality-based or volume-based price incentive terms offered by processors, extension provision, and direct or facilitated provision of inputs and services. Through this support, a pre-commercial procurement will be developed, and related financing needs identified. This support will facilitate youth- and women-led alliances.

## Sub-activity 2.1.2.3 Promote digitalization and traceability of dairy operations

The Programme will promote digitalization and traceability to ensure quality and entry into formal supply arrangements that come with services, and provide training on the use of digital tools for farm management and record keeping. The programme will also support legal solutions to scale-up uptake and usage especially ensuring female farmers’ benefit. Use of transformative end-to-end (E2) digital solutions is currently limited. Digitized systems for monitoring on-farm activities, milk production

# Output: 2.2 Breeding, feed and fodder, and management improved.

## Activity 2.1.3 Promote climate-smart dairy farming adapted to climate change: breeding and genetic improvement of animals and plants, and resilience of dairy cattle while preserving resilience of livestock keeping stocks by crossing indigenous and exotic breeds through artificial insemination (AI). Exotic breeds, such as Holstein-Friesian or Jersey, are more vulnerable to high temperatures, and consequently to climate change.

### Sub-activity 2.1.3.1 Promote the adoption of climate-smart dairy farming adapted to climate change: breeding and genetic improvement of animals and plants, and resilience of dairy cattle while preserving resilience of livestock keeping stocks by crossing indigenous and exotic breeds through artificial insemination (AI). Exotic breeds, such as Holstein-Friesian or Jersey, are more vulnerable to high temperatures, and consequently to climate change.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.1.1 Assess the need and the viability of renewable energy and waste management solutions. The aim will be to assess the clean energy needs of dairy cooperatives and promote RE solutions (photovoltaics – PV, biomass systems, etc.), energy-efficient equipment, and solid and water waste management solutions. The assessment will look at the potential for solar power generation on rooftops and other surfaces.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.1.2 Support the acquisition of renewable energy and waste management solutions. This would include the upgrading or equipping of dairy cooperatives with suitable RE, PV-powered technology for milk coolers for milk aggregation, milk transfer tanks, solar water heaters, water recycling systems, heat exchangers, milk pasteurizers, buffer tanks, etc., as well as equipment to treat and recycle waste from dairy operations. The aim will be to reduce energy demand from natural gas or generated by standard generators to cool down milk to 4°C. This activity will facilitate support for women to acquire energy and labour-saving equipment for use in dairy or home or dairy value chain activities.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.1.3 Organisational capacity building in energy efficiency and waste management. This training will focus on the management of the RE solutions in dairy cooperatives, SMEs and MICS. Such will include training for women as lead trainers and participants in waste management activities.

## Output: 2.2 Breeding, feed and fodder

**Page 171**

but present a higher productive potential. Indigenous breeds, despite their lower productive potential and small size, are a reservoir for resilient traits such as hardiness, mobility, rendering them more adaptable to adverse climatic conditions and diseases. Differences in the composition of milk proteins among indigenous breeds contribute to maintaining the resilience traits of indigenous breeds, while also adding production traits to the gene pool, increasing the offspring's productivity in milk production, therefore contributing to reducing GHG emission intensity. Available studies indicate that crossbred cows produce more milk than their purebred counterparts and have higher reproductive performance compared with indigenous breeds. This activity will target women and youth from both districts and pass on or improved breeds to increase milk incomes especially.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2.1 Make sure the quality semen for climate-smart breeds available, adaptable to extreme climatic conditions and affordable. The objective is to ensure that there are no limitations of germplasm of exotic that will be crossed with local. This will be achieved through (i) the identification of potential climate-smart cattle breeds; (ii) the import of seed semen and/or embryos, if needed; (iii) the procurement of high genetic merit bulls to produce seed semen and/or embryos for crossbreeding purposes; (iv) the establishment of a semen bank for climate smart breed: germplasm using laboratory techniques like in vitro-fertilization, or simply artificial insemination (AI); and (v) capacity development of laboratory personnel including youth to produce seed semen to increase production of dairy calves.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2.2 In vitro breeding and AI centres. To utilise bottleneck in AI and embryo transfer, the Programme will support scaling up capacities for production and distribution of liquid nitrogen, semen, and embryos. This includes the availability of liquid nitrogen, storage and quality control equipment, dry ice pilots for conservation of semen, and other necessary equipment to ensure that semen and embryos are available at all times. The establishment of an AI centre will involve the Kenya Livestock Breeders Association, which is specialised in extension and training, breed inspection and registration, performance recording and reporting and feedback. In Rwanda, support will be given for the establishment of a semen bank and embryo production facility at the National Animal Genetic Resources Centre in Songata Station to acquire micro-manipulation to enhance semen production operations. Moreover, RAB will be strengthened in record keeping and continuous updates of the Stockbook to identify resilient and high productive crossbreeds. The programme will also support the establishment of a semen bank for climate-smart breeds, with the possibility of an LN unit in Zanzibar will be analysed. In Uganda, this investment will strengthen the NAGC and Data Bank to improve the production and access to high-quality semen in small and medium size farms. DUMA will facilitate women and youth farmers to access high-quality semen.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2.3 Ensure that use of climate-dairy come to make the breed more productive and climate resilient. The Programme will (i) train technicians, who can be private veterinarians, para-veterinarians, lead farmers of IFS and District livestock officers (at least 30 percent youth and 40 percent women); (ii) scale up the immunisation of dairy cattle against diseases such as mastitis, Johne's disease, and other endemic diseases; (iii) integrate climate change in the LfS curriculum. In Kenya, the Programme will support the National Breeding Strategy and Action Plan, in collaboration with the Kenya Livestock Breeders Association. Public and private partnerships (PPP) will be an important component of this strategy. The capacity building for the livestock sector in terms of RAB will be scaled-up including the training of more private AI clinics. In Tanzania, additional technicians will be trained and equipped with start-up units. In addition, bull centres will be established in regions where AR is not available. Uganda will scale up the services offered by the hatchery and will be equipped with AI units focusing on the regions where AI services are still poorly developed.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2.4 Organise awareness campaigns and support breeders' organisations. This activity will facilitate female and youth members of dairy cooperatives and IFS groups to create awareness on the use of seed semen,


---
**Page 172**
---

Activity 2.2.3 Scale up vaccination campaigns to mitigate the impact of climate change on the spread of diseases. The Programme will scale up and extend the vaccination campaigns to target in priority dairy farms in the Programme area. The campaign will be conducted through a combination of traditional and innovative creative campaigns such as using road shows and other community media. The vaccination campaign will focus on: (i) the spread of East Coast Fever (ECF), Contagious Bovine Pleuropneumonia (CBPP) and Heartwater disease to new endemic areas in Kenya, Tanzania and Uganda; (ii) the control of Rift Valley fever (RVF) in Kenya, Tanzania, ECF, FMD, RVP and CBPP in Tanzania. (iii) trans-boundary animal diseases (TADs) like TFM, CBPP, and LSD in Uganda.

Activity 2.3 Promote climate-smart forage pasture and conservation. The adoption of climate resilient forage varieties and conservation technologies is essential to reduce enteric methane emissions intensities in the dairy value


---
**Page 173**
---

chain, while increasing milk yields of dairy cattle. The benefits are multiple and encompass CC adaptation through adoption of a resilient forage variety for livestock purposes, Brassica, Chenopodium species (Napier grass), legumes, Calliandra calothyrsus, Giant Paspalum, Imperatae, in addition to the provision of training to farmers, managers and handlers (feeding and milking) to meet forage production needs during dry periods and to combat climate-induced pests and diseases.

### Sub-activity 2.2.3.1 Make certified forage seed available to private and community multiplications
This sub-activity will be supported by the provision of breeder and foundation seeds for climate-smart, nitrogen-reducing fodder by the national mandated public bodies. It will facilitate a seed certification scheme and sensitise the private sector to procure certified forage and fodder seeds.

### Sub-activity 2.2.3.2 Support multiplication of climate-resilient seed for fodder crops and pastures
This sub-activity will include contracting community and private seed multiplications (including the youth and women) for multiplication, conservation, and distribution of pasture and fodder crop seeds. These approaches will be used to supply mulips seeds and make them available to LFS, PFS, cooperatives, and other groups.

### Sub-activity 2.2.3.3 Build capacity of dairy farmers on forage production and conservation on intensive and semi-intensive dairy farms
The LFS will be the knowledge-sharing vehicle to promote improved forage production and conservation (e.g., PVS) with focus on: (i) training on good forage management practices (e.g., grass and legume intercropping, agronomic practices of sowing, fertilization and irrigation); (ii) demonstration of technologies that reduce forage crops; (iii) training and demonstrations of mechanisation of forage production with fodder choppers/cutters and ensiling machines; (iv) training on the use of silage as a feed source to meet livestock requirements in response to seasonal feed availability and quality (e.g., protein content, digestibility) variations. Youth and women groups will be supported to venture into commercial fodder production, which may include production using hydroponic technology.

### Sub-activity 2.2.3.4 Access to water for animals and forage production in intensive and semi-intensive dairy systems
The Programme will provide small-scale equipment for rainwater harvesting and recycling (e.g., individual dam sheets, 3m³ rootlet catchment water tanks and water distribution kits). This activity will target to provide green solutions for women aiming to reduce workloads related to accessing water.

### Activity 2.2.4 Promote climate-smart feeding practices to reduce enteric methane emissions
Appropriate diet formulation (e.g., balanced diets, reduced concentrate feeds) reduces enteric methane while reducing methane (CH₄) emissions. Balanced diets (more energy-dense or more digestible) decrease retention time in the rumen and reduce ruminated fermentation and methane yield (kg CH₄/kg dry matter intake). Better nutrient utilisation (e.g., avoiding deficiencies or surpluses) reduces the environmental impact of livestock.

### Sub-activity 2.2.4.2 Build capacity on appropriate feed formulation
In each country, the Programme will support the capacity building in technologies such as co-digestion, crop residues, small feed processing plants and LFS to lead farmers on sustainable animal feed formulations such as total mixed rations (TMR) to enhance productive efficiency and mitigate GHG emissions.

### Sub-activity 2.2.4.3 Promote appropriate diet formulation that reduces enteric methane emissions
In the four countries, the LFS and the dairy cooperatives will be the vehicle to reach out to dairy farmers and promote

**Page 174**

Activity 2.2.1.3 Promote sustainable nutrient recycling of manure and dairy waste to reduce emissions: livestock manure is a source of methane (CH4) and nitrous oxide (N2O) emissions, as well as of pollutants such as ammonia (NH3) and nitrate (NO3). At the same time, manure contains essential nutrients and is often the primary source of crop fertilization. The use of livestock manure for fertilizer has increased in recent years due to the increase in cattle numbers in some countries. However, the increasing demand for milk and meat products has led to an increase in emissions from livestock farming. This has a positive effect on livestock productivity, as better manure management also improves the nutrient availability for feed and forages, which increases their growth, biomass yield and nutritional quality and ultimately improves milk production. Better manure management reduces soil nutrient mining and soil degradation and prevents additional CO2 emissions from loss of soil organic carbon.

Sub-activity 2.2.1.3.1 Manure management in intensive and semi-intensive dairy production systems: The Programme will create awareness and build capacity on low-cost manure management for soil health and soil moisture improvement, as well as the use of manure-based fertilizers. This will be done through training workshops using existing manuals developed by NAMO in Uganda, IIRI, etc., or on scalable and already tested manure management practices (pit storage, manure ponds, manure covering with banana leaves, compaction, etc.), and manure use applications offering livelihoods opportunities. The target is to attract women and youth milk farmers as demonstration centres to attract more women and youth to participate.

Sub-activity 2.2.1.3.2 Promote adapted animal housing: The Programme will promote adapted animal housing with the possibility of separating solid and liquid manure. Good example of animal housing promoted by Heifer International can be scaled up targeting women and youth dairy farmers.

Sub-activity 2.2.1.3.3 Manure management in extensive systems (pastoral and agro-pastoral grazing regions): The Programme will promote the use of biogas digesters for manure collection from livestock粪便for soil nutrient generation at community level, and to establish community manure sheds to promote manure collection, storage and covering for better manure management and reduce emissions. This activity will target to support women and youth led groups.

Activity 2.4.1 Build capacity on on-farm use of biogas digesters and沼气消化器. Biogas digesters utilize microorganisms to break down organic matter such as livestock manure or other organic waste, during which biogas and a nutrient-rich by-product (called digestate or bio-slurry) are produced. This technology offers a clean source of energy for people who do not have access to clean cooking fuels. In addition, it creates a fertilizer (for use on organic fertilizers on nearby crops), reduces the amount of manure that needs to be applied and can reduce emissions of methane and nitrous oxide emissions. Biogas digesters can reduce deforestation and empower women by reducing the time they spend collecting firewood. The Programme will promote the use of biogas digesters for livestock manure on farm biogaseters for intensive and semi-intensive dairy farms. Women are the main consumers of biogas and should be


---

Involved in trainings on how the setup works as well as identifying where outside and inside the house the equipment should be placed to not curtail the functionality of the kitchen.

### Sub-activity 2.3.6.1 Support capacity building in biogasletter management

The Programme will support the training and accreditation of biogasletter technicians and consumers, with a particular focus on women and youth, in the target districts. In addition to installation-related aspects of biogas valorisation and quality management, including safe working standards.

### Sub-activity 2.3.6.2 Support investments in biogasletters at the household level

The Programme will support investments in biogasletters for dairy farms ensuring women and jointly owned farms are supported in the intervention area.

#### T. Output 1.3.7 Pasture and grasslands management improved

**Activity 2.3.1 Strengthening communal grazing areas**

This activity will finance interventions to improve rangeland management (including access to water), mainly in Kenya, Tanzania and Uganda. Engagement and awareness raising among local stakeholders involved in landscape management is essential for locally led adaptation and people-centred planning.

#### Sub-activity 2.3.1.1 Participatory rangeland management planning

In selected districts/counties, the Programme will (i) map and assess existing communal grazing areas; (ii) identify key actors and stakeholders (including women and youth); (iii) establish a steering committee of women and youth representatives; (iv) develop a participatory rangelands management plan, including land tenure to secure tenure rights, water access, as well as possible conflict resolution over land and water use; and (v) support the development, revitalisation and strengthening of laws/charter for communal grazing areas (including water). The groups to be supported will be required to meet the women and youth quotas in representation.

#### Activity 2.3.2 Restoration of degraded pasture lands, including access to water

This activity will finance interventions to restore rangelands.

##### Sub-activity 2.3.2.1 Training on rangeland and pasture management

The Programme will facilitate ICF/FFPS aimed at guiding and engaging farmers towards improving the management of rangelands and pastures. ICF5 will focus on (i) reseeding and integration of legumes with grasses in natural pastures; (ii) integration of multipurpose leguminous trees (MPTs) into existing pastoral systems to improve soil fertility, water retention and livestock productivity; (iii) integration of sylvopastoral practices (multipurpose leguminous trees for better carbon sequestration and reduction of live stock exposure to diseases); (iv) establishment of shade (shade particularly around water harvesting points); and (v) biochar production, either in situ or including shade (made from invasive tree species). This activity will support women and youth farmers with animal shades and biochar production.

##### Sub-activity 2.3.2.2 Rangelands restoration

This sub-activity, focusing on communal grazing areas, will focus on: (i) reseeding and integration of legumes with grasses in natural pastures; (ii) integration of multipurpose leguminous trees into existing pastoral systems to improve soil fertility, water retention and livestock productivity; (iii) integration of sylvopastoral practices (multipurpose leguminous trees for better carbon sequestration and reduction of live stock exposure to diseases); (iv) establishment of shade (shade particularly around water harvesting points); and (v) implementation of soil and water conservation measures.

# Sub-activity 2.3.3 Water Availability

Depending on the inquiry to enhance access to water for dairy cattle in extensive systems, the Programme will invest in water harvesting systems such as micro-dams, small-scale valley dams, and rock catchments schemes. Feasibility studies will be carried out before the start of the work to assess sustainability. The Programme will also enhance the capacity of the community-managed irrigation schemes to ensure that they are able to operate an all-water harvesting and storage infrastructures. The design of water infrastructure will follow the guidance of the Government authorities and integrate gender sensitive facilities such as toilet and sanitation blocks and washing bays.

## COMPONENT 3: GREEN DAIRY FINANCING FACILITY (GDFF)

Component 3 aims to enhance access to finance and support the implementation of climate-smart solutions and practices, thereby facilitating the transition to sustainable, low-carbon, climate-resilient dairy value chains. Component will have two key outputs:

1. The establishment of an investment window.
2. The establishment of a technical assistance facility.

The investment window will be used by investors to invest in projects that are aligned with the GDFF’s goals and objectives, based on the need to develop or climate-smart technologies, productivity-enhancing technologies or SHF extension services and have a track record of SHF allyship. Investment decisions will be made on a case-by-case basis depending on business need and alignment with the GDFF’s goals and objectives.

The technical assistance facility will provide grants to dairy farmers who are willing to invest in products tailored for women and youth. Dairy Loan Products will target smaller businesses, and investors will be eligible to apply for loans that meet their financial needs into investment requests, and they can be connected to other (DAMA) components to access any paid-out TANs.

Through the (3.1) Investment window established, the main objective of the GDFF is to facilitate the large-scale transition to sustainable, low-carbon, climate-resilient dairy value chains in Kenya, Uganda, Rwanda and Tanzania. To do so, it will provide grants to investors who are willing to invest in projects that are aligned with the GDFF’s goals and objectives.

The technical assistance facility will provide grants to dairy farmers who are willing to invest in products tailored for women and youth. Dairy Loan Products will target smaller businesses, and investors will be eligible to apply for loans that meet their financial needs into investment requests, and they can be connected to other (DAMA) components to access any paid-out TANs.

### Output 3.1 on the Technical Assistance Facility

Low-emission cold chain and inefficient, low-loss milk processing units will be established and upgraded. This will overcome the limited processing infrastructure and inadequate utilization of dairy products in the region. The GDFF will provide grants to farmers who are willing to integrate their business plans into their business plans. Key expected outputs expected from the unit will be to:

1. Create jobs for male and female youth aged 18-30 years as part of their workforce (to be) gender-responsive.
2. Create an inclusive policy environment where women and youth have equal opportunities in dairy value chains, one that is socially inclusive policies and practices and inclusive project in the respective. These units will aim to employ 40% women, 30% youth and vulnerable groups.

### Activity 3.1.1 Fund set up

This activity aims to establish the GDFF as a functioning entity with the required policies, procedures, and staff, and to crow in private commercial capital in pursuit of the programme’s climate impact ambitions. The GDFF staffing and structure will include provisions for gender and youth expertise.

### Sub-Activity 3.1.2 Fund Management

The Fund Manager will be responsible for identifying, engaging and carrying out due diligence on prospective investors. The Fund Manager will develop criteria for screening investors demonstrate forward and backward linkages with women and youth.


---
**Page 177**
---

Activity 3.1.2 Identify eligible businesses for investment. While this will focus on developing a screening framework to assess prospective investors for potential impact and financial return. This activity will ensure identification of women and youth-owned enterprises that are most likely to be successful in the Green Dairy Partnership.

Sub-Activity 3.1.2.1 Develop screening framework. The Fund Manager will establish a screening framework to ensure consistency across all investments, with particular focus on targeting women and youth-owned enterprises. Investment categories which are justified and eligible for the Fund including those that facilitate jobs and youth empowerment by creating jobs and employment opportunities.

Sub-Activity 3.1.2.2 Identify investment pipeline. The investment pipeline for the GDF will be sourced through three main channels: (i) GDF Investment Advisors, (ii) DAaM’s PCIs, and (iii) TA providers under Component 2. Investment Advisors will screen prospective investors against pre-defined screening criteria, which will include women and youth considerations.

Activity 3.1.3 Climate-smart lending products and services are rolled out by GDF to eligible businesses. These will include labour and energy-saving products aimed at reducing workloads for women. The two products: Green Dairy Payments (GDP) and the Dairy Loan Facility, will be developed in collaboration with DAaM and TA providers.

The Dairy loan tickets will be developed to ensure the ticket sizes are affordable and accessible to these groups. Provisions for community outreach will be made through different media to ensure uptake. The Fund Manager’s gender and youth considerations will be embedded into the design of these products.

Output 3.2 Technical Assistance Facility established. The Green Dairy Financing Facility will invest in climate-resilient dairy producers, off-takers, processors, and livestock investors. Tailored financial products with technical assistance will increase access to finance and affordable credit for on-farm investments especially for youth, women and vulnerable groups. This will be achieved through the following: (i) The GDF will provide technical assistance under transformative approaches such as confidence building, communication skills and agency-building. The GDF will use a technical assistance facility to maximize both impact and returns by enhancing the capacity of investors including gender and youth-sensitive approaches. (ii) The GDF will support investments in climate-resilience, operational efficiencies and achieve growth.

Activity 3.2.1 Pre-Loan Development for Dairy Loan Products. These businesses will receive light-touch TA to transition them into a loan, into a well-defined investment request prior to the deal. Tailored support will be developed to support women and youth-friendly loan products.

Sub-Activity 3.2.1.1 Pre-Loan Development for Dairy Loan Products. These businesses will receive light-touch TA to transition them into a loan, into a well-defined investment request prior to the deal. Tailored support will be developed to support women and youth-friendly loan products.

Activity 3.2.2 Post-deal TA. Providing ongoing support post-deal to ensure successful implementation, monitor progress and ensure outcomes are met. Activity 3.2.2 focuses on providing post-deal TA to Green Dairy Partnership investors, ensuring that outcomes are achieved. The type and level of support will be tailored based on the specific needs of each investor. This will include: (i) Technical assistance for women and youth-owned enterprises in South investments for learning exchanges and scale-up. Considering the Dairy Loans will be short-term, Dairy Loan investees especially women, youth and vulnerable groups may be considered for follow-ups in place of post-deal TA.

Implementation arrangements

The GDF action plan shall be implemented through implementation partner or Service Providers activities as defined by the TA providers under Component 2 investment projects.

DiMA will seek implementing partners with experience working on gender equality and women empowerment, youth sensitive programmes, nutrition, and social inclusion, with emphasis on community centre approaches promoting integration of persons living and/or vulnerable groups, persons with disabilities. This may include youthful enterprises, NGOs, the academia and researchers.

**Component 3: NGO programme management. Actions to strengthen the GESI dimensions of programme management:**

- **Support for GESI Action Plan implementation:** Provided through the Technical Assistance, which includes capacity building at different levels including for implementing partners and end beneficiaries.
  
A GESI specialist for each Country operation will ensure the full use of the GESI action plan for each country. Additional support is provided under the following areas: (a) strengthening the capacity to ensure access and update for women and youth. The specific TA will include: i) capacity strengthening and technical support to partners, ii) capacity building for input and service providers to support outreach, gender-informed approaches and capacity building at the community level; iii) follow up on the Monitoring and Evaluation team; and iv) ongoing GESI technical support during implementation.

- **Include a specific commitment to gender-responsive planning:** in terms of the reference of the implementation teams and implementing organizations. This shall be backed by capacity strengthening on identifying and addressing gender issues. Annual performance reviews, aligned to the logframe targets will track performance on delivering GEWE and GESI goals.
  
Select implementing partners (input and service providers) with a proven commitment to community participation and gender equality. Partners must comply with the relevant GESI skills. The implementing partners shall be held accountable for delivering on GESI targets.

- **Ensure that annual work plans and budgets are prepared, delivered and tracked from a gender perspective:** and that the funds allocated and/or locked in implement gender-related activities are appropriate.
  
Ensure the DiMA project and implementing partners promote facilities, processes and norms that are gender-responsive. This includes ensuring equal access to employment opportunities and wages between women and men for the same grade of employment; life-work balance; policies and communications on the prevention of sexual exploitation, discrimination, harassment and the abuse of authority.

- **Ensure the systematic integration of GESI considerations:** including indicators related to representation and expertise – in the project design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation. This will ensure that expert advisory bodies and decision-making structures:

# GENDER AND SOCIAL INCLUSION ACTION PLAN MATRIX

## Impact Statement:
If technological solutions for reducing methane and other greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and increasing climate resilience are adopted by dairy farmers and milk processors in East Africa, and if financial services that enhance revenue or lower production costs are provided to these actors, then an increase in the profitability of dairy enterprises will be observed. This will lead to a reduction in supply chain GHG emissions.

## Outcome Statement:
DAMA will secure climate change-resilient dairy enterprise-dependent livelihoods with the adoption of low-emission technologies and services by smallholder farmers and vulnerable groups. Business and employment opportunities and incomes will improve with more equitable benefits to all key players DAMA intends to achieve the following:

1. Strengthened systemic and institutional capacities of the livestock sector, which will enable it to transition to a low-carbon, climate-resilient, sustainable pathway.
2. Reduced GHG emissions from dairy production and other GHG emissions.
3. Increased access for smallholder farmers to market knowledge and tools for smallholder dairy producers and private sector value chain operators for a low-emission, climate-resilient, and sustainable pathway.

4. Enabled access by smallholder farmers and financial services to make the necessary transition to low-carbon, climate-resilient, and sustainable livestock production and value chain development in East Africa, with support from public and private sector stakeholders and actors.

The overall budget required to implement the GESI responsive DAMA program is estimated to be USD 2.9 billion.

## Outputs, activities and sub-activities

Activity 1.1.3 Improve land tenure security for sustainable dairy farming: The Programme will adopt a life-for-purpose approach to identify and mitigate tenure risks. This includes mapping of local tenure risks and preparation of training materials on how to secure tenure rights with decentralised technical assistance (e.g., developing material to increase local authorities' capacity to secure gender-specific tenure rights). The Programme will also develop tools to assess the capacity to identify and secure legitimate tenure rights. Based on the national tenure context, each dairy production system's tenure risks in terms of gendered access to land will be identified and assessed, and appropriate mitigating strategies will be proposed.

#### Sub-activity 1.2.1.1 Support the installation of both private and public veterinarians and veterinary paraprofessionals in the Programme area

The Programme will implement the animal health support and disease control systems in Rwanda, which will be enhanced by providing training to both private and public veterinarians and veterinary paraprofessionals. The programme will have three levels: (i) support private veterinarians and para-veterinarians, particularly young female and male practitioners; (ii) provide mentorship for young women and men workers, while ensuring gender (sex and age) equity in participation at local level; (iii) provide training to young women and male youthful farmers from the LIFS for coaching their peers and assist private female and male para-veterinarians and livestock extension workers.

In Kenya, Tanzania and Uganda, training programs to sponsored and non-from both private and public veterinarians and veterinary paraprofessionals in the Programme area will be enhanced. In Rwanda, support will be provided for the installation of both private and public veterinarians and veterinary paraprofessionals as technical as private practitioners to enhance accessibility of services while creating a conducive environment for their practice. The Veterinary Clinics (VACs) will be equipped and trained, and private male and female paravets will be incentivized to work in the VACs.

In Uganda, the Programme will build capacity of female and male veterinarians, para-veterinarians, technicians, and community-based animal health workers (CBAHWs) on One Health approach for disease control, eradication and managing diseases because of CC.

#### Activity 2.1.1: Strengthen dairy cooperatives on organizational management and service delivery.
This activity aims to strengthen cooperative, organizational, and technical capacities of dairy cooperatives, and raise their climate change and gender equality performance. ### Sub-activity 1.4.3: Strengthening the quality and safety at the aggregation stage
Up to approximately 8 percent of the milk is lost via spillage and spillage. Aiming at reducing unproductive GHG emissions from dairy production, this sub-activity will support MCGs and MCPLs in upgrading their technical capacity in milk quality control and handling equipment (e.g., milking machines, cooling systems). This will go beyond the sale of raw milk, in line with national and regional food safety standards. The activities include: (i) training on milk quality and safety (motorcycles, milk cans and can holders, testing equipment), (ii) insulated milk transport vehicles (demonstration purpose), and (iii) training on milk quality, food safety and handling of raw milk, as well as the use of insulated milk transporters (after men) and technical staff of cooperatives, MCGs and MCPL.

In Kenya, the Programme will support the National Breeding Centre to train AI technicians and provide them with equipment. The Breeders Association. Public and private partnerships (PPP) will be promoted to have AI services accessible to the male and female farmers. In Rwanda, the breeding centre will train private inseminators in addition to public and private AI technicians. In Tanzania, additional technicians will be trained and equipped for AI services. In Malawi, the programme will support existing operations where AI is not available. In Uganda, more AI technicians will be trained by the build-up of capacity associated with AI kits focusing on the regions where AI services are still poorly developed.

Campaigns and support breeders’ organizations. The campaigns will target male, female and youth members of dairy cooperatives and L-FS groups to create awareness about the benefits of using AI for improving herd productivity further reduce absolute GHG emissions and the emission intensity per kg of milk produced. This will lead to a reduction in the number of cows kept and increased high performance crossbreeds, thus reducing the herd size. In the four countries, the programme will support the development and success for animal registration and maintenance of breeding standards.

Activity 2.3.2 Provide support for better animal care, herd management and disease prevention. Day to feeding and management is mainly a female and male responsibility in most communities. The Programme will provide training on animal health and reproduction management as part of a change in the L-FS curriculum targeting women and youth. Updated modules of the L-FS training will be implemented. Dairy farmers will receive support for better care of their animals and to adopt biosecurity measures to prevent common diseases such as mastitis, Johne’s disease and other diseases that reduce milk consumption. They will also receive support to better manage their herds, reproduction demand (and semen) and to reduce animal mortality rates.

Sub-activity 2.2.2.2 Sub-activity 2.2.2.2 Support dairy farmers to monitor animal health and herd performance. Activity 2.3 Promote climate-smart forage production and conservation. The adoption of climate-smart forage management practices is essential to reduce enteric methane emissions intensities in the dairy value chain, while increasing milk yields of dairy cattle. These are multiple and encompass a wide range of species (e.g., Napier grass, Leucaena, Crotalaria juncea, Lathyrus sativus, Lablab purpureus, Brachiaria sp., Cenchrus ciliaris (Napier grass), Leucaena, Crotalaria juncea, Lathyrus sativus, Lablab purpureus, Brachiaria sp., Cenchrus ciliaris) and include increased capacity of farmers, managers and handlers (feeding and milking to meet forage requirements during dry periods and to respond to climate-induced pests and diseases.

Sub-activity 2.3.3 Build capacity of dairy farmers on forage production and intensive dairy farms. The L-FS will be the gender inclusive knowledge-sharing vehicle in this sub-activity. The support includes: (i) training on good forage management practices (e.g., grass and legume intercropping, use of organic fertilizers); (ii) demonstration and promotion on small-scale irrigation to produce forage crops; (iii) training and demonstrations of mechanized equipment for harvesting and conservation equipment; (iv) training and demonstrations on forage conservation practices, to respond to seasonal feed availability and quality (e.g., protein content). The support will be provided by the L-FS facilitators. This sub-activity is expected to venture into commercial fodder production, which may include production using hydroponic technology.

Activity 2.3.4 Build capacity for on-farm use of biodigesters and biogas. Biogas digesters utilize microorganisms to break down organic matter such as livestock manure or food waste in a sealed container where methane gas is produced, the product (called digester or bio slurry) is produced. This technology offers a clean source of energy for cooking and lighting, and can be used for heating water. In addition, it creates a biogas fertilizer (the use as organic fertilizer on nearby fields), reduces greenhouse gases (GHG) emissions (e.g., methane, nitrous oxide storage) and ammonia and nitrous oxide emissions. Biogasters can reduce deforestation by reducing the need to cut down trees for fuelwood or charcoal burning firewood. As part of the low-carbon pathways, the Programme will encourage the uptake of on-farm biodigesters for intensive and semi-intensive dairy farms. Including women in this activity is a priority.

Sub-activity 2.3.4.1 Support capacity building in biodigester management. The Programme will support the training and accreditation of biodigester technicians and ensure that they are equipped with the necessary tools to service biodigesters districts. In addition to installation-related aspects of biogas valorisation and quality management, including safe working standards.

Sub-activity 2.3.4.2 Rangelands restoration. This sub-activity focusing on communal rangeland areas will support (i) the integration of legumes with grasses in natural pastures; (ii) integration of multipurpose leguminous trees and shrubs into existing or new tree nurseries; (iii) preservation of traditional non-tree forest products (medicinal and other) known to be threatened by over-exploitation; (iv) promotion of conservation practices to increase water availability and biomass productivity.

COMPONENT 3: GREEN DAIRY FINANCING FACILITY (GDF)

Activity 1.3.1 Fund set up. This activity aims to establish the GDF fund as a functional entity, including the development of a business plan, staffing, and to crowd in private commercial capital in pursuit of the programme’s climate impact ambition. The GDF staffing and structure will include provisions for gender and youth expert.

Sub-Activity 1.3.1.1 Investor engagement and negotiation (led by Fund Manager, in consultation with other relevant stakeholders). This sub-activity aims at engaging and carrying out due diligence on prospective investors. The Fund Manager will be responsible for identifying potential investors, building backward linkages with women and youth enterprises. Expertise may be needed to support youth and women screening.

Activity 3.1.2 Identify eligible businesses for investment. While this will focus on developing a screening framework to assess prospective investors for potential impact investments in agribusinesses and smallholder agriculture, it will also look at youth led businesses by making gender and youth provisions in the screening framework.

Sub-Activity 3.1.2.1 Developing screening framework. The Fund Manager will establish a taxonomy of investment categories that are justifiable under the GDFF. This screening framework will be underpinned by a taxonomy of investment categories which are justified for impact investments, including those that facilitate women’s and youth empowerment by creating jobs and employment opportunities.

Sub-Activity 3.1.2.2 Pipeline development and screening. The pipeline for the GDFF will be sourced through three main channels: (i) GDFF Investment Advisors, (ii) channel partners, and (iii) direct outreach to potential investors. A team will screen prospective investors against pre-defined screening criteria, which will include a focus on gender and youth inclusion strategies to ensure uptake. Undertake mobilization drives for women and youth investors.

Activity 3.1.3 Climate-smart lending products and services are rolled out by GDFF to eligible farmers in the GDFF pipeline. The two key products are: (i) Green Dairy Partnerships and dairy financing, which will be targeted at smallholder dairy farmers, particularly women and youth farmers. The loan ticket size will be developed to ensure the tickets can be accessed through mobile banking. Outreach will be made through different media to ensure uptake by these groups.

Output 3.2 Technical Assistance Facility established. The Green Dairy Financing Facility will invest in climate-resilient dairy producers, off-takers, processors, and financiers. This facility will provide technical assistance for the dairy sector which will increase access to finance and affordable credit for on-farm investments. The facility will also provide training and capacity building workshops, introduce tailored BDS and financial literacy modules that incorporate gender transformative approaches such as confidence building, communication skills and agency in decision making. These interventions are expected to have both impact and returns by enhancing the capacity of investors (including on gender equality), increasing access to finance, promoting effective strategies, improving operational efficiencies and achieve growth.

Investment Advisors will liaise with the TA providers of Component 2 to ensure targeting of women and youth linked enterprises.

3.2 focuses on providing post-deal TA to support dairy partnerships, investees, existing cooperatives and others. The type and level of support will be tailored based on the specific needs identified for each business. This TA will codify lessons learned from project good case examples or develop youth friendly tools for learning exchange purposes. Consequently Dairy Loans will be short term. Dairy Loan investors especially women, youth and vulnerable groups may be considered as they are more likely to require post-deal TA.
